BACKGROUND ΤΟ PRIMULA

"SHE's the only girl in London, positively and absolutely," babbled young Bobby Blair.

He had been on this theme for some time. 

Fitzroy Sholto-Scott, a little weary of it, glanced at him with raised eyebrows. "I fancy I've noticed one or two others," he murmured. "At least, they looked like girls to me."

He gazed round him. There happened to be a great many girls this evening at the Berkeley, where he was dining with his young second cousin. Two pretty ones at the next table-Coral and Turquoise he called them in his mind-kept casting glances of unabashed and lively interest at Bobby and himselfno, not at Bobby, at himself. They had, of course, recognized him from the pictures in the papers. And he had flattered himself that he would cut an inconspicuous figure in his ordinary evening clothes! It was his eyebrows; he must shave them! All of a sudden, he felt immensely bored, and a little shy. 1

Bobby gave Coral and Turquoise a disparaging glance. "Wait till you've seen Primula,” he said. "That reminds me," put in Sholto-Scott. "Has this Primula got a surname? You haven't mentioned it, and one can't very well go up to one's hostess and call her Primula." "You could," returned Bobby, with an admiring glance. "Especially when she grasps the fact that you're the chap that won the Pinkerton Trophy. She's tremendously keen on flying. Got her pilot's certificate in a fortnight, and—” "For heaven's sake, don't mention the Pinkerton 

Trophy," said Sholto-Scott, with a groan. "What I went through on the boat, coming back!— I wished 

I was a haberdasher! But you haven't yet told me what her name is." "Staniforth." "Staniforth? Any relation to the banking people?" "I should say so! Why, she's Sir Everard's only grandchild. She's got pots now, but some day she'll have millions." 

Bobby's eyes were round with awe.

Sholto-Scott appeared to be searching his memory. "Then I read something about her the other day," he remarked. "Something about a speed-boat. Only they didn't call her Primula." "Her name's Mary Primula."

"That was it. Miss Mary Staniforth, the richest girl in London—” "They call her the Golden Girl,” said Bobby. “Awful piffle, but jolly good name for her, too. I say, do you mind if we cut coffee? We'll get some at Primula's -best coffee in London."

Sholto-Scott did not mind. Against his will he was beginning to feel some slight curiosity about this 

Golden Girl; this modern equivalent of the Fairy

Princess, whose magic wand-consisting, he supposed, of share certificates, bonds, and money-in-the-bankcould work as many wonders for its owner as those in the nursery stories. He wondered whether Bobby would be quite so much dazzled by Miss Primula if she sold calico over a counter.

They went back to his cousin Barbara's flat, and there he pursued these meditations in the drawingroom, while Bobby changed into a pirate's costume. 

For Primula's party was a Black Sheep party. "I'm all right, then," Sholto-Scott remarked to 

Bobby. "Lots of Black Sheep wear 'immaculate evening dress." " "Oh, you can do what you like," returned Bobby. "You won the Pinkerton Trophy. Besides, with your arm in a sling, you can't do much in the dressing-up line.” "Where are we going?" asked Sholto-Scott, a little later, as the car, after crossing Oxford Circus.

plunged into dark side-streets Bloomsbury-way. 

Somehow, he had taken Park Lane for granted, or 

Grosvenor Square. Mayfair, at any rate. "Caroline Square." "Never heard of it.” "It's in Bloomsbury. Primula discovered it, and now two or three people are living there. It's Queen 

Anne, or something. She's got the most marvellous flair. She bought No. 20 and No. 21, and had them thrown into one, and got Lambert Jacks to decorate them." "Not much Queen Anne about Lambert Jacks," remarked Sholto-Scott. "I should rather hope not," replied Bobby. "She's pretty dead by now. Primula's taken the whole top floor for her studio, and she has a kitchen up there, and-" "I don't quite grasp," said Sholto-Scott. "Is she an orphan ?" "Oh, no. She's got a mother and an aunt of sorts, who live with her, and run the house, and sack the cook, and so forth. But they don't show up much, as a rule. They're well trained," said Bobby, who spent his life well under the thumb of his capable mother. "Parents should be seen and not heard. .. Hey!"

They were swinging out of a side street into Caroline Square, and Bobby hung out of the window to direct the taxi-driver to No. 20.

There was no need. Across the dark square garden the lights of No. 20 blazed out from area to attic, with a magnificent disregard of economy. Surely nobody in the Square but the Golden Girl could afford to waste so much electric light! The taxi-man drove there without a moment's hesitation, and took his place behind a Baby Austin, a Rolls Royce, and an 

Isotta-Fraschini. 

Inside the house, Sholto-Scott observed that Lambert Jacks had been given a free hand. A vermilion staircase, which might have led straight to some Chinese paradise, rose into the upper storeys from the shining grey metal floor of the hall, which was strewn with jade-green rugs. The frescoes on the walls were of the passing minute. He caught glimpses, going upstairs, of aeroplanes, motor-cars, young men golfing in plus-fours, girls diving and girls drinking cocktails at a cocktail bar, and all in the brightest possible colours.

Sholto-Scott liked it. It was alive and jolly and modern, and above all, it was triumphantly young. 

Sounds drifted down to them as they climbed the fantastic staircase behind a tramp, a brigand, and a girl in yellow trousers, who had come in the Isotta Fraschini; sounds of an orchestra, laughter, and a high clatter of voices; all the audible signs of a party going with a swing. "Jove, we're in luck! She's dancing," said Bobby, as soon as they were well inside the big room. Sholto-

Scott knew that for the moment Bobby had only one she. He flattened himself against the wall, and looked with interest into the middle of the room where two people were dancing alone. "By Jove," he murmured to himself. "Bill Sikes and Nancy!"

The man, tall and graceful, with a red comforter twisted round his bare throat, and a sinister-looking cap crammed down upon his dark brows, was clutching the girl by the throat, while she retreated before him with an upturned stare of terror. Half-way round the room they went with the utmost speed and grace, and then the end of the dance came abruptly. Nancy sank lifeless into the arms of Bill Sikes, and he held her out at arm's length. Sholto-Scott received an instantaneous impression of very slender ankles, and fair hair streaming back from a small head. A moment later she had dropped lightly on to her feet and was executing a brilliant pirouette. "I a-always tell Prim she re-ally o-ought to go on the stage," said a clear high voice beside him, a voice he felt he ought to know, with its deliberate enunciation and drawling cadences. It belonged to a slender young person with vine-leaves in her auburn hair, who sat smoking at a table near by. But her beautiful white back was towards him, and he was not able to recognize her. 

And now the dancer curtseyed, waved her hand

carelessly in acknowledgment of the clapping, and came forward with a little swagger, tossing her short fair hair, silver-gold, back from a very white forehead like a child's. She was an unusually pretty girl, but it was not that which caught the attention of Sholto-Scott. It was the little air of royalty which hung about her. · • "The Princess of Stocks and Shares," he said to himself, amused. "The Queen of Safes and Strongboxes. Incognita!" he added, becoming aware for the first time of her costume, a pair of full red trousers, and a very scanty sleeveless bodice of some material like white oilcloth. "Come along and be introduced," said Bobby. "Oh, damn-the Guildensterns have got her!" She was being besieged by the brigand, the tramp, and the girl in yellow trousers, who had come in the Isotta-Fraschini. 

The music began again, and the Golden Girl was swept into the dance by a demon in red tights. Sholto- 

Scott and Bobby sat down on the settee, and Sholto- 

Scott looked about him.

The big room had jade-green walls, and long magenta curtains, but its usual furniture had been removed. It was now arranged like a night club, with small tables round the walls. Waiters insinuated themselves cleverly among the dancers, and on one wall hung a large and brilliantly executed poster of

a Black Sheep. It was a young, merry, noisy crowd gathered there, chattering all together, and shrieking with laughter at incomprehensible jokes of their own, at anything or nothing. Nobody looked a day over twenty-five. "That's Ivo Fitzhubert, Labour Member for Bilberry North," explained Bobby. "And that's Fabian

Fane, who wrote Potiphar's Grandmother-fellow in the sombrero, talking to Lady Pamela Pamphlett. 

They say he makes over a thousand a week."

Sholto Scott looked at the celebrated features of Lady Pamela, whom he had met before, surf-bathing at Honolulu. "Why do the girls go in so for trousers?" he asked. "We-ell, it's not so easy for a girl to go as a female 

Black Sheep," replied Bobby. "You see, they don't look any different from the others. Hullo, there's Mrs. Staniforth." Across the room, down a fluctuating vista made by the dancers, Sholto-Scott caught sight of a small woman in a matronly evening gown of grey satin and jet. She sat at a table, perfunctorily smoking a cigarette, and perfunctorily sipping at a cocktail. Her face wore the amiable expression of indulgence proper to the middle-aged when watching the frolics of the young, but once, when she raised her dark eyes and looked straight across at Sholto-Scott, it was as if she had spoken to him.

"Bored!" he said to himself. "Bored to the backbone!" "Fitz!" came Bobby's voice at his elbow. "Miss 

Nevill would like to know you. My cousin, Captain 

Fitzroy Sholto-Scott-Miss Nicolette Nevill."

Bobby rolled the names on his tongue, pleasantly conscious that he was introducing the air-hero of the moment to the newest theatrical star.

Nicolette Nevill held out her hand with a far-away smile. She was the lady of the vine-leaves, a tall, beautiful creature in a vaguely classical drapery of purple and gold; the Empress Messalina, he discovered afterwards. "How too-oo wonderful to re-ally meet you!" cooed 

Nicolette. "Claud and I—my husband-listened in to the race on the wireless, and we were thrilled to the teeth. I think it must be mahv-lous to be in a big race like that! I'm crazy about flying. I always fly over to Paris to buy my frocks, and I always say if I were not an actress I would be an airwoman. I believe I've got the air sense." "I'm glad you're not," said Sholto-Scott earnestly. "You see, I went to Potiphar's Grandmother last night, and I want to go again. . . . Do you care to dance?" "I'd adore to, but-" she glanced at his left arm in its sling. "Ought you?" "I'm going to." He slipped an arm like steel round

her waist and dexterously guided her into the crowd. 

For all his quietness, Sholto-Scott was an exciting partner. His dancing had something of the brilliance and dash which marked his flying. "How divinely you dance!" sighed Nicolette. "Even with one arm in a sling. What's happened to it? Was it a crash?" "Yes," said Sholto-Scott. "A sort of one. I slipped -did some- on a banana skin the day after the racething to a tendon. No, thanks, it's not too painful. · · 

Jolly house, this!" "Yes, isn't it? Primula has the most mahv-lous flair. I always think that if I hadn't a flat myself in the Savoy, I'd adore to go hunting for some quaint old Georgian house like this. I have a sort of—feeling for beautiful things, if you know what I mean.

But the Savoy's so convenient for the Ambassadress."

By this time Sholto-Scott was experiencing the sensation, tiresomely familiar to him, of being stared at, openly or furtively, by nearly everybody in the room. Of course he realized that this was probably the lot of any man who happened to be dancing with beautiful Nicolette Nevill. Still, he knew that only two days ago his own face, with its unmistakable brows, had gazed out from every newspaper in the country. eye- Miss Nevill was also aware of the glances that fol-

lowed them round. She enjoyed them immensely, and went on discoursing with silvery nonchalance about her dogs, her country cottage, her art, and her political opinions. Then, at a turn of the dance, Sholto- 

Scott found himself looking into the surprised grey eyes of Primula Staniforth. 

He received a shock. He really must go and get introduced to his hostess! He didn't want her to take him for a gate-crasher.

When the music stopped, and Nicolette Nevill had been handed over to a hovering, jealous-eyed young man, whom he took to be Claud, he sought out Bobby. "Look here, Bobby, you've got to take me to Miss

Staniforth at once. I may be a fossil, but I do like my hostess at a party to be acquainted with me." "Right you are, old chap!" replied Bobby. "I want to see her myself."

Primula was talking to Fabian Fane, but when they approached, she dismissed him as casually as if he had been a schoolboy brother. Sholto-Scott had an amused impression of the petted young dramatist's astounded face, and the cool, frank gaze which the Golden Girl turned upon themselves. "Her Serene Highness," he thought. "She's a lovely girl, though, in spite of that abominable oilcloth bodice. Like a little Greek statuette." "My cousin, Captain Fitzroy Sholto-Scott," said Bobby.

Her face underwent a sudden charming change. 

The Serene Highness became an eager girl. "The?" she asked, though Sholto-Scott knew quite well that she had recognized him before. "No, a," he replied quickly. "Rats!" interposed Bobby. "You're the chap that won the Pinkerton Trophy, aren't you, and beat two world records ?" "Don't be modest," said Miss Staniforth. “It's terribly Victorian. Besides, I knew you at once. 

You're exactly like your photographs-only betterlooking," she added frankly. "I do envy you being in the Pinkerton Trophy Race. It must have been too marvellous. Run away, Bobby, and dance with Poppy —over there, Lucrezia Borgia. I'm going to have this with Captain Sholto-Scott." 

The Golden Girl made no inquiry about his arm. 

She had seen him dancing with Nicolette Nevill, and if he could dance with Nicolette he could dance with 

Primula. That was that!

She was enchanting to dance with; he might have been holding Titania in his arms. And her off-hand, quick questions about the race showed an intelligent grasp of technical points that astonished him. He told her so. "Oh, I've been flying for three years," she said. "I have two Moths, and I've just bought one of the new Kingsley-Drake Dragonflies."

Sholto-Scott stared. The Kingsley-Drake Dragonfly was the last word both in efficiency and expensiveness, and this girl spoke of buying one as if it were a bunch of violets. "Lucky you!" he remarked. "To be able to buy a

Dragon. 99 "Yes, they're marvellous, aren't they? Perhaps you'll fly over with me one day, and have a look at mine when it comes. I keep them down at Havisham, my grandfather's place. . . . Damn! there's Prince 

Dmitri! . . . I didn't know he was coming. I've got to go and receive him, I suppose. Dashed nuisance! 

You don't mind, do you? I'll see you again. 99 She gave him a glance from the very depths of her grey eyes, and turned away. Each of them felt a little stirring of excitement. He knew that she liked him, and she knew that he would think of her again. 

He watched her walking with her slight graceful swagger to the door, where a slender, weary-eyed young man in the dress of a Balkan brigand stood smiling mechanically and exchanging polite remarks with Mrs. Staniforth. He saw her sketching a nonchalant curtsey, and gliding off on the arm of his 

Serene Highness into the dance.

The party rioted along. All round him, people did their best to be Black Sheep, and with some success, to judge by the shrieks of laughter. Sholto-Scott was soon pounced upon by Lady Pamela Pamphlett, and

carried off in triumph to a lively and admiring group, called "Poffles" and "Babs" and other odds and ends of names. He signed his autograph on several handkerchiefs, a slipper, and a fan. He danced with a novelist, a musical comedy star, and a Lady Moira Something, and he said the same things to every one of them. The conversation of a popular hero is, he found, apt to become standardized. But he thoroughly enjoyed himself.

After midnight, however, his injured arm was beginning to ache dully. Perhaps he ought to have it 

X-rayed again. Perhaps he had been a fool to come. 

A creeping weariness and misgiving began to invade him. Could it be that one is really too old at thirtyone, or was it only his arm? 

He looked round for Bobby, but Bobby was laughing so very heartily at the jokes of a sad-faced man in rags and tatters that Sholto-Scott was not unkind enough to drag him away.

He decided that he would slip off quietly by himself after saying good-bye to his hostess. He had an idea that not all of Primula's guests would trouble to be so formal, but he felt a sneaking desire to speak to the 

Golden Girl again, and look once more into her cool grey eyes. 

He went in search of Primula. Bad luck! The Balkan more-or-less royalty had selected the very same moment for his departure. His farewells were pro-

tracted. He and his equerry stood clicking heels and bowing, talking and smiling, till Sholto-Scott sat impatiently down at a table to wait his chance. 

And it was at that moment that a black Pierrette drifted across the shining floor, and sank, with a billowing of tulle skirts, into the opposite chair. 

§ 

He had not noticed her before-Primula's party had become a little overcrowded-though her rather oldfashioned fancy dress picked her out from the anxious modernity of the other feminine Black Sheep. They would probably have had less skirt and more back, he reflected. "Thank you, Dickie, that was very nice," she said, looking up at her partner. And after a moment's pause: "Please don't wait. I'm too tired to talk." 

With apologetic murmurs, the fresh-faced boy went off, and the black Pierrette, becoming aware that she had a neighbour at the table, instinctively turned her head and glanced at Sholto-Scott.

The astonished young man leapt in his seat. The black Pierrette looked at him vaguely, quite unconscious of the emotions she was exciting. 

Her face was made up with rouge and powder to a

doll-like prettiness of complexion, and under faintly surprised eyebrows her periwinkle-blue eyes still kept the memory of some past joke. It lurked about the long corners of her lovely mouth. And yet, for all its gaiety, her face was indefinably haunted by the ghost of some sorrow. A lock of fair hair had escaped from under the little black Pierrette hat she wore, and was falling across her forehead. She put up her hand mechanically to brush it back, and Sholto-Scott was wafted far away in space and time from the jadewalled studio and the noisy Black Sheep. 

He was ten years old again, and staying with his Aunt Edith at Understrand, on the Norfolk coast. 

She had taken him out to tea with her, to some people called the Jolliffes, who lived in a house on the cliff. 

He could still smell the hot fragrance of their treeless, sun-drenched garden.

There had been Roy and Sylvia, about his own age, to play with, but he had also been aware of Norah, Sylvia's elder sister, in the background, because she had seemed to him so intensely grown-up; about the same age as Aunt Edith, he thought, or even perhaps his mother. 

There was also Flip. 

Everybody called her Flip; he never heard her real name. She was Norah's school friend, come to spend a fortnight with her, and she had a tremendously long fair pigtail, so that Norah could hardly have been as

old as Aunt Edith after all-probably sixteen or seventeen at the most.

Flip did not pay much attention to him, but he was not able to keep his eyes off her. She and Norah walked about arm-in-arm, talking earnestly about immensely important matters, and as she talked, Flip's dark, delicate eyebrows looked more and more surprised, and one fair lock kept falling over her periwinkle-blue eyes and having to be pushed or shaken back again. 

And when an elderly man came out of the house, she rushed at him and caught him by the arm, coaxing and wheedling him. Fitz never knew what it was all about, but he vehemently hoped that the elderly man would give Flip exactly what she wanted.

After tea, they all went into a field on the edge of the cliff, where two white goats were kept. One of them began to chew the ends of Flip's pigtail, and she had whirled round with shrieks of laughter and dismay.

It had seemed then the funniest thing that had ever happened; he wished he could find jokes half as amusing nowadays. He could still see Flip, slender and long-legged like a colt, against a background of sea and sky and scarlet poppies.

After that, all the princesses in the fairy tales which he still read surreptitiously, had the face of Flip; and later on, Juliet had a long fair pigtail, and Helen and Cleopatra had white skins, lightly tanned and freckled by the gentle ardour of an English sun.

Twenty-one years ago. . . .

The black Pierrette moved a little uneasily in her chair, and Sholto-Scott came back to himself. What was happening to him? Overwhelmed by the rush of memories, he was staring hard at a perfect stranger, who had certainly nothing to do with Flip, even if she did possess some of her tricks of manner and appearance. Flip would now be thirty-seven or thirty-eight, somewhere about forty, and the black Pierrette didn't look anywhere near that. Besides, Flip would be married! Anybody so fascinating as he remembered her would infallibly be snapped up the moment she was old enough.

He felt annoyed with himself. "I beg your pardon," he said stiffly, turning rather red, "for staring at you like that, but you reminded me very strongly of someone I used to know, long ago."

She turned her blue eyes on him consideringly, as if she also was trying to track down some memory. "Long ago?" she repeated. "How long ago? You don't mean you couldn't have been in the War?" "Yes, I was," said Sholto-Scott. "Two years. One in training and one in Mespot. Air Force.”

He did not say that he had added two years on to his age. "Oh," she said. "Then you aren't one of the boys

I met in France. I thought perhaps you see, I was in 

France from 1915 till after the Armistice." "Nursing?" he asked. Such a voice, he thought, would be delightful in a nurse. It brought to mind the old-fashioned word "dulcet." "No. Driving lorries. And afterwards an ambulance." "You never drove lorries!" cried Sholto-Scott, looking at the slight build of the Pierrette, her slender wrists and ankles, her small hands and feet. "Didn't I, though?" she replied gaily. “And did the repairs. Why, I remember once in '17-" She broke off with an apologetic laugh. "I mustn't bore you with my antiquated reminiscences. We veterans " "You couldn't bore me," said Sholto-Scott, with an earnestness that surprised himself, and caused the black Pierrette to raise her eyebrows a little. "Oh, very well then, I'll tell you about it, and then you tell me one about Mespot. That'll be fair, won't it?" 

So, carelessly enough, she told him an astonishing tale of danger and hard work, and he ransacked his memory for one about Mespot. He had forgotten all about the Golden Girl. It gave him a queer thrill of excitement to be talking to this chance-met stranger, for all through their conversation Flip seemed to vanish and return.

While he was plodding through his Mespot yarn with only half his attention the other half was watching out for Flip-she stared at him with a little frown of perplexity. "Do you know-" she said when he had finished, "your face is perfectly familiar to me, somehow, and yet I can't think where I've seen it before."

Sholto-Scott knew. It was on the front page of the 

Prattler, and the Onlooker, the Daily Post, the Morning News, and all the rest.

But evidently the black Pierrette had not been studying the newspapers with any care. "Would you mind telling me your name?" she went on. "I should so like to know." "Fitzroy Sholto-Scott," he said, and waited for an outburst of what he would have called "gush" from most women. But it did not come. She looked at him with a smile that was deprecating, even a little coaxing. "I've heard it hundreds of times, of course," she said. "But my memory is shocking. I'm sure you've done something wonderful—I'd love to know what.” "Oh, I just won a race," said Sholto-Scott. "An air race. In America. Three weeks ago.' 99 She waved her hands about in the air, and Flip was startlingly present again. "I know. The Pinkerton Trophy. How stupid of me! But I happened to be in Berlin at the time, and

much too strung up to wade through the German newspapers. I was tracking down a little pink teacup. However that's very tame compared to an air race. How fast did you go?" 99 "Three-forty-five miles an hour on an average,' replied Sholto-Scott. "But I want to know about the pink tea-cup. It intrigues me immensely. It's much more exciting than an air race."

The black Pierrette made an amused little sound. "What an un-modern sentiment!" she remarked. "But really, there is something thrilling about this particular cup. You see, once upon a time, very long ago 99 She got no further. As suddenly as if she had sprung out of the polished floor, the Golden Girl stood before them, flushed with long dancing, but fresh and unwearied still. "Hullo, Aunt Rosey!" she said coolly. "I thought you said you'd have to go at twelve, like Cinderella." "So I have!" cried the black Pierrette, jumping up. "What is it now? Heavens! it's half-past one, and I've got to be up at seven! I must fly." 

And she would instantly have flown if Sholto-Scott, accustomed to lightning promptitude of action, had not put in his word. "Please introduce me," said he to Primula.

There was, unconsciously, something imperious in his tone which made the Golden Girl stare at him. Not

in this way did people talk to Primula Staniforth! "Miss Forey-Captain Fitzroy Sholto-Scott," she said meekly. Deep down in her being, some bird began singing; a little wave of happiness ran through her veins. 

The black Pierrette smiled at him, shaking a fair lock back out of her periwinkle-blue eyes. "It's been very nice to meet you," she said, holding out her hand. "Hail and farewell!' Good-night, Captain Sholto-Scott! Good-night, Prim dear!" "But what about the pink tea-cup?" said the persistent young man, whose tenacity was a byword among his friends. "I shan't be able to sleep." "Oh-won't you?" The Pierrette laughed. "Well, we're always in to tea on Sundays-half-past four." 

She went away, taking Flip with her, and he was left with one of the loveliest girls he had ever seen, whose grey eyes now looked at him with a softness, a touch of shyness that thrilled and surprised him.

They danced together again. Holding her supple young body in his right arm, a little intoxicated by the fragrance of youth and freshness which seemed to breathe from her burnished hair and satin skin, Sholto-Scott forgot the dull ache in his left arm and went on dancing. They talked shop gaily and without apology, and it was nearly three o'clock when he finally retrieved Bobby and took his leave. "Er-by the way," said he, after farewells had all

been spoken. "What is Miss Forey's address?" He had nearly called her Flip! "She lives here," said Primula. "Wednesdaydon't forget." "Rather not!" he said, with a last glance. "Aunt Rosey!—Aunt Rosey!" the black Pierrette mused as she hurried along a jade-carpeted corridor on the second floor. "Why-she hasn't called me that for years!"

WHEN she opened the lacquer-red door of the room at the end of the passage the black Pierrette felt for a moment, as she always did, that she had stepped into the eighteenth century. For Lambert Jacks had left this room untouched.

The old ivory-white panelling remained on the walls; there was a powdering closet, and deep window seats, and shutters. The beautiful Hepplewhite settee and chairs might have borne the weight of Mrs. 

Thrale and Fanny Burney, and the small child's portrait over the mantelpiece "Miss Harriet Hornblower"—was certainly painted by Romney.

There were other things in the room, however, which did not belong to the world of Miss Burney: a fat 

Victorian arm-chair, shapeless and comfortable and upholstered in red damask, squat book-cases full of modern books, and two or three large photograph studies of Primula standing about on shelves and tables.

An inner door, leading to a small bedroom, now opened, and Mrs. Staniforth came out; not the Mrs. 

Staniforth whom Sholto-Scott had watched a couple of hours before, but a slight, schoolgirlish figure in a red dressing-gown with dark hair fluffed out round her pale face. She had a Thermos jug in her hand. "Hullo, Rosalind!" she said. "You're just in time for some hot cocoa. It's like old times at Newnham.... What's the matter?" • · · For the black Pierrette had dropped into a chair, and sat hunched up in the midst of her tulle like a dejected marionette. "Oh, nothing, Vivi dear. Only an attack of middle age. I suppose I'd have been more sensible not to have gone to-night. . . . But I really did enjoy myself until quite near the end. 

And then I suddenly felt like a fool." "I didn't," said Vivien promptly. "I was there for two hours, and I came away bursting with pride of intellect. I was bo-o-o-red." She made the word last nearly a minute. "They had a police raid before you

came in. Jack Twickenham and Freddie Iffley and

Lord Chiswick and two or three I don't know came dressed up as policemen and arrested people, and they gave false names-Pamela Pamphlett said she was

Elizabeth Barrett Browning-and it was an immense success. I expect they're laughing about it still. But I left off being amused after I'd laughed for half an hour. So when His Stupidity had arrived, I took myself off. I don't know whether it was etiquette or not, and I don't care." "Vivi," said her sister. "Do you think there's anything in it?" 

Vivien's face grew troubled. "You mean Prince 

Dmitri and Prim? I don't know. You see, unfortunately, she's able to buy herself a very expensive husband indeed, and she may think it good value to be a demi-semi-royalty. I hope not. But I don't know.

Prim's a modern daughter and I'm a modern mother, so some day my son-in-law will be presented to me out of the blue, so to speak. She's a darling, of course, and 

I'm sure she's really fond of me, but she doesn't tell me anything at all that really matters. I haven't half so much influence as Lady Mary." Here Vivien made a face. “I can't help it. I've done my best, but I can't compete with Lady Mary. She's a grande dame, and a great beauty, and besides-" Vivien checked herself. "No, I won't repeat scandal about my motherin-law. tige among the young things. So Primula listens reverently to her, and sees me as a comfortable background-of a becoming neutral shade.” "And you've given up your life to her," said Rosalind indignantly. "What else could I do? After all, she was my one and only baby."

I'm not the head mistress type." "Are you ever sorry you eloped with Ralph?" asked Rosalind, sipping her chocolate. 

Vivien shot a quick glance at her. "I have been terribly.. Oh, terribly!" she added in a low voice. "When I've felt-side-tracked, and—and at other times. But not, on the whole. If I hadn't, there wouldn't have been any Primula. And Primula's quite an achievement." "She is," agreed Rosalind heartily. "She's lovely.

She looks like a little Tanagra statuette, and she dances like a-like a wave of the sea, and she's so

The old fogeys run down the present, and brag about their young days; but the new ones are tumbling over themselves to flatter the young and run down their own contemporaries. 

They think, poor dears, it shows how modern they are themselves. Lady Mary's always doing it. Did you read her latest interview in the Daily Scream? 'I

Adore the Modern Young,' it was headed, and in it she set forth how narrow and underhand and petty girls have always been up to the present minute!" 

Vivien snorted. She detested Lady Mary Staniforth, and not in secret. "Think of the girls we were at school and college with," she went on. "Frank and honest and outspoken! Why, I never came across anyone more frank and honest and outspoken than you were, Flip, when you were a schoolgirl. You were terrifying sometimes.

Only you didn't babble about birth-control and perversion as these babes do.” "But don't you think the present boys and girls are fine and frank and honest ?" "If they are, they are," said Vivien inexorably. "And if they're not, they're not. Just like us in our time. I hate generalisations. The present batch is

about the same as our batch. Of course, they're permitted to babble about more subjects than we were. 

To-night, for instance, I overheard Poppy Singleton discussing contraception with Freddy Iffley. But I didn't think her fine and frank. I thought her an ignorant and foolish brat, and I longed to spank her.' "Is Poppy really going to marry Freddie, do you think?” asked Rosalind, with some curiosity. 99 "Only over Elfrida's dead body,” replied Vivien. "And Sir Toby's. I don't blame them. If Primula brought me a son-in-law like Freddie Iffley I'd drop some arsenic into his cocktail with a perfectly clear conscience."

Rosalind laughed. She now sat upright, spreading out her tulle skirts. The hot chocolate had brought colour into her face underneath the rouge. She was recovering from the effects of that "Aunt Rosey," spoken half an hour before. "It seems," she remarked, "that there are some troubles which I have escaped.' 99 "I only wish you hadn't," exclaimed Vivien vehemently. "Why, why did Richard pick up that bomb?" 

Rosalind looked at her with startled eyes. Her lips quivered, and she frowned a little as if in pain. "Don't, Vivi," she murmured. "He saved the lives of six men. I'm glad to think of that-now."

"I know, but I can't help it. I do with all my heart and soul wish he had come back to marry you." 99 "So do I," said Rosalind, turning her head away.
