§ 

ROSALIND was lying in bed in the luxurious bed-sitting-room which she occupied in Primula's big house. 

The clock had just struck four, and the party was now a thing of the past—the remote past, for such festivities, of half an hour ago. But though she twisted and tossed indignantly on her embroidered linen pillows, sleep did not come. And she had promised to be at Waterloo at eight o'clock to see Stella Wilcox off to 

China again.

Rosalind did not like to lie awake at night. She

feared it, because sometimes in the unreasoning hours after midnight Miss Forey, the serene and sweet-tempered, with her subdued sense of humour and her expert knowledge of Chinese ceramics, would vanish utterly, and in her place there tossed and turned another, far more passionate creature-Rosalind Philippa, whose schoolgirl nickname had been Philip, or Flip. 

Flip had been the gayest of the gay; and at times, the saddest of the sad. She had enjoyed everything ardently: chocolate pudding on Tuesdays, Miss

Leonard's history lessons, the Rector talking about a "half-warmed fish," the Rector talking about Immortality, the lovely bareness of Beachy Head, and the sea-gulls flying round the heads of the "crocodile," roasting chestnuts in the Prefects' Room, discussing marriage and party frocks with Norah Jolliffe, and darling Eastgate in the holidays.

Most of Rosalind's early recollections were of school, for Mr. Philip Forey, finding the presence of two attractive daughters rather an embarrassment in the household of young men whom he was cramming for various examinations, conscientiously packed them off to good boarding schools during the term. In the holidays he hoped for the best.

This had worked very well for a long time. The brilliant Vivien, with her lively tongue, was rather too much given to poking fun at the youths, who, it must

33 

be confessed, were seldom intellectual giants. But at least this seemed to prove her immune to dangerous sentiment. Rosalind, seven years younger, was only a child.

And then, in the middle of Vivien's third year at Newnham, when all her tutors were prophesying great triumphs ahead, she had come home for the Christmas vacation. Ralph Staniforth was spending that Christmas at Eastgate too. Sir Everard and Lady Mary were in Egypt, and besides, it was imperative that Ralph should at least scrape through his examinations.

Ralph had accepted this decree with astonishing resignation for the spoilt handsome young mother'sdarling that he was; and three weeks later, Vivien had run away with him to be married in London, leaving a note on her dressing-table, fastened-Vivien could not resist a joke to a fat pincushion which belonged to Mrs. Gillett, the cook.

Rosalind was only fifteen at the time, but she could remember afterwards every detail of Vivi's last day at home; how gentle and submissive she had been, how more than once her dark eyes had filled with tears, how of her own accord she had mended a long slit in her father's alpaca coat-Vivi detested sewing—and how she had given her, Rosalind, her coral necklace "for ever."

Rosalind had been packed back to school immedi-

ately, but she gathered something of her father's vexation and distress; distress that his brilliant Vivien should have thrown away all her gifts upon a young nincompoop; sharp vexation that the nincompoop should be one of the most eligible young men in England, and under his own charge. Nothing of the sort had ever happened before in Mr. Forey's well-known and well-run establishment, and he could not help it now, but that did not save him from the displeasure of Lady Mary Staniforth, who came rushing back from Egypt in what would have been strong hysterics if she had been her grandmother. She gave Mr. Forey the most disagreeable hour of his life.

Ralph and Vivien had spent a struggling year or two in the winter of the Staniforth discontent, and then things changed. Reggie, the second Staniforth boy, became a monk. Nigel, the youngest, was killed playing polo. Ralph was the only one left. Besides, by this time Mary Primula had come into the world. 

Rosalind heard only echoes of these events in Vivi's gay letters, for as soon as the school year was up, her father had taken her to another school in Germany.

Sometimes he came out to her for the holidays; sometimes she spent them with school friends. But she saw no more of the jolly young men at Eastgate. When she was there, they were not. Mr. Forey turned a grim eye upon the unfolding charm of his fascinating Flip. 

And then-Rosalind's heart beat painfully even

now, in London twenty years after, lying under Primula's silken eiderdown with the early morning sounds beginning outside then, she went to spend a week with Norah Jolliffe at Understrand. Dick Jolliffe was home on leave, the sailor brother whom she had never seen. He was just going out to the China station. 

Every day of that week, it seemed to Flip, the sun shone in a vault of turquoise. Every night the sky blazed with a million stars, and warm and fragrant airs wandered about. The nightingale sang. Flowers broke into blossom in the grass, and on the branches of the trees which thronged the garden. 

But there were no trees in the Jolliffes' cliff garden. It was winter, the ground was hard as iron, a bitter wind blew in from the sea, and only sea-birds screamed overhead. It was her own life breaking into flower, her own heart singing.

On the fourth day, they stood on the path below the cliff, a little sheltered from the merciless wind which blew the sea-gulls sideways in the grey sky. They were talking rather disconnectedly about aeroplanes, and yet not about aeroplanes. And then-a nothing, a turn of her head, an upward glance of her blue eyes, and aeroplanes dropped out of existence. She was in 

Dick Jolliffe's arms, being kissed a hundred, a thousand times, and returning his kisses with all her heart and soul. "Darling-darling-darling," murmured Dick,

over and over again. They had never said a word of love before. He was not eloquent, but if he had been a poet, he would have written another Love in the Valley. Flip felt it, and was content. "I've got your ring," said Dick at last, fumbling in a pocket. "Got it in Norwich yesterday. Hope you'll like it, darling. Of course, it isn't anything like good enough for you, but I chose it because it's the colour of your eyes. 99 "You do take things for granted, don't you?" murmured Flip, and never said another word about his presumption.

She gave him her hand, and Dick put the turquoise ring on her third finger. The wind dropped for a moment and in the pause the two young creatures gazed at each other, startled, with a certain sense of awe. "It's lovely," said Flip softly, putting it to her lips. "And now I suppose we're engaged. Oh, Dick, we've only known each other four days." "That's got nothing whatever to do with it,” said he, stoutly. "We were meant for each other. Why, if you hadn't come a week before you were expected we should never have met at all.” "Oh, Dick!"

They clung together, appalled at the narrowness of their escape. "Why shouldn't we get married-now?" whispered 

Dick, after a pause.

Flip looked up into the handsome dark-browed young face. "I'd love it!" she said. "But there isn't time. You're going to China next Friday, and I've got to go back to school for another term." "I could get a special licence. I'm twenty-one. How old are you-Rosalind? I hate 'Flip." " "Nearly eighteen. Seventeen and—and a half." "Damn! That means you'll have to get your father's consent." "He won't let us." "Why not? We'll jolly well make him listen to 

Sweetheart, your throat is as soft as a reason. bird's. • • • 

Perhaps, next Tuesday or Wednesday, you'll be my wife!"

Mr. Forey did not listen to reason. He even refused his consent to an immediate engagement. He liked 

Dick Jolliffe, but they were too young. Flip was only seventeen and two months. They could wait three years till Dick came back from China, and then, if they were still of the same mind— But they refused to wait. They sent notices of their engagement to The Times and the Morning Standard. They announced it everywhere. Flip flaunted her engagement ring, and Dick contrived to spend almost all the rest of his leave with his schoolgirl fiancée. 

They would not even promise not to correspond. Mr. 

Forey was helpless.

And then Dick went to China, and Rosalind, comforting herself with the turquoise ring, returned to the sedate and cultivated atmosphere of her Dresden school. Twice a week Dick wrote to her, only missing three times, when he was delirious with pneumonia. 

And Flip, the lazy, who could scarcely force herself to answer an invitation, spent most of her leisure time in writing long, long loving letters in return. In their case, absence did make the heart grow fonder. They came closer to one another every day. 

Mr. Forey and the Jolliffes were obliged to acquiesce. At least, Mr. Forey reflected, it prevented any further entanglement when Flip came home to keep house for him. His pupils (who included at the moment the Earl of Clackmannan and Lord Charles Temple-Templeton-Temple) were as amply protected by her gay indifference as by the modestly flaunted blue engagement ring.

At last the three years dragged to an end, and Dick came home. Rosalind was with his mother at the London station; she was running to meet him; she was throwing herself into his eager arms. "Half-past six, Miss," said the voice of Esther, the housemaid. There was a slight rattle of early-morning-tea china. Esther moved across the room to draw back the green silk curtains, and Rosalind, with a familiar pang, awoke to here and now.

8 "I LOOK at least forty-five," she said to herself, relentlessly inspecting the face which stared back at her from the mirror. It was white and drawn, with dark circles round the periwinkle-blue eyes, and in the hard morning light she saw, or fancied she saw, all sorts of truthful but unbecoming lines-round her eyes, across her forehead, round her mouth. "Aunt Rosey." 

No wonder!

It was true she had only had two hours' sleep, and was tired-out and nervous, but in her present mood she refused all comfort. The tulle Pierrette dress lay on the floor in a heap. She looked at it with disgust. 

How could she have been such a fool? "Aunt Rosey." 

And quite soon, possibly in two or three months, Primula would very likely marry one of her young men-Captain Sholto-Scott perhaps and have a baby in a year or so, and then she would be "Greataunt Rosey." 

But at this Rosalind had, reluctantly, to laugh.

With another withering glance at the black tulle foolishness she put on her severest garments, a hard grey tailor-made and a plain black felt hat. In them she felt anonymous, invisible, secure from the scorn of the young. She brushed her shining hair out of sight

beneath the brim, and unconsciously assumed an expression to match the coat and skirt. 

At Waterloo, Stella Wilcox's square figure was encased in a very similar coat and skirt of grey. A broad-brimmed black felt was perched at an unbecoming angle above her shrewd weatherbeaten face. 

Poppy Singleton would have said that she looked like nothing on earth. "And she's a year younger than I am," thought 

Rosalind, with a pang. "Hullo, Rosey!" she called, in a deep, mannish voice. "Nice of you to come and push me off."

She gave Rosalind a hearty, painful handshake, and an admiring stare. “Pretty as a picture, still," she said, "Picture of a ruin," said Rosalind to herself. "Glad to be going back, Stell?" "We-ell. I feel it a bit leaving mother, of course, but-" her eyes lit up. "I'm aching to be on the job again. Want to know what my nurses have been up to while I've been away."

Dr. Stella Wilcox was in charge of a mission hospital and training home for nurses in a Chinese city. "She's wonderful," reflected Rosalind in the bus going towards South Kensington. "She absolutely doesn't care whether she's pretty or ugly, dowdy or smart. She's beyond it. She's wrapped up in her work.

As I've been up to now. Well-I must get back to it again."

She worked the whole morning in Cromwell Road among the priceless vases of the Van Lank collection, which her late employer, Sir Benjamin Cuffe, had bought a few weeks before his death. She was translating the catalogue from the original Dutch, and adding a good deal of new material. Sir Benjamin Cuffe had left his collections to the nation, after the death of his wife, but he had expressed a wish that the catalogue should be completed by his valued secretary, Miss Rosalind Forey.

So Flip quietly scribbled away in the library, now staring critically at some beautiful Ming pot, blue, like summer midnight, or a rich, satiny cream-colour, and now consulting a reference book. A shaft of sunlight touched her fair head. The servants, all of them elderly, made her feel younger, and gradually a "Chinese calm," as she called it, seemed to slide into her soul. Perhaps there was some influence issuing from the beauty round about her, something of the patience and tranquillity of the unknown Chinese craftsmen, which kept one contented in one's rut. Vivien seemed to think so.

At twelve she put on her hat again, and looked at herself in the mirror with less dissatisfaction. "This hat isn't fashionable," she thought. "But it is becoming, especially if I pull my hair out a little. I'll buy

myself a new blouse, and have lunch at Fuller's." 

She bought herself a charming lace blouse, a little too expensive, and exhilarated by this piece of extravagance, went lightly down Regent Street towards Oxford Circus. A passing stranger gave her a bold stare of admiration; impertinent, of course, but it did show that one wasn't a Gorgon. Not unpleasant at thirtyseven. "Ros-a-lind! Ros-a-lind!" cried a laughing, breathless voice behind her, and someone caught her by the arm. She turned round.

Primula; and some distance away, sauntering up and taking her time about it, Poppy Singleton.

Instantly she was back again in the light of common day, a woman of thirty-seven. "Where were you rushing off to? I had to run to catch you up," said Primula. "I want you to come and have lunch with us. We're going to Carson's. Poppy, you know Rosalind-Miss Forey?" 

Poppy gave her a flashing smile, and an unfriendly glance. She was passionately taken up with her own affairs, and could not imagine why Prim should choose to inflict this dowdy relation upon them at a moment when-well, at a critical moment! However, Prim did all the paying, no small consideration when you adore 

Lobster Newburg only less than Coupe-Jacques Jeritza. "We shall be late," was all she said, gazing im-

patiently round at the moving cars and buses, the light glittering on the shop-windows, and the pale blue sky. "No, thank you, Prim, I was going-" began

Rosalind, but Primula cut her short, raising her little grey-gloved hand to a passing taxi. She packed them into it without wasting any more words. "The Staniforth manner," thought Rosalind, submitting to it with some amusement.

She felt less amused when she had a full-length view of herself and her companions in a mirror, as they went up the pink-carpeted steps into Carson's fashionable restaurant. Prim and Poppy were two flowers of girlish elegance; Prim in a slim suit of pale grey, with pale grey fox round her shoulders, Poppy in a short coat of astrakan over a red pleated skirt. 

Each of them had the newest little absurdity of a hat, the neatest of shoes, and an expensive handbag which she would probably throw away next week. Prim's appleblossom skin was lightly dusted with powder, but Poppy had concealed her clear gipsy colouring with a mask of make-up that was almost apricot, and she had painted a vermilion mouth on top of her own red lips. Prim wore violets, Poppy gardenias. "I'm exactly like the ghost of a district visitor," thought Rosalind with the morbid self-depreciation which follows a sleepless night. "Dowdy, and washedout, and old."

It was worse when they sat at a table decorated with scarlet carnations in the white-walled dining-room. 

A flood of sunlight streamed in upon them, and they had time to look at one another.

Poppy Singleton was a young person who never took the trouble to control her expression, or conceal the thoughts which passed across her face like a procession across a stage. "Frump!" said Poppy's face, distinctly.

The experienced Primula ordered the meal, which included Lobster Newburg and Coupe-Jacques Jeritza; and while they were engaged on their grapefruit cocktail, she pointed out to Rosalind the celebrities who seemed to throng the place. "That's Lady Hunter-Fox with Fabian Faneover there in the green finger nails. And that's Sir 

Frank, coming in with Barbarova. . . . What rotten luck! . . . And that's the Yugo-Latvian Ambassador with Nicolette Nevill—the fat man in grey. The other man's the Duke of Bamborough. · · 99 She rattled on very kindly, and, Rosalind felt, carefully too, to avoid shocking her. Poppy was frankly searching for somebody. 

Then Primula took an interest in Rosalind's parcel, and she had to tear open a corner to show the lace blouse. "I think it's a darling," said Prim. "Isn't it, Poppy?"

4."Sweet," said Poppy politely. "As if it mattered!" said her face, and her eyes wandered round the room again. 

Rosalind was rather puzzled by her niece's unwonted attentions, not knowing that this was a bit of reparation. Primula felt a little ashamed of the obscure instinct which had made her say "Aunt Rosey" the night before. She didn't understand why she had done it, or at least she didn't want to understand, and didn't mean to inquire. It had made her-Prim Staniforth-do something which she dimly felt wasn't cricket. Besides-Rosalind really was rather pathetic! 

All at once Poppy sat up alertly. "Oh, Prim!" she exclaimed. "There's your man." "Which man?" asked Primula. “I've got several.” "The latest one, darling. The Pinkerton man. Captain Sholto-Scott." "O-oh!" said Primula, smiling broadly. "But he isn't mine, angel. I never set eyes on him till last night." "Well, but you made up for it last night, didn't you, dearest?" "What do you mean?" asked Primula, her eyes dancing. “Oh—nothing. But I do think he's terribly fascinating. So quiet, and yet one feels he could be a perfect devil if he liked."

Miss Singleton could bestow no higher praise. "Bill says he's marvellous in a race," said Primula carelessly. "Who's he with?" 

"Sir Marmaduke Something-the Civil Aviation man," replied Poppy.

Lobster Newburg put an end to this little conversation. Rosalind's back was turned to the door, but she had a brief glimpse of Sholto-Scott's dark profile as he followed his host into an inner room. Attractive young man, and not at all "devilish," in spite of Poppy

Singleton. His eyes had been wonderfully-friendly, she thought. Something about him, his eyebrows perhaps, reminded her of Dick. 

Remembering Vivien's forebodings, she could not help watching Primula's face when his name was mentioned. In vain. The cool young creature was armed at all points. She was not going to allow her rose-tints to deepen, and give away her secrets. But most likely she had none. Modern girls were so frank and open. 

They scorned concealment. 

How lovely she was! How fortunate that young man would be if Suddenly they were submerged by what seemed to Rosalind a tidal wave of young things, who had caught sight of them on their way out. 

Primula made some attempt to introduce them, but

Rosalind could only gather that Jean had just fixed it up with Noel, that they intended to perform at St.

Margaret's on 7th June, that Prim's party was marvellous, that Lady Moira had been out with the Quorn on Wednesday, and was Poppy going to be Scarlet

Fever at the Aches-and-Pains Ball? They were so young, so good to look at, so sure of their own enormous importance, so pleasantly arrogant, that Rosalind felt like a dim survival from an earlier age. When they subsided, Mr. Frederick Iffley was left behind. "Coffee? Thanks, Prim, that's a sound scheme," he said, sitting down in the empty chair next to Poppy, whose black eyes ceased their restless wandering and fixed themselves in bored abstraction on the wall opposite.

There was a little talk about the Aches-and-Pains Ball, to which Mr. Iffley was going as a Measle, and meanwhile Rosalind studied this young man who was so peculiarly objectionable to a number of highly respectable people. 

Outwardly he was very much like the other clean limbed, good-looking boys who had just gone. His clothes were just right, excellently cut, old enough and new enough.

His parents, without any money in their sense of the word, had sent him to Harrow and Cambridge. He was twenty-six, and if five healthy people died, he would be 

Lord Iffley.

Although he was dimly connected with the selling of cars, his real mission in life was to give Freddie Iffley

a good time. So far he had succeeded very well. And now he was in love, having taken the precaution to fall in love with an heiress. "Do you mind if we run?" she heard Primula say, after five minutes. "Rosalind and I have to be atat the British Museum by three." "British Museum?" said Freddie with a grin. He looked at his wrist watch. "You'll have to sprint.”"

Poppy seemed to come alive again, and Rosalind for all her misgivings had not the hardihood to insist on staying with them. "Prim!" she said indignantly outside. "How could you?" "Do you mean the British Museum? Well, we'll go there if you like." "How could you scheme to leave those two together? 

You know Lady Singleton would be very angry. 99 "I'm on their side," said Primula coolly. "Elfrida- 

Lady-Singleton is my enemy." "I'm not devoted to Lady Singleton," said Rosalind. "But I'm sure she's only thinking of Poppy's happiness."

Strange how she seemed destined to range herself with the aunts and mothers! "Well, then, she ought to let her marry Freddie." "I expect," said Rosalind cautiously, "she doesn't think him the kind of man who's likely to make her a good husband."

"I suppose you mean Women," said Primula, with a little air of sophistication. "Well-if there have been 

Women in his life, all men are the same all the ones worth knowing. I wouldn't give a ha'penny for a man without Experiences."

The child looked so pretty as she uttered these preposterous sentiments, that Rosalind could have kissed her as well as smacked her for her exasperating innocence. "There are disadvantages in having a dissipated husband," she submitted. "Very practical disadvantages, I mean." "I know" began Primula, then paused on the brink. She herself did not mind saying absolutely anything, but after all Aunt Rosey was an Edwardian, if not a Victorian, and therefore unaccustomed to hearing a spade called a spade. "Well, anyway," she went on, "they'll never be happy with anybody else. They're terribly in love." "Why don't they marry, then, without asking anyone? They're both of age." "That's just the point. Poppy doesn't come of age until she's twenty-eight, so far as the money's concerned, at any rate. Unless she marries before, with

Elfrida's consent. That's how old Adolphus fixed it up. So if they get married now, they'll have to starve for seven years and then they'll be too old to get any fun out of it."

"Hasn't he got a salary?” "Oh, only about £500 a year. He's an absolute pauper." "I see," said Rosalind, remembering a couple who had planned to live together in Paradise on very much that sum. "Of course it's impossible."

FITZROY SHOLTO-SCOTT came out of a door in Wim pole Street with a worried frown between his black brows. Mr. Cedric Sellars, the well-known specialist, was not satisfied with his arm. The injured tendon was not going on as it should, six weeks after the accident. 

He had been threatened with a nursing home, and he had a long programme of precautions and remedial measures, including massage from Mr. Sellars' special blind masseur.

But, though worried, he was not seriously disturbed in mind. He had no doubt that his arm would be fit again in a couple of months. One or two schemes would have to be postponed, that was all, and of course games were impossible for the present. 

Sholto-Scott sighed. All his life he had enjoyed the feeling that this body of his, trained and disciplined and hardened, swift and strong and graceful, was his

obedient servant. At five, he had been swimming like a minnow in the river pool below Inverdurie, his Scottish home. At ten he had two golf clubs, and his father was already boasting about the child's natural swing. At Fettes, he had been a mighty footballer, till he had run away at seventeen to join the Air Force by means of a couple of lies about his age. 

At this moment, walking sedately along sedate

Wimpole Street, he felt he would have given his ears for a fast game of tennis on a hard court, or failing that, a game of squash at the club. But he knew that what he actually would do was to go back to the rooms in Cleveland Place which had been lent him by his friend Anderson, get the housekeeper to make him a cup of tea, and wait in till five o'clock, when the masseur was coming to give him beans with those powerful fingers of his. 

Mrs. Renfrew, the housekeeper, was delighted to have the chance of making tea for him; he was so often out. This contact with Captain Sholto-Scott, the famous Captain Sholto-Scott, whose photograph had been in all the papers, was the greatest thrill in Mrs. 

Renfrew's experience since her wedding day. But he disappointed her in one way, she told her gossips; he was so quiet. After what her nephew Ernest, who was a flying fan, had told her about the great race, she'd expected something more-dashing, like! But the real

Captain Sholto-Scott might be just anybody-though

Ernest said there wasn't a bigger dare-devil in the world. 

One thing satisfied Mrs. Renfrew, however, the number of invitations which came for him—more in a fortnight than Mr. Anderson got in a year. They lay in heaps just inside the bureau; she saw them when she was dusting.

The waste-paper basket was full of pieces of cards from Countesses and Ladies and Sirs. He could not accept half of them, though he did go out a great deal. 

He would sit scowling and scribbling at the desk while she was clearing away the breakfast things, and later on Stanley, the boot-boy, who worshipped him, would have to carry a sheaf of replies to the post. 

Several times, imposing people had called; yesterday it was Sir Somebody Something with a high-nosed lady in Persian lamb and sable. Mrs. Renfrew had brought in tea in Mr. Anderson's best cups-Stanley having been despatched to the French shop round the corner for little pastries and sugar cakes—and had heard the high-nosed lady calling Captain Sholto- 

Scott "my dear boy” in a beautifully distinct voice, "talking posh,” as Stanley described it.

But to-day the prettiest visitor of all had come, driving up to the door in a big shining car, jade-green to go with her leather coat. She had looked straight at 

Mrs. Renfrew with her large grey eyes like a little princess, and had held out a pale grey envelope. "Will

you please give this to Captain Sholto-Scott?" she had said. "One of these Society girls," reflected Mrs. Renfrew, watching the green car round the corner. "Wonder if she's the one!" She loved a romance.

She put the grey envelope on the top of the pile which lay waiting for Sholto-Scott, and hung around to watch him opening it. But he didn't look at his letters till he had drunk two cups of tea, and then he sat sprawling about in a big leather chair, and stretched out his hand with a sigh.

Primula's he did not recognise, but the pretty black hand-writing roused his curiosity, and he put it on one side. He sorted the others into two heaps; handwritings he knew in one, unknown ones in the other. 

He threw the latter, much larger heap into a drawer. 

Let them wait till to-morrow, damn them!

He read his mother's letter first. His mother had married again, and was bringing up a large and very noisy second family away in Sidmouth. He skimmed the four pages; Molly, Bobby, Peggy, Billy, Anne and 

Basil-measles, rabbits, scholarships, dentists, school reports. When was he coming down to see them? The boys talked of nothing else. He put it away with a smile and a shake of the head. Poor dear mother! But those noisy youngsters would be the death of him just now. A letter from Mr. Murchison of Aerial Transit, Ltd. Business, but at the end Mr. Murchison hoped he

would take a good long rest after the terrific nervous strain of the Pinkerton Trophy Race. . . . Well he seemed likely to have to!

The third letter was from Brookes, the publishers. 

Would Captain Sholto-Scott consider writing a handbook of flying? They were sure there would be a big demand. .. Well, and why not? Write it while he was laid low, and make the damned tendon pay for itself. Then, with a quickening of interest, he opened the grey. "Dear Captain Sholto-Scott," said Primula's clear handwriting, like Primula's clear voice. "Don't forget you're coming down to Havisham on Wednesday to look at my Dragon. I'll call for you at ten o'clock. Car, not Moth. Yours very sincerely, Mary

Primula Staniforth." 

The Golden Girl! The Serene Highness! He hadn't forgotten Wednesday, but he had thought it might be only a royal caprice. It was plain that Primula Staniforth did whatever she pleased. Barbara had told him that she came into two fortunes when she was twentyone. That slip of a girl was mistress of half a million already, and there were the Staniforth millions to come. 

Sholto-Scott thought it a pity. Without that monstrous incubus of gold, she would be a little darling.

With it, she could hardly help being a young despot, whose little tyrannies were piquant and endearing now, but might come to be a rod of iron for those luckless ones who were dependent on her. He didn't despise

money, and had no sentimental preference for garrets and dinners of herbs. Not at all! Any girl was the better for five hundred a year or so. But half a millionnobody, man or woman, had a right to that kind of power.

The blind masseur had arrived, escorted by his pretty little wife, and proceeded to give his patient alternate doses of drowsy soothing and sharp endurance. 

During the drowsy spells, Sholto-Scott's thoughts wandered far and wide, but they always came back to 

Primula. Her clear gaze her little swagger ... her supple body she was a little darling! 

Lucky he had kept Wednesday free . . . he wanted very much to go down to Havisham with her. 

A-a-ah-h! That was a twinge!

Havisham strange how he was always in other people's houses, never in a place of his own. 

Rooms did not count. They were soulless, temporary, and often hideous, reflecting the tastes of some incredible person in the background, landlady or former tenant. He never stayed long enough in one place to have his own things about him. His mother's house in Sidmouth was a welter of healthy young Thompsons, all talking at the same time. . . . No! He was a nomad, living here and there, climbing other people's stairs. Here Sholto-Scott caught himself up. He must not grouse! No use pretending that he hadn't enjoyed

most of it enormously. People had been extraordinarily kind to him all his life, even in his schooldays at Fettes. 

When he had won fame in the air, they had of course been even kinder. He had stayed at the places of the great, and the houses of the interesting. He had made good friends and amusing acquaintances. He had met charming girls by the dozen, both here and in America, and had had abundant chances of falling in love. And yet he hadn't fallen in love; and fantastic as it might seem, he felt it was all because he had encountered, when far too young, the rapture and the pain of love at first sight.

Yes, he had enjoyed things as they came. Underneath, however, there was a secret dissatisfaction. He had a desire for order, peace, stability. He would like to have beauty round him. His father, who had the true collector's eye, had left him a few fine bits of furniture, Chippendale and Hepplewhite, and a couple of water colours, a David Cox and a Girtin. He himself had a taste for such things. ... Suppose, now, he were to disinter them from Harrod's depository and set up a home of his own? He could take a small house. 

But a house wasn't enough. There ought to be someone in it, someone waiting for him by the fire, someone with- No, he was not quite sure that she had large grey eyes and silver-gold hair. . . .

All at once Sholto-Scott startled the masseur with a shout of mocking laughter. By Jove, he had some

modesty! Of all the fatuous idiots! Here he was refusing in advance the wealthiest heiress in England! 

Why, she would not look at him for a moment. She was probably destined for the Ruritanian chap with the equerry.

He explained to the masseur that he had suddenly thought of something very funny-very funny indeed; but he did not tell him what it was. "Two and a half hours from Cleveland Place," said 

Primula, looking at her diamond wrist-watch. "Not too bad, considering we were held up at Wickford." 

She smiled into his eyes, and Sholto-Scott smiled back. The Golden Girl was a little darling this morning, not a young princess. Already some instinct was making her subdue her natural instinct to command, and soften her youthful ruthlessness when she was in his presence. He had sat beside her, admiring the judgment and dash with which she had driven the jadegreen car through the traffic and along the country roads, and finally had pulled up with beautiful precision at the foot of the steps at Havisham Hall. 

He helped her out, then looked around him. The long grey front of the Georgian house stretched out

on each side of a pillared portico, surmounted by a pediment. Daffodils thronged the flower-beds which flanked the wide space of gravel before it. Beyond, there were glimpses of undulating meadowland, with groups of splendid trees already misted with spring. 

He noticed how perfectly everything was kept; the hard flat surface of the gravel, the sharp edges of the turf that bordered the drive. "They lunch at one," said Primula, "and it's now half-past twelve. We'll have lunch first, and afterwards-we'll and look at the Dragon.' go 99 They looked at one another and burst out laughing. 

Up to now, not a word had been said about the Dragon, the ostensible reason for the expedition. 

The hall at Havisham was an imposing eighteenthcentury apartment, floored with black and white marble and supported by red marble columns brought back from some Grand Tour like the busts of Roman emperors which stood against the walls. The present owners had made it cheerful with a crackling log fire, some glowing Persian rugs, and masses of spring flowers. 

A deferential butler appeared, and a couple of footmen. "Oh, good morning, Dunn," said Primula graciously. "Will you please send someone to take the car round to the garage? Where's her ladyship?" "On the terrace, Miss, with Sir Everard." "I might have known Grandy was in the garden,"

said Primula to Sholto-Scott. "She loves out-ofdoors."

Sholto-Scott followed her through a long back hall on to a paved terrace bordered with flower-beds where more daffodils, hundreds of them, swayed and fluttered. Below it the ground fell away to a lake. As he walked along the terrace beside Primula he had an impression of a clear-washed spring sky with shreds of fleecy cloud, and the cawing of rooks from a rookery close by. This was in the background of his mind, however; he was chiefly conscious of curiosity about the two people he was to meet-and especially Lady Mary. 

They were standing at the end of the terrace, with their backs to Sholto-Scott and Primula, a tall elderly man, stiffly bending to examine something in a flowerbed, and a slender woman, almost as tall, standing beside him, equally intent. Sholto-Scott looked about for the handsome elderly lady. But she was not there.

The woman looking at-whatever it was-was as slim and upright as Primula herself. Her feet and ankles, sufficiently displayed beneath her skirt, were exquisite, and her silk stockings and dainty shoes were worthy of them. Her curly bobbed hair, paler than

Primula's, stood out childishly from a perfectly shaped small head. She wore an unusual caped garment of a soft white woollen material, embroidered in primrose yellow. Even Sholto-Scott perceived that that gown had been expressly designed for her in Paris.

"Found a blue daffodil?" called out Primula, and at the sound they turned round. The elderly gentleman straightened himself up, and the lady waved her hand. 

Sholto-Scott could now see her lovely face; she looked about thirty.

But when he was shaking hands with her, and being introduced to Lady Mary Staniforth, he could see that she was at least thirty years older than that. The fine soft skin which covered the beautiful bone structure of her face, a little too loosely now, was scored with a network of tiny lines. Her hair was silver, no longer silvergold. The large blue eyes were rather weary, though they could still dart an intimate or an imperious glance. In spite of her sweet and caressing manner, there was something slightly intimidating about Lady 

Mary. She had the beauty of an iceberg.

Beside her, her husband, the great financier, faded into insignificance. Sholto-Scott could only remember afterwards that he looked rather like a horse-a thoroughbred with an irritable temper, kept well under control. "Dear child!" said Lady Mary, kissing Primula, and patting her cheek. "You look like the spring. And you need not tell me that this is Captain Sholto-Scott." 

She smiled upon the young man. "We were utterly thrilled about the Pinkerton Trophy. We sat up all night to listen to the broadcast. You are quite one of our national heroes now."

• · "Very fine piece of work, sir," said Sir Everard heartily. "I see you used a Mandel-Henderson engine.

I should like to know your opinion of the future of those engines.

No, pussy, it was not a blue daffodil. . . . Well, well, well-" And he allowed Primula to drag him off, murmuring but not ill-pleased, to the flower-bed he had just been inspecting. Primula wanted Grandy to get to know Captain Sholto-Scottshe didn't quite know why. Grandpa didn't matter so much. “Oh, there's a lot of luck about these things," said

Sholto-Scott to Lady Mary with the proper modesty. "Nobody knows how much, unless they've been in them." “And a lot of skill and daring and endurance," said she graciously. "I think we all know something about that."

They took a turn up and down the terrace, Sholto- Scott reflecting with amused wonder that he was actually tête-a-tête with the heroine of a very famous scandal of the days before the war. He felt he could understand it. She was still the enchantress. Her powers of seduction had scarcely waned. She could still make a man feel that he had something of Sir Lancelot in him. But as Primula's grandmother—!

He discovered afterwards on thinking it over that Lady Mary had in those few minutes contrived to find out a good deal about him without asking a single in-

quisitive question, though perhaps her questions might have seemed inquisitive from a less fascinating woman.

She was pleased to find that he was one of the Sholto- Scotts of Cluden, and he resisted the temptation to tell her that he was, equally, one of the Browns of Streatham. She was greatly concerned about his arm, and gave him the address of another specialist and another masseur. 

Lady Mary, on her part, enjoyed the little interlude. She was a connoisseur of men, with a weakness for good looks in a man, and particularly for the beauty of fine build. She found it difficult to be severe upon any man who was strong and lean and graceful. She liked the bold outline of Sholto-Scott's nose and chin-she hated men without noses!-and as she divined him capable of audacity, she liked the quietness of his manner. For a final touch of charm, she was not unaware of the effect that she herself was producing on him. 

So that for the moment she quite forgot the conversation she had had with Sir Everard half an hour before. "Who's this feller, Molly, that Prim's bringing down?" asked Sir Everard, with the suspicion he always showed towards male friends of Primula's. "Captain Sholto-Scott, dear, the famous airman.” "I know, I know, but-what's he after?" "How can I tell, dear? I've never seen him."

"Because if he's after Prim, I'm not going to have any damned fortune-hunter___" "Listen to me, Everard," said Lady Mary, speaking seriously. "Whatever he's like, we must treat him as if he were the one man in all the world we should have chosen. I don't for a moment suppose that there's anything in it-after all, Prim's mad about flying, and the man's a lion-but with these modern girls, one has to go carefully. They're perfectly easy to manage, though, if one knows how-they're so perverse! So whoever Prim brings along, you must be charming to him and leave the rest to me. Remember, Prim has got

Adelaide's money now, as well as Roderick's, and can do whatever she likes." 

Sir Everard muttered something about Adelaide and Roderick. 99 "I thoroughly agree with you," said Lady Mary calmly. "But don't forget what I say. "All right, Molly, I suppose you know best. You're a damn clever woman."

There were other guests at lunch; a Colonel Marsden, who had made a flight to India, and young Sir 

Thomas Singleton with his pretty wife, the Staniforths' next-door neighbours. Sholto-Scott found them a very congenial set. Being all of them airminded, they understood what he was saying, and they hung on his words with flattering attention. Even 

Lady Mary had been up several times, and talked

about learning to fly. Only Primula sat a little silent; this wasn't quite what she had expected-all these people. Still, there would be the drive back. 

And Lady Mary watched her quietly.

All through the talk, lively and absorbing as it was, Sholto-Scott was aware of its background; the panelled Adam dining-room, with its dominating Gainsborough; the white tulips and ruby glass on the table; the exquisitely cooked food; and the deft footmen, moving noiselessly to and fro behind their chairs. 

Everything he looked at pleased his eye. Wealth was agreeable, there was no doubt about it! 

After lunch, the whole gang-for so they now appeared to the discontented eye of Primula—went out to the West Meadow, where stood the aeroplane sheds, to examine and exclaim over the new Dragonfly.

Sholto-Scott looked at it with a loving gaze. He would have liked to try the beautiful thing, but his arm put a stop to that scheme. Besides, Primula had suddenly remembered a dinner engagement, and they would have to start back quite soon.

Primula had had a short encounter with Sir Toby before lunch. "Hullo, Prim!" said he. "Seen anything of Poppy lately?" "Yes," said Primula innocently. "I saw her with 

Freddie Iffley at Carson's on Saturday."

Sir Toby had glared, and Jill Singleton had laughed. "Why was Toby so-dignified?" asked Lady Mary, when Primula was putting on her green leather coat.

She watched the radiant creature in the mirror, and had a fancy that she was looking again at the reflec tion of Lady Molly Fitzhubert. 

"Oh!” said Primula. “It was something I said about

Poppy. Grandy, why won't they let those two be engaged? They're perfectly crazy about each other." "The Iffleys have not always been very-steady," Lady Mary suggested gently. "But it isn't fair to punish Freddie for his grandfather," cried Primula. “Grandy, don't you thinklove matters ?" "Of course, it does," replied Lady Mary, to whom it had always mattered supremely; only she had never looked for it in marriage. "Tell Poppy to be patient," she said mysteriously. "Lady Singleton will come round."

She had no grounds whatever for this opinion, but it produced the desired effect. "Oh, Grandy, why are people of that generation so stuffy?" cried Primula. "I mean Elfrida's. You are so different, so broad-minded. You can understand." 

She threw her arms round her grandmother. "Dear child!" cooed Lady Mary, who in Elfrida's

place would undoubtedly have kept Poppy on bread and water for a year rather than allow her to marry 

Freddie Iffley. 

All the same, she reflected, Elfrida was a fool!

LADY MARY sat up late that night by the fire in her bedroom. Her maid had undressed her, massaged her, brushed her silver hair for half an hour, and arrayed her in a négligée of shell-pink chiffon and fur. No elderly woollies for Lady Mary! And she looked more beautiful in her shell-pink draperies than most young girls would have done. 

On the table beside her lay The Mysterious Universe and The Testament of Beauty; the book in her hand was Les Maris de Paulette. But her thoughts wandered, although she was approaching page 51, the famous page 51, which even the most blush-proof admitted to be really rather too!

She found herself staring into the fire and seeing pictures there, a sentimental diversion to which she was little inclined as a rule. She saw her first lover, Henry Lomax, poor Henry, whom she had forgotten for forty years. She had been fifteen then, like Juliet, but unlike Juliet, she had not hesitated to climb down

from the balcony to meet her Romeo-by the croquet lawn. Lady Mary smiled to herself sometimes when she heard the young things talking. It tickled her to think that nearly fifty years ago, Molly Fitzhubert had been as "modern" as any of them. · • 

What made her think of Henry? Captain

Sholto-Scott. Yes that was it! They had the same facial outline, only poor Henry, of course, was hand- 

But perhaps with less character? . . . 

Still, he could make love. . . . But so, she felt sure, could Captain Sholto-Scott. . . . somer. Lady Mary now allowed Paulette and her Husbands to slip to the floor while she gathered up her impressions of the day. Primula was a little bit-taken, she could see. There was that not-very-clever fib about the dinner engagement-of course what she wanted was to have him to herself. And there was that little hesitation and-softness when she said the word "love"; a topic on which Lady Mary had several times heard her discoursing with fluency and decision.

But then, fifty thousand other girls were equally smitten; it was like the measles. No doubt, many of them had sent love-letters to the famous airman, girls in shops and offices and little suburban houses, girls who had not Prim's opportunities of getting to know almost anyone who made a stir in the world. 

As for Sholto-Scott himself, he was not yet a lover; so judged the woman of experience. Perhaps he never

would be. At any rate, there was no immediate danger, for he was not a fortune-hunter to take advantage of a young girl's momentary fancy. All she had to do was to be patient, and sympathetic, and never openly to oppose.

For though she thought Sholto-Scott would make a charming husband, she did not intend him to have 

Primula. She had a curious twofold affection for her granddaughter; Prim was all that remained of her darling eldest boy, and Prim was in some way a reincarnation of her own brilliant youth. Not so brilliant, and not so wild; for Prim was, regrettably, the child of Vivien as well as Ralph. Mixed with the Fitzhubert and the Staniforth elements in her was a strong dash of caution, prudence, almost prudery, which Lady Mary put down entirely to the middle-class Vivien. 

Vivien. . . . Lady Mary's face hardened. For her,

Vivien was the designing minx who had taken Ralph away from his mother, and ruined his career. In medieval times, Lady Mary would have suspected her of employing a love-philtre, so low did she rate her natural attractions. A little-pale-insignificantwisp of a girl, who had got round poor susceptible 

Ralph with her dark eyes and her quick tongue.

Of course, she was well-educated, and in a sense, clever; but with the sort of blue-stocking cleverness which doesn't matter, which is all very well for teachers and people like that, but which men don't care for!

She had no adaptability, no savoir-faire. She wasnobody.

When they had at last forgiven Ralph (but not 

Vivien), Sir Everard had made him his agent, and he had come with his wife to live at Havisham Hall. Lady 

Mary could recall picture after picture of those uncomfortable years; Vivien sitting almost silent through long dinner-parties-Lady Mary often felt bored herself at "county" merry-makings, but she resented Vivien's boredom; Vivien telling old Sir John 

Hunter-Fox that hunting was a relic of barbarism; 

Vivien going for long walks by herself, and leaving Damaged Goods about the drawing-room; and finally, Vivien blundering in when she was . . . saying goodbye to Roddy Sloane. Lady Mary never forgave her daughter-in-law for the look of involuntary repulsion, like a horrified child's, which showed on her face for an instant before she hastily withdrew. Little-priggish-middle-class-prude!

Then there was the fight over the naming of Mary Primula. Of course, that had happened when they were in cheap lodgings, unforgiven, but Lady Mary had imperceptibly drawn the story out of her son; how 

Ralph had insisted on "Mary," and Vivien had objected; how she had been obliged to give in, but had insisted in her turn on tacking on the absurd "Primula”—Primula, after a pot of primulas a kind landlady had brought her when she lay in bed! And after

all, "Primula" had stuck, and ousted "Molly." Her young friends seemed to think it a joke to call her "Prim."

There were other fights over Primula, after Ralph had been killed in France. They held divergent views on education. Vivien had insisted on sending the child to a good High School, where she would learn Latin and geometry and a little science; but Lady Mary had concerned herself with her dancing, and sent her to Paris for a year, to be finished. 

Yes.... Lady Mary sighed. . . . Vivien was a thorn in the flesh. But Primula was a rose! 

Prince Dmitri? Lady Mary had good reason to know that the match-with the heiress—would be smiled upon in high quarters. Prim a Serene Highness! It was very, very tempting. 

On the other hand, she had her moments of family feeling when she considered the claims of her own great-nephew, the young Marquis of Lydney. George was a nice boy, though too much given to Boys' Clubs when Night Clubs would have been more natural in his position. If he married Primula, the money would go back into her own family. This also was very, very tempting.

Well-she must keep a judicious finger ready to put into any pie that—that might happen to turn up! 

This settled, Lady Mary picked up Paulette, and read page 51 three times before she went to bed.

PRIMULA was a little disappointed with the run back. 

Sholto-Scott, who had evidently enjoyed the day immensely, praised Havisham in a way that gave her a faint thrill, for Havisham and its Adam ceilings and mantelpieces, its Gainsboroughs and Romneys, and its beech woods, would be hers some day. He had a good deal to say about the Dragonfly, too, and she liked having her possessions admired. It was so wonderful to be Prim Staniforth, with everything in the world! 

Then he told some funny stories about his American experiences; he had had so many letters after the race that he had to engage a secretary to deal with them; someone had carried off the banana skin on which he had slipped, as a memento. "Did you get any love letters?" asked Primula, with her usual directness. "Er-yes," said Sholto-Scott. "Anything like the 

Pinkerton Trophy Race always seems to bring out a lot of latent lunacy. But of course I didn't read them. 

The secretary did that." "Poor girls!" murmured Primula.

Sholto-Scott laughed. "Not at all. They'd have preferred the secretary if they'd only known! He was a very nice young fellow. . . . Er-talking of water-

melons, did I ever tell you about the old darkie in Honolulu-_99 Primula had to laugh at the old darkie, but privately she felt that water-melons opened up a less fascinating field for discussion than love letters. Sholto- 

Scott was further away from her than he had been, coming. She wondered why. Could it be that this visit to Havisham had brought it home to him that she was the Staniforth heiress? She knew that the really nice men were not too keen on-being attentive to heiresses. 

Primula felt vaguely worried.

When they were approaching London in the gathering dusk, Sholto-Scott gave her more food for thought. "Er do you know if Miss Forey will be at home next Sunday?" he asked. "You said she was your aunt, didn't you? I'd like to call on her." "Yes," said Primula briefly. "Rosalind is my mother's sister, and she lives with us. I expect she will be in." "She reminds me very much of a-friend of my youth," he said. "Friend” was hardly the word, but he could not very well say "charmer" or "ideal"! "Oh!" said Primula.

She wished now she hadn't told him so definitely that she was spending the week-end with Pamela Pamphlett. Otherwise, she could easily have put 

Pamela off. She would have liked very much to be in on Sunday. Still-poor Rosalind!—he would get a bit

of a shock when he saw her without her Pierrette's frock and make-up! 

Primula felt honestly sorry for Rosalind, and her slight jealousy passed away. "Hullo!" said Vivien, as Rosalind came bursting into the small drawing-room on Sunday afternoon. “I didn't expect you till to-morrow." "Cecil was motoring back to-day, and he offered me a lift," replied Rosalind. "So I took it. It was heavenly coming along. Heaps of primroses."

She threw her rough blue motoring coat down on a divan, and came to the fireplace, where Vivien had been reading in one of the squat, toad-like easy-chairs which Lambert Jacks had designed on purpose for this room. "You're Flip, to-day," said Vivien, looking at her admiringly. Rosalind had brought in a breath of country freshness. She was wearing a plain coat and skirt of navy blue, with a bunch of primroses tucked into the buttonhole. The wind had whipped a colour into her cheeks, and when she took off her hat, a lock of ruffled fair hair fell across her brow. She tossed it back with her characteristically impetuous gesture, and smiled rather teasingly down at Vivien. "It's the blue serge," she declared. "I spent half my life in blue serge when I was young, and nobody wears it now except schoolgirls. Besides-I'm re-

covering. I shall soon think thirty-seven the perfect age."

Vivien felt vaguely dissatisfied with this, but decided to change the subject. "How are the Jolliffes ?" "Oh-the same as ever. Norah's rather worried, though, about Noël. He's eighteen. Would you think it possible that Norah could have a son of eighteen?” "What's the matter with him?" "Oh-just rebellion! He's a born rebel-rebels against every mortal thing: law and order, society, religion, and morals. He's perfectly furious with his parents for being married—he says it was merely cringing hypocrisy on their part. And lately he's taken to rebelling against having meals at fixed times -he says it's a bourgeois convention. And it's no use 

Norah telling him that legs of mutton are essentially conventional and will take their own time to cook. He just goes on rebelling. It's very wearing, and Norah doesn't know what to do." "She must wait for the day when he rebels against rebellion," said Vivien laughing. "It will surely come. Rosalind!" Her change of tone was so mysteriously significant that Rosalind stared at her sister. Vivi sat there in her usual easy-chair, in her usual nice, not-smart afternoon frock-the collar, as usual, fastened a little

askew-but her eyes were shining like a child's and she was full of suppressed excitement. "What's the matter?" asked Rosalind, catching some of the excitement. 

For answer, Vivien pulled a paper from the front of her dress, and held it out to Rosalind. 

Five lines of typing under an imposing heading. 

The Editor of Pallas Athene declaring that he would be glad to use Miss Vivien Staniforth's story Betrayal, and offering fifteen pounds for it. And would she let him know if she agreed to this? Below came a signature with a well-known fierce crossing of "t's." "Vivi!" gasped Rosalind. "Do you mean to say that 

Augustus Pettitt has taken one of your things?" 

Vivien nodded. Years had dropped from her; she looked about twenty, with her shining eyes. "I don't know how I dared to send it," she said. "It's one of the things I wrote last year when I broke my leg, but I've been re-writing it the last few weeks, after going to bed. I can't write in the daytime-too many interruptions." "But-but it's a shame," cried Rosalind. "You must get a housekeeper." 

Vivien slowly shook her head. "No, Rosalind. That's my job. That's how I earn my board and keep. It's the only thing I can do for Primula. Mothers lose their jobs early nowadays. But I can, and I do, run this

house well. You see, I said to myself that if I had enough brains to tackle Provençal and Old High German I had enough to run a house. There isn't a better run house than this in London. I've got down to the job. And now I know the one queer little shop down by the docks where you get the very best caviare in London; and what to say to the kitchen-maid when the cook has gone temperamental and thrown a saucepan at her, and—oh, a hundred things like that. But I daresay after this I'll feel encouraged to try one or two more stories." "I should hope so," said Rosalind. She looked a little depressed. "I had a letter too, Vivi. From Dr. Zellich, the American collector, whom I met in Berlin. He wants me to be his secretary. Six hundred a year and live in Philadelphia." “Oh, Rosalind!" said Vivien, looking at her in dismay. "You wouldn't think of taking it?" "I-don't-know," replied Rosalind, thoughtfully. "Perhaps I ought to. It's rather a tempting offer. I'm not likely to get another like it. You see, it's in my line. 

I do know a little about antiques, thanks to Sir Benjamin, and especially porcelains. Dr. Zellich is very well-known, and he's rather a nice old boy, too." "Flip, darling," said Vivien earnestly. "Don't go to America. I'd hate to lose you, but I hate still more for you to shut yourself up in a museum with a lot of antiques and antiquarians. Why-"

rry She broke off with a start. The blue and green door was thrown open, and "Captain Sholto-Scott," announced Robinson, the parlour-maid.

"BOTHERATION take him!" was Vivien's first thought.

She had forgotten all about callers, and could not remember who he was. But Rosalind remembered. The 

Pinkerton airman ! So he had come after all! And Prim was away!

She smiled at him as he advanced into the room. "How nice of you to come and see us!" she said, holding out her hand. "We feel terribly honoured." "B-but-why should you?" stammered Sholto 

Scott rather stupidly, feeling momentarily bewildered as he met the bright, unself-conscious gaze of her periwinkle-blue eyes. Again, by some queer juggling with time and space, he was simultaneously in the Jolliffes' garden twenty years ago, and in this London drawing-room to-day. For he had no doubts now. This was Flip herself, a little graver, older, of course, with her pigtail somehow disposed of, and wearing the same sort of navy blue clothes. There was the same air of faint surprise on her delicate eyebrows. Only Flip had never really looked at him—in the old days.

A wave of the old boyish enchantment swept over him, and again in looking at her he saw Beauty; the princesses in the fairy-tales, and Juliet and Helen and Cleopatra. His heart beat faster. "Oh, because you're a celebrated man," Rosalind was saying when he recovered himself. "And we're just -ordinary people. Vivien, let me introduce Captain 

Sholto-Scott. This is my sister, Mrs. Staniforth." 

He shook hands with the small, dark-eyed lady in brown, who did not look like the mother of a Golden Girl, and was soon sitting in one of the toad arm-chairs and answering her inquiries about his arm. Flip, excusing herself for a moment, had picked up her motor coat and disappeared. 

Vivien, who did not like celebrities, liked this one. 

She had had her eyes on him during the minute before the introduction, and she liked the little air of shyness which sat so oddly on what she called to herself his "adventurer's face." Then, all of a sudden, she had grown intent. Why was he looking at Rosalind like that? Like one who sees a vision? By her quick sympathy she felt that this was for him a moment of special emotion. Later, she remembered it again; but it passed, and she found herself chatting with a young man, so cool and adequate to the occasion that she almost thought she must have imagined that look of eager wonder and longing. "It's not Prim," she said to herself. She knew by

experience the first furtive exploring glance of those who sought Primula, and their resigned and barren conversation when disappointed. 

Tea was brought in, a rather homely refreshment, with hot scones, sandwiches, and Scotch shortbread, which was not unlike schoolroom tea, and seemed incongruous eaten in this queer, sophisticated room, where nothing looked familiar but the wood fire. 

Sholto-Scott liked it, it took him back to Inverdurie, and when Rosalind came back without her coat, and settled herself on a stool near him, balancing an eggshell cup in her delicate fingers, he felt extraordinarily contented. He wondered why more women did not wear white silk shirts a little open at the throat. He hoped that no one else would come for a very long time. "You promised to unravel the Mystery of the Pink

Cup," he reminded her after a while. "Oh!" said Rosalind. "So I did. At Prim's party. Well-listen." 

She put down her cup, brushed back her unruly lock, and fixed her blue eyes on his face. "Once upon a time," she began, "an Emperor of 

China, long ago—I don't know how long exactly, but he was a Ming Emperor-had a wife whom he adored. 

He ordered the Imperial porcelain factory to make her a special set of tea-cups. There were to be six cups and saucers, as thin as paper, as clear as ice, as resonant as a jade bell, and coloured like prunus flowers. On each

cup there were to be two medallions containing paintings of landscapes near the Empress's home in the South, so that whenever she drank tea she would be reminded of her own country. Well-everything was all right until the Emperor cast his eyes upon a beautiful lady called 'Clear Sky.' And after that, the Empress would cry her eyes out-he spent all his time with 'Clear Sky.' He loaded her with silks, furs, jade, and embroideries, and still she was not satisfied. She must have one thing more. She must have the Empress's tea-set, because she too came from the South! 

The Emperor offered her another set, but in vain! The artist who had painted the medallions was dead, and she would have no other. "So the Son of Heaven was in a terrible stew. He didn't quite venture to take the cups away from the 

Empress. So he compromised. He had one of them stolen, and gave it to 'Clear Sky,' who smiled upon him again, rejoicing in her triumph over the Empress. "But it didn't last long. Soon afterwards, she was strangled in her bed by the servants of the Empress, and her house was ransacked for the little pink cup.

But it wasn't found. It was never brought to light again till I discovered it in Berlin last month. 

While you were winning the Pinkerton Trophy. 

Funny what different things people do, isn't it?” she said, with a teasing glance.

"By Jove, yes," said Sholto-Scott. "And what happened to the other cups?" "They're the gem of the Cuffe Collection," she said. "Sir Benjamin Cuffe bought them from a Japanese collector for a simply terrific sum." "But how do you know that they're the same ones?" “Oh, well—that is a legend. But, oddly enough, they do correspond to it. You see, they were illustrated and described in a catalogue made by a Chinese connoisseur in the seventeenth century. We always wondered what had happened to the other-whether 'Clear Sky' had had a careless housemaid, or what. And thenquite by accident-I got hold of a typed catalogue of a quite small private collection in a German Castle, and from the description of one of the cups, I felt sure it was 'Clear Sky's.' And Lady Cuffe authorised me to try to buy it. So I flew over-yes, in an aeroplane-and that was lucky, because Dr. Zellich, the American collector, had also got on to the trail somehow. I beat him by three hours. I found the old Count in Berlin-he had moved his pots there and I talked, and begged and coaxed, and at last, because he was an artist, and liked to see the set complete again, and because it was going to the Victoria and Albert Museum, he consented to sell-for a very good price, of course. He was a delightful old man. So when Dr. Zellich arrived, I had the cup in my handbag—perhaps at the very same

moment that you were passing the winning post," she said, laughing. "We both know what triumph is.” "What amazes me," he said, "is your courage." "How? Do you mean-in daring to tackle the old Count?" "No. In daring to carry that cup about with you in a bag. I should have dropped it twenty times out of sheer funk." "Oh, of course, it was in a special padded case. But, anyway, I've handled so much priceless china that my fingers have grown-sort of adhesive. You see, I was 

Sir Benjamin's secretary for eleven years.” 99 She went on telling about the Cuffe Collection, and he listened more to the sound of her voice than to her words. 99 "She needn't have mentioned those eleven years,' reflected Vivien, watching them. "She doesn't look a minute more than twenty-eight when she's interested.

Not quite to Sholto-Scott's satisfaction. He could have gone on listening to Flip for a long time yet, and he hadn't looked at her half enough! He almost had a superstitious fear that she might vanish like a sprite if he took his eyes off her.

But he did his best to be agreeable to Mrs. Staniforth's young friend, and presently, when other callers arrived, he performed the same duty by them. He could not help perceiving that he was now in a world where air prowess counted for far less than with his usual associates. Well-probably that was wholesome for him.

He got no more of Flip's attention until he was saying good-bye, and then she turned her lovely eyes upon him in careless friendliness. "Good-bye," she said, shaking hands. For the first and last time, he noticed the half-dozen silver hairs which now threaded Flip's unruly front lock, and the faint lines which crossed her white brow. It's a greater miracle than-flying," she ended, with one of the swift and merry glances he was beginning to look out for. "Oh-flying is not a miracle," returned Sholto Scott. "We don't want it to be. We want everyone to take to it quite naturally." "I think I'd love it," she said. "I'm not like Vivien, who declares she's bus-minded." "I'll teach you one day," he said, wondering at himself as he spoke. Never before had he volunteered to teach a woman to fly. "Oh!" cried Rosalind, looking at him with sparkling eyes. "That would be like having Kreisler to teach one the violin. But I wouldn't venture to take up your time."

"Why not?" she said, laughing. “As a matter of fact, it is to earn my living. I've had a very good offer of a post as secretary to Dr. Zellich, the art expert, and I don't think I ought to refuse it." "Oh—I see!" said he grudgingly. There was nothing more to say. Of course she had a right to go to any place in the world if she wanted to, and it was simply not his business to butt in. Dashed impertinence, in fact! All the same, it was extraordinary how he disliked the scheme; it took all the pleasure out of the day. 

Rosalind passed on to the next cabinet, and he followed her in a discontented silence. Strange young man, she thought. Probably some disagreeable experience in America had put him against the whole country.

But she forgot all about it as she unlocked the door, and showed him one lovely thing after another, telling him about the Sung celadon, and the K'ang Hsi period, pointing out the exquisite glaze on one piece and the brilliant painting on another. She showed him a tiny fragile cup of black, speckled with white, and mounted with a silver rim.

"This is a 'partridge cup,'" she explained. "Very precious-almost our star turn. It's very ancient." 

Sholto-Scott was struck by her obvious knowledge, technical as well as æsthetic. "How did you learn all this?" he asked abruptly. "It's quite out of the ordinary run of things that women know." "Sir Benjamin taught me. I didn't know Ming from 

Woolworth before I met him. . . . Or care," she added in a lower tone, the ghost of the old sorrow coming back for a moment to haunt her face. 

Sholto-Scott was silent. He felt he wanted tremendously to know what had happened to subdue the gaiety of Flip, and turn her life into this backwater. 

But he did not know what to say. He could only hope that she would understand, underneath speech, that he wanted to be a friend. "He was awfully good to me," went on Rosalind, as if she had read his thoughts. "We met during the 

War. I think I told you I was driving an ambulance?

Well, he was one of the Red Cross orderlies, such a quiet, precise, scholarly little man! Above the age, of course, but he had wangled it somehow. I didn't know who he was, but I liked him, and we became quite friendly. Then . . . one day. . . I had bad news... the sort that smashes your life to pieces. 

I had to work all day, but in the evening . . .

I couldn't bear

it any longer so I went out to where the ambulance was . . . and absolutely went off the deep end. 

He found me there, and talked to me he told me how his three sons had been killed in one week, and ... and I don't know what he said, but it sort of helped me out of the worst · • remembering it afterwards. So after the War was ended, he asked me to become his secretary, and I did. And that's the autobiography of Rosalind Philippa Forey." "Hard lines," said Sholto-Scott, with a tenderness which he had never felt before. Poor, gay Flip, going through her agony in the dark by the ambulance! “Oh, I mustn't grumble, I've had a very interesting time," said Rosalind. "Only-not quite what I'd expected. You see-" She raised her blue eyes to his. "I was going to be married in a fortnight. He was in the 

Navy, and we'd been engaged for a long time. I was so happy I sometimes thought I'd die of it. And then I heard that my dear-my dear-he was killed picking up a bomb. At the same moment, Sholto-Scott, under the hands of the masseur, was occupied with two questions: firstly, how do you prevent a lady whose affairs are no concern of yours from going to America: and secondly, when he was going to see Flip again.

ABOUT a fortnight afterwards, Primula decided at short notice to give a cocktail party. The guests were summoned by telephone and telegram, and as usual a large number found themselves able to accept. Sholto- 

Scott was not one of them, as he had an engagement with Mr. Murchison of Aerial Transit. Primula's pleasure was a little clouded by this, for she was beginning to divide parties into two kinds: those at which he was present, and the utter flops. 

Still, life was good! She had just returned from a three days' visit to Hadleigh Castle, where she had had two days out with the Haddon-glorious runs! Prince

Dmitri had been staying there too, and she knew that

his attentions to her had formed the chief thrill of that house-party. It was what the women speculated over in their bedrooms. Her Serene Highness

Now she stood in a Lanvin frock of deep blue chiffon, laughing at the little cynicisms of Fabian Faneso like salted almonds—and nibbling at a small and subtle sandwich of a kind she had never tasted before. 

It was delicious. Lobster? And olive? And what?

Really, Mother had a perfect genius for this sort of thing!

And over in a corner George was glowering in the most amusing way. He hadn't been invited; but that was nothing to George. Neither did he care for cocktails and intriguing little sandwiches; bread and cheese and beer were more to his taste. But he could not deny himself the torment of watching her with other men, and Primula gave him a full opportunity. Only once did he smile when Vivien came to speak to him. 

Other guests were more hilarious. Some of them were dancing in the big drawing-room, but Primula had had enough of dancing, and presently went across to join a group of special cronies who were gathered round the fire. Here, however, she came across her one crumpled rose-leaf, for they were eagerly discussing Deb Temple's newly published novel, Admit Impediment, which was attracting a good deal of notice at the moment.

Deb Temple was Deborah Temple-Templeton- Temple, daughter of Lord William Temple-Temple ton-Temple, and she had been a rival of Primula's at school. Afterwards, however, instead of plunging into the joys of society like Primula, she had gone to Somerville. Admit Impediment was apparently the result.

Privately, Primula thought as little of Admit Impediment as she did of its author, but there was no denying that the Dictator had said it possessed authentic genius, equal at least to that of Emily

Brontë; and when everybody was telling everybody else what a brilliant book it was, one was obliged to join in the chorus. "I think that's a heavenly bit," Lady Moira was saying, "about the sunset, when she's going away to 

Tunis with Clement-no, Michael. Clement was the

Capri one, and Basil the Brioni. I remember, because their names begin with the same letter as the places." "Couldn't make head or tail of it," cheerfully declared Floppy Fitzjohn. "But of course I know it's marvellous—she's made a thousand pounds already." "What I like,” said Cosmo Parsley, who sometimes did reviews, "is, she's had the courage to break away from Conventional Form. Most books begin with the beginning and end with the end, and in between there is a middle. Now Deb's thrown all that overboard." "It's rather muddling, though," put in Lady Moira,

"for Gerda to be dead at the beginning and alive at the end, and in between she's sometimes one and sometimes the other. Of course I admire it enormously,' she made haste to add. “Only it is puzzling." 99 “Oh—you've got a Succession Complex,” said Arthur Peacock. "You probably think that Monday comes after Sunday, and seven after six." "Well-doesn't it?" "My dear girl, why should it? Now Deb sees that.” "Would you call her a Patternist ?" "N-no, I shouldn't say she was a Patternist, exactly-" “Then what do you make of the Gipsy with the 

Green Parrot, who comes in every ten pages?" "Oh, she symbolises rhythm, of course." "I thought that was quite marvellous," said Lady 

Moira. "But I couldn't quite understand how the

Gipsy with the Green Parrot could get into the operating theatre. I mean, would the hospital people allow her to?" 

The others looked at her in silent pity. "She's a Kaleidoscopist," announced Oliver Lane suddenly.

Primula sat there, smiling and looking lovely, but bitterly envious within of Deb Temple, who was no cleverer than herself, but who was making everybody talk about her rubbishy book.

This was a new sensation for her, Prim Staniforth,

who had everything in the world, and had done everything worth doing except this!

Primula reflected, firstly, that George had really done very well at Cambridge; and secondly, that Deb 

Temple-Templeton-Temple was not the only girl in the world who possessed a fountain pen.
