VIVIEN went slowly up the vermilion staircase and along the jade-green corridor to her eighteenth-cen-

tury room, which now, more than ever, seemed to her a refuge from the modernity which swept like an unquiet wave through the rest of the house. 

She felt tired and middle-aged. Certain phrases were saying themselves over and over in her head: "a gem you mustn't miss," "a flower of middle-class morality."

On the table lay the bright-green cover of Pallas Athene, just come from the newsagent, but instead of pouncing on it with delight, she picked it up soberly, and turned the pages listlessly over. 

Yes, there was her story in print, looking so much more authentic than it had seemed in typescript. Like a real story, she thought. The people who read it, if any did, would never imagine that it was merely by Vivien Staniforth, an obscure nobody. The "Victor 

Stanley" on the cover looked like the name of a real author.

Putting aside these childish reflections, Vivien set herself to read her story through, as critically as she could. No, she could not find any "middle-class morality” in it, only the morality imposed on men by life and the stress of sin and suffering. For she had taken a story from life: the history of Will Woodhouse, one of the Havisham shepherds, and his wife Elsie, as she had almost lived it through with them in the years when she used to take long walks alone, and had made friends in farms and humble village homes. She had

put it down simply, using all her skill to make her reader see what she had herself seen in the lonely cottage on the downs. 

Still, she no longer wanted to show it to Prim. All the pleasure with which she had looked forward to giving Primula a surprise had vanished, because she felt now that Prim would see it with the eyes of her friends, however sweet she might be to her dear little, queer little mother.

Vivien pulled herself together, and went resolutely over to the bureau, where she knew that a pile of account books had been lying in wait for two days. After checking the baker's and the greengrocer's lists, she found that her mortification had somewhat subsided. 

After all, who was Cosmo Parsley? A boy of twentythree, who had made a success of impudence with his Cosmo Parsley: A Study, published at twenty. 

Whereas Augustus Pettitt was the most famous of living editors.

The housemaid Rose came into the room at this moment, bringing Mrs. Staniforth's black lace evening gown which she had been pressing, and at the sight, Vivien's thoughts flew back to where they had been dwelling all day, until diverted by Pallas Athene. Rosalind.

Last night, she and Rosalind had been out to dinner at a very smart restaurant, with Captain Sholto-Scott. 

She had been secretly rather scared when she saw the

name of Ruffo's in the invitation, for that was where royalties and film-stars supped, and she hadn't been to dine at a smart restaurant since the days of Ralph. 

Secretly too she felt some misgivings about Sholto- Scott, and especially about his friend, who would be the fourth member of the party. She half suspected them of belonging to the class of whom she had met so many among the adorers of Lady Mary; the experienced, the arrogant, the successful, the sophisticated.

Rosalind had no such qualms. Indeed, she didn't take the matter seriously enough, refusing to buy a new frock. Her flowered chiffon would do, she said; she had only worn it three times. Vivien was vexed. For some reason, not clear to herself, she did want Flip to look young and lovely at that dinner.

When Sholto-Scott had come forward to meet them in the lounge at Ruffo's, her misgivings disappeared. 

He was so eager, and so pleased to see them; and the friend was a pleasant American, of a good dining-out age, no supercilious man-of-the-world. 

She was amused when Sholto-Scott explained with unnecessary care that he had been out of London for ten days, called to Sidmouth by the illness of his mother. Otherwise, he said, he wouldn't have waited so long- 

So long! Why, she had only known him a fortnight, and Rosalind not much longer. She could see Flip's expressive eyebrows lifting ever so slightly.

The talk round the daffodil-decked table was gay and general, but afterwards, in a box at the Connaught Theatre, Vivien slipped into a tête-à-tête with 

Mr. Jackson Robert. Through his entertaining drawl, however, she was aware that Captain Sholto-Scott was talking very earnestly to Rosalind, who was listening with grave attention.

Then, at the end of the third act, had come the moment which had set Vivien to wondering. Lights were low, the whole house was hanging on the words of the leading lady, when she happened to look round. In the half-darkness she saw Rosalind's clear profile intent upon the stage, and the face of Sholto-Scott, intent upon Rosalind. 

She turned her head quickly back, hating to see what was not intended for her. Sensations from the past rushed over her in a flood: Ralph looking at her like that in the church at Eastgate on Christmas Day -she had pretended to drop her hymn-book, in order to hide the outrageous happiness she knew to be showing in her face. . . .

Was Captain Sholto-Scott falling in love with Rosalind? Ought she to drop her a hint? But, no. Flip would find out soon enough by herself, and a love affair would do her all the good in the world. 

Better not interfere!

"Have you seen this week's Pallas Athene?" asked

Rosalind of Primula, when she came upon that young person lying flat on her back on Vivien's divan. Prim was going to three dances that night with her aunt, the 

Countess of Paddington, and she was resting. 66 'Augustus on Deb Temple? Rather!" said Primula, stretching herself luxuriously. "Wouldn't have missed it for worlds." "I didn't mean that. I meant your mother's story." "Mother?" almost shrieked Primula, sitting up with a start. "Has she been and gone and perpetrated a story? What's it called?"

Her face was alight with amusement. "Betrayal,” replied Rosalind, feeling a strong desire to smack the pretty creature before her. "Betrayal? But how sweet!" She stopped abruptly, a sudden compunction seizing her. That was the name 

Cosmo had mentioned. What was it he had said exactly? Had Mother been there at the time? No-Primula was almost certain she had gone away before he had made his devastating remarks. "I did read itsome of it," she went on, reassured. "It's awfully well written." "Augustus Pettitt seemed to think so, too," said 

Rosalind drily. "Oh, I know he's an awful swell," said Primula hastily. "And immensely highbrow. Knew Ruskin, and

all that, didn't he? I must congratulate her. Justfancy-Mother!"

She flopped down on her back again, an amused little smile playing round her charming mouth. A moment passed, in which Rosalind contemplated, halfadmiring, half-exasperated, the nymphlike form of her niece in her rose chiffon négligée. "Have a good time last night?" asked Primula casually, then. "Yes, thank you. We went to see The Lady of

Dreams." 

Primula's hand crept to her breast, where, between white skin and pink georgette, there lay a folded sheet of paper. It was the first time in her life she had ever felt inclined to be "sloppy," and she was glorying in it.

All the note contained was an invitation to dine and dance. She knew that he had invited two or three others as well, but she didn't mind that—for the moment. It was all a part of his absurd darling punctiliousness.

He wasn't going to run after the heiress, and she loved him for it. When the time came, she would find a way. 

She loved, too, the old-fashioned feeling which had made him ask Mother and Rosalind to dine with him last night; he wanted them to know him, to see what kind of man he was. Young men nowadays didn't bother much about that sort of thing; if they saw their

people-in-law at the wedding, it was as much as you could expect! She thought the old-fashioned way was rather sweet. 

And all this in a man who was a perfect dare-devil!

It was terribly chic! 

Rosalind, having found the book she was looking for, went away, and Primula lay still, feeling very happy, lazy, and warm. She made up her mind that she would be very, very sweet to Mother about that unlucky Betrayal, and she would give Rosalind a diamond wrist-watch for her birthday. 

Poor darlings! It must be terrible to be middleaged. "YOU'RE wanted on the 'phone, ma'am," said Robinson, appearing at the door of Vivien's sitting-room the next Monday evening. "Lady Singleton." "Very well, Robinson, I'll come,” said Vivien, absently, dragging her thoughts reluctantly away from the beginning of a new story. As she went downstairs -for she refused to have a telephone in her eighteenth-century room-she wondered with a sigh what Elfrida, Lady Singleton wanted now; and she had a vague foreboding of worry and fuss. All contacts with Elfrida were irritating; she always bothered you

to do something, or buy something, or go somewhere you didn't want to. Would it be a bridge party this time, or what?

She took up the receiver. "That you, Mrs. Staniforth?" came the decided voice of Lady Singleton. "Yes? Well-would you be so very kind as to send Poppy home at once-immediately. We've had bad news, I'm sorry to say. 

Her grandmother, old Lady Singleton, died suddenly this evening-we've just heard. So of course Poppy won't be able to go to the Hospital Ball-it wouldn't do at all! So fortunate we are able to stop her in time. So if you'll just send her home" 

Vivien's head whirled, and she stared with astonished eyes at the wall-paper opposite. "I'm very sorry," she replied, "but Poppy is not here! I think there must be some mistake" "Haven't they come back yet?" interrupted the imperious voice. "Then what about Primula? She ought to be dressing for the ball." "She is dressing for the ball, upstairs," replied Vivien, thoroughly bewildered, and scenting trouble yet more serious. "She's been resting all the afternoon. But I haven't seen anything of Poppy." "But I don't understand. Hasn't she been spending the week-end with you and Primula at your cottage in Sussex ?" 

Lady Singleton's voice was high and astonished.

Then she dropped it suddenly, giving Vivien the impression that she was-frightened. "She left here on 

Saturday afternoon," went on Elfrida in an urgent undertone. "She was to come back with you this afternoon, and go with Lady Paddington and Primula to the ball. . . . I didn't intend going-" "Wait a minute," said Vivien. "I'll go and ask

Primula if she knows anything about this.” 

She ran upstairs, feeling worried. What crazy escapade had that peculiarly foolish Poppy been up to now? She hoped, but hardly expected, that her own 

Prim was not concerned too deeply in anything very serious.

In her anxiety she burst into Primula's room without knocking. "O-o-oh!" shrieked Primula. "I'm not fit to be 

Oh, it's you, Mother. What ever is the seen. matter?"

At any other time Vivien would have delighted in the beauty of Primula in her little white silk sheath, but now she felt too much distracted. "Just leave us for a few minutes, Sophie, please," she said to the French maid, who stood waiting with an armful of green silk draperies. "I want to speak to Mademoiselle." "Primula," she said seriously, when they were alone, "Lady Singleton is on the 'phone. Do you know where Poppy is?"

Primula's grey eyes met her mother's with an enigmatic look; frank and innocent, but was there some covert amusement in it? "No, I don't, Mother," she replied without hesitation. "I wish I did! She ought to be here this minute dressing for the ball. Her things came this morning. 

They're in the Yellow Room." "Did you invite Poppy to spend the week-end at the cottage?" "No," said Primula. “At least, not particularly.

I gave her a general invitation to go down there any week-end she liked. . . . Why?"

Her daughter's wide-eyed innocence exasperated Vivien. She could not think it genuine, with that subdued twinkle lurking behind it all the time. "Because she told her mother that she was spending the week-end down there with you and me. She went away on Saturday, and hasn't come back. So

Lady Singleton's terribly anxious, naturally. I'll go and tell her, then, that you know nothing about it." "You can say I didn't ask Poppy to spend this week-end with me," said Primula, "and that I don't know where she is."

Vivien felt that there was prevarication somewhere about, but it was time to go back to the unfortunate Elfrida, hanging anxiously on to a telephone receiver, away in Portman Square. When she reached

the little telephone room, however, she found that 

Lady Singleton had rung off. "Ringing up someone else," she surmised. "She must be feeling desperate. No wonder, with a girl like Poppy. Oh, dear! what it is to have a daughter!"

She would have liked to settle down to her writing, but her sense of duty compelled her to go upstairs again to Primula's room. She must try to get to the bottom of this, to see if Primula could throw any more light upon the doings of the silly, the tiresome, the spankworthy Poppy.

She found Sophie fastening a slim, apple-green robe on Primula's white shoulder with a round gold clasp; for Primula was to be Hygeia, the Goddess of 

Health, at the Aches-and-Pains Ball. Vivien stood by, waiting till she should have finished, and watching the enchanting reflection in the glass, with pure motherly pleasure. It struck her that the child was a little nervous, a little uneasy. She fidgeted, and consulted her wrist-watch more than once. "I can't think what's keeping Poppy," she said at last, while Sophie was fastening her golden sandals. "She'll simply have to scramble into her things—or go without dinner. Aunt Violet's calling for us at halfpast eight." "Why didn't you tell me she was coming here to dress?" asked Vivien. "Forgot," said Primula. "She asked if she might,

last week, and sent her things along this morning-or somebody did." "Can you think of anyone else she could have spent the week-end with?" asked Vivien.

A spark of unholy amusement danced for a moment in Primula's eyes. "Darling, there are thousands," she said vaguely. "Poppy knows everybody."

Vivien was about to reply, when they heard the sound of a car stopping outside the house. The bell rang, a long, determined peal. "There she is!" cried Primula, dancing up and down with excitement. "She's taken a taxi!"

She ran out on to the landing, and was half-way down the staircase before her mother had reached the top. Vivien, following her, heard Robinson opening the front door, and then there came a voice, the voice which at Havisham dominated both mothers' meeting and dinner-party-that of Elfrida Lady Singleton. "Please ask Mrs. Staniforth if she will see me at once."

Primula stopped dead, half-way down the bottom flight, and turned as if to flee upstairs again. Vivien had the same impulse, but, reaching her daughter, she took Prim's arm and gently propelled her down into the hall. "We've got to clear this up, Prim," she whispered, "if we can."

In the hall, looking oddly out of place among the gay modern frescoes, stood Lady Singleton, a handsome figure of a woman in a coat of Persian lamb. "Oh-how do you do, Lady Singleton ?" said Vivien, for the benefit of Robinson. "Won't you come in here?"

She led the way into the small drawing-room. Lady 

Singleton refused to sit down, so they stood awkwardly on the rug in front of the fire. Vivien felt sincerely sorry for her colleague, though she could not help a momentary recollection of George's saying that she was like a good-looking parrot. 

She heard herself offering suggestions. "It's more than likely she's gone to stay with some other friends. Girls are so thoughtless-they don't know how we worry about them. And if she was coming back by car, there may have been a breakdown-" "I have come to know," said Lady Singleton, ignoring Vivien's efforts, "whether Primula can throw any light on this.”

She fixed a cold, unadmiring eye on Primula. 

Primula threw up her chin. Elfrida Lady Singleton was an ancient enemy, and Prim Staniforth was not afraid of anybody! "If you mean-did I ask Poppy down to the cottage this particular week-end, I didn't. But I did tell her

she could go down there any week-end she liked. She have gone this one I don't know." may "Alone?" 

Again that look of mysterious amusement flickered across Primula's face. 

"I don't know," she murmured. "There's a housekeeper there," put in Vivien. "A nice, kind woman. Shall we 'phone to the King's Head, and ask them to take a message over to Mrs. Borden?"

But Lady Singleton disregarded Vivien again, and addressed herself pointedly to Primula. "Will you kindly inform me since I have some slight interest in the matter-whether my daughter is secretly married to that-that—” "Not that I know of," returned Primula coolly. "I think they'd have told me if they were. And if you don't mind my saying so, Mother's suggestion is quite a good one. You'd get an answer from old Mother 

Borden in about half an hour. . . . Hullo, what's that?"

A TAXI had pulled up at the door and the bell was ringing furiously. The three on the hearthrug suspended

hostilities for a moment, and stood listening involuntarily. "Oh-Robinson-" came a breathless, laughing voice, with a note of hysteria in it. "We've had such a breakdown. Will you tell Miss Primula—”

Primula was quick, but on this occasion Lady Singleton was quicker. Her tall fur-clad figure was in the hall before Poppy could finish her sentence, and she was confronted with the Medusa-like countenance of her mother. "Oh-hell!" she exclaimed, and darted to the front door, which was still ajar. But she did not bolt, as 

Vivien half expected her to do. She spoke a few hurried words to someone waiting on the step outside, and slammed the door to, viciously. Then she turned to tackle her mother.

Lady Singleton took her daughter by the shoulder, and marched her into the first open door. Vivien and 

Primula, following them, found themselves in the dining-room. Vivien was not sure whether they were not intruders in their own house, but Primula had no such qualms. She was full of the joy of battle. She glanced across at Poppy, who gave her a brief nod, packed with meaning. "Who was that with you?" demanded Lady Singleton. "Freddie Iffley," returned Poppy coolly.

There was a battle of glances between mother and

daughter, the hard young face with its gipsy bloom staring defiantly into the hard middle-aged one. Vivien twisted her hands together, filled with helpless pity for them both. "Shall we?" she began. "Where have you come from?" 99 "From Sandbourne," said Poppy. "We ran out of petrol miles from anywhere, or we'd have been here ages ago. We had to walk four miles, and wait- "Where did you meet-Mr. Iffley?" "At Victoria, on Saturday."

Vivien, watching in a sympathetic agony, saw Lady Singleton flinch. She recovered herself, however, in an instant. "Come, Poppy, I think we'd better go home," she said. "There's no need to bore Mrs. Staniforth with these details, which only concern ourselves. We can get a taxi in the Square.' 99 "No, Mother," said Poppy inexorably. "Since you are here, we'll have it out now. Prim's my pal-she knows all about it. Besides, I'd like someone outside the family to know—" "What?" gasped Lady Singleton. "That Freddie and I went down to Prim's cottage on Saturday afternoon, and on Sunday we went to Sandbourne and stayed at the hotel there." "Together?" "Yes!"

There was a pause. "Are you married to him?" asked Lady Singleton then. Her voice had gone small and weak. Vivien felt inclined to burst into tears. "No," said Poppy. 

A stricken silence fell. "Oh, Poppy!" murmured 

Vivien. Primula went and stood beside the other girl, slipping her arm through Poppy's.

The two young things against the world, thought Vivien. But for once her sympathy went to Elfrida, who for the first time since Vivien had known her showed signs of being only a weak mortal. "I believe she really does love Poppy," she said to herself. "When are you going to be-?" Was this the voice of Lady Singleton, husky, quavering, and touched with fear? "I don't know," said Poppy nonchalantly. "And you've spent two nights together," burst out her mother. "Yes," said Poppy. "One at Prim's cottage and the other at the Beach Hotel at Sandbourne. We called ourselves Mr. and Mrs, Macgillicuddy, if you'd like to know. . . . It was immense fun." She gave a forced, hysterical laugh. "Only, you see, Freddie wouldn't care to marry a girl unless he had the consent of her people.

There was a tense moment, during which Lady 

Singleton almost visibly swallowed the bitterest pill of

her life. Vivien looked away, feeling miserable and ashamed, but Primula's young face was as inexorable as Poppy's. "Of course," said Lady Singleton at last, making an effort, "your brother and I—will be very glad—to welcome-the man of your choice." "But we can't marry on nothing," pursued Poppy, "and Freddie's only got five hundred a year." "There's the money your father left you," said her mother. "Well, then, in that case, I'm sure Freddie'll be only too pleased to make an honest woman of me." 

Lady Singleton winced at the phrase. Poppy went over and kissed her mother. "Cheer up, old thing.

You won't be losing a daughter, but gaining a son, as the Victorians used to say."

Lady Singleton was about as responsive to this embrace as the Sphinx would have been, but behind her stony countenance Vivien could divine an immense relief. 

Relief... at the prospect of Freddie Iffley, his debts and his infidelities. "We shall have to make arrangements at once," she said, her practical mind flying for the moment, as Vivien knew, to licences, registry offices, and the best methods of saving one's face. 

The little clock set in the wall chimed the hour. "Gracious!" cried Poppy. "It's eight o'clock. I

must simply hurl on my Scarlet Fever kit. Freddie's gone home to change. . . . . . . Are my things here, angel?" she asked of Primula. "In the Yellow Room, darling,” replied Primula.

Vivien cast a glance of genuine disgust at her daughter. "You can't go to the ball to-night," observed 

Lady Singleton grimly. "Your grandmother was so thoughtless as to die this evening." "Oh-dash!” cried Poppy. "I mean, I'm frightfully sorry and all that. . . . I didn't know she was ill, When did she die?” even. "We won't inflict any more of our private affairs on 

Mrs. Staniforth," said Lady Singleton firmly. “I'm only sorry she's been dragged into so much.. . . But, innocently or not, she seems to have played a considerable part in this—this transaction," she added, with uncontrollable bitterness. "Not to speak of Primula.” "Now, Mother, that's not fair," said Poppy, before the indignant Vivien could open her mouth. “Prim didn't know-well, anyway, she didn't know it was to be this week-end. And Mrs. Staniforth's rather more innocent than the babe unborn. I only used her name to stop you asking questions; I thought she wouldn't mind. But she didn't know anything about it.” "Then it was her business to know," snapped Lady

Singleton, with a rudeness which Vivien felt to be only too excusable. "Come, Poppy!"

She swept out, ignoring Primula and Vivien, and Poppy followed, making a queer little face behind her mother's back, which expressed triumph, amusement, and apology all at once. 999 "Wouldn't mind,' said Vivien disgustedly, the moment the door had closed behind them. "I'm not going to speak to you, Prim, about this to-night. I can't trust myself. Run along now and eat some dinner. Your Aunt Violet will be here in twenty minutes. 

I shall have mine upstairs."

LADY SINGLETON's parting words came back to Vivien again and again in the sleepless night which she spent after Poppy's escapade. "Then it was your business to know.”

Was it really her business to know what the young things were doing, or thinking of doing? If so, what a difficult job was that of the modern mother, and how impossible if she was the mother of an heiress! "Would I were a neatherd's daughter 

And my Leonatus our neighbour shepherd's son," sighed Vivien, with Imogen. "If I had five hundred a year and lived with my Primula in a little house! Per-

haps I should know something then about my daughter and her friends! "Now I know nothing. Prim has twenty-five thousand a year, to spend on-face powder if she likes, and the flies gather from far and near-all sorts. This house is like an hotel, and I know about as much of our guests as a clerk in an hotel knows of its clients. "Perhaps I ought to go out with her more.. 

But she doesn't want me. She'd far rather go out with

Violet Paddington, or Lady Mary. They are smart and important, and dance beautifully, and go everywhere.

And they love to take her about and give her a good time. Whereas I can't be smart, and have left off wanting to dance myself, and am bored to madness if

I have to spend the whole evening watching other people. "The fact is—I ought to have been an entirely different person! "To Prim's friends I'm a nonentity. I have so little dignity, so little personality that Poppy used my name as a cover for her-dirty little plot, and thinks 

I 'wouldn't mind'! "I'm a woman of forty-four with good brains, and,

I believe, a special talent. Augustus Pettitt said sohe told me not to waste it. And I spend my days seeing that a lot of pampered young people are always provided with the most delicious food procurable, and plenty of it, whether they turn up two or twenty.

"Am I being a-damn fool?"

It was on the disturbing query that Vivien fell asleep. 

At half-past ten next morning, after discussing lunch and dinner with the cook, she went to Primula's bedroom. Sophie was just coming out with a breakfast tray. Empty coffee-pot, empty cream jug, sketchy traces of kidney omelette, skins of grapes and peaches.

Vivien perceived that her daughter had made her usual hearty meal. Mademoiselle was still in bed, said 

Sophie, reading her letters.

Vivien knocked and went in, and stood at the foot of the cream lacquer bed. 

How pretty the child was in her little blue silk jacket, with a close cap of Venise point over her silvergold hair! Vivien had to steel her heart by remembering her old nurse's favourite saying: Handsome is that handsome does.

The bed was strewn with letters and invitations. 

Primula was reading a long one, written on thin foreign paper. She looked up at her mother with an odd look, half amused, half defiant. "Well, Mother," she said. "Come to give me my spanking?" "Yes, Prim," said Vivien seriously, fixing her dark eyes on the girl. "I'm obliged to tell you how utterly ashamed I am that my daughter should lend herself to a-dirty, despicable scheme like that of Poppy's yes-

terday.

The Singletons are old country neighbours, too-" "It wasn't dirty," cried Primula, nettled, and giving an indignant plunge in her bed. "At least-they brought it on themselves. They ought to have given their consent." "You know well why they didn't. Freddie Iffley's a rotter. Did you read what the coroner said about him in that case last summer-when that chorus girl gassed herself?” "It was a mistake," said Primula hotly. "He wasn't the man." "Nobody in their senses would want their daughter to marry Freddie Iffley," pursued Vivien. "That was why her father tied up the money to guard her against such a marriage to give her time to recover from a foolish infatuation. . . . He couldn't foresee that his daughter would stoop so low as to spend two nights with a man to whom she wasn't married in order to blackmail her family-" "Darling," said Primula, with a little weary air. "You're fearfully Victorian. People don't take that sort of thing so deadly seriously nowadays.. .. Besides, she knew she would marry him in the end.” "Would you do it?" asked Vivien.

Primula wriggled uneasily under the bedclothes. "I might," she muttered. "If I was in love. She did it for love."

"She did it for money. If they'd had real, genuine love for one another, they'd have married and lived on his five hundred. Poppy has two hundred, hasn't she?

Lady Singleton would have come round before long. 

She may be . . . she is a super-bore and nuisance, but I honestly believe she loves Poppy. . . 

But you can't understand. You're too young. When you have children of your own-" "I'm not going to have any children," declared 

Primula. "Unless"-she shot a glance at her mother- "unless I have to have a son and heir." "I hope you're not going to marry Prince Dmitri," said Vivien plainly. Perhaps it was foolish to rouse the spirit of perversity in Primula, but she felt sick of management and finesse. She was finished with it! "Why?" asked Primula, with a naughty gleam in her eye. "Because he's a Bad Man? Do you know, I like Bad Men. And as Poppy's my best friend, I'm afraid we'll be seeing rather a lot of the Iffleys after they're married. Sorry, darling- 99 She stopped short, for Vivien was regarding her with a long, strange look that made her a little uneasy. 

She couldn't understand what was the matter with Mother. She seemed somehow stirred up. She looked quite young and excited, and spoke with a schoolgirlish vehemence. "Very well," said Vivien briskly. "And now I must go. I have shopping to do, and I intend to send in an

advertisement to the Morning Standard for a housekeeper. I'm going to be very busy." 

After posting the advertisement to the Morning

Standard with her own hand, Vivien told the chauffeur to drive to Bond Street and put her down at Pumphrey's, the super-stationers. She was not in the habit of dealing at Pumphrey's; the windows, full of gold stamp boxes and malachite inkstands, were enough to frighten away anybody who had ever lived in cheap lodgings and counted every penny. 

To-day, however, having withdrawn twenty pounds from her small private banking account, she was about to perform a ceremonial rite. 

She bought twelve large writing-pads, twelve pencils, a new bottle of blue-black ink, a small red typewriter, and, to finish up with, a large and flaunting scarlet quill. Not that she ever wrote with quill pens, but this was symbolical. It stood for revolution and a new order of things.

When these purchases were delivered in Caroline Square that afternoon, she sharpened all the pencils, arranged the typewriter on a little table in the window, and placed one of the writing-pads on her bureau. 

She dipped the scarlet quill into the blue-black ink, and wrote in large, bold letters across the front page of the pad, "The Heart of Sarah Sorel. By Vivien

Staniforth." So absorbed was she in these childish proceedings that Rosalind, coming back from a day's work among the catalogues, surprised her in the midst of them. "My dear Vivi," she said. "Where did you get that pen?" "It isn't a pen," returned Vivien. "It's a Red Flag -a standard of revolt. Rosalind-I've advertised for a housekeeper to-day. I'm going to be too busy to housekeep, and much too busy to hunt for caviares. 

I'm going to write my novel. Look!" she held up the writing pad. "The Heart of Sarah Sorel. That's the beginning of it. Oh-I've had Sarah Sorel in my head for months-"

Rosalind stared in astonishment at her flushed, excited sister, who seemed to have gone back again to the schoolgirl. "I'm delighted to hear it," she said. “I think it's a pity you ever let yourself dwindle into Primula's housekeeper and you don't care for Society. . 

I wish I'd a talent like yours."

She sighed as she took off her black velvet beret and pushed back her hair from her brow. "Tired of the pots?" asked Vivien sympathetically. "I think I am—a little. Or else it's the spring.

But there's nothing else that I can do." There was a tap at the door, and Rose came in with some letters.

"One for you, Rosalind," said Vivien, throwing it across. She fancied that she had seen the writing before. "Dentist," said Rosalind, tearing it open without interest. She ran her eye over it. "Well, I never did!" she exclaimed loudly at the end of a minute. "It's from Captain Sholto-Scotthe asks me to dine and dance with him at Borgia's.

Why, it's only a week since we went to The Lady of Dreams!"

Vivien stared steadily out of the window. "Probably he wants to continue the conversation you were having at The Lady of Dreams," she said in a colourless voice. "You looked like the Ideal Listener -listening with your eyes-” “Oh, he was quite interesting-telling me about his book." "Book?" said Vivien. "Oh, only a technical one on flying. He's awfully keen on it, though. And then we got on to period furniture he's not so ignorant as you would expect—” "Darling Flip!" murmured Vivien. “Well, I do know something about it," returned 

Rosalind, reddening a little. "Shall I accept?" "I think I should. It'll amuse you to see Borgia's." "I could wear my black," mused Rosalind, "and talk about Primula. Vivien-would you like him as a husband for Prim?"

"Prim?" said Vivien, casting about for words sufficiently vague. "Well, of course, if Prim liked him and he liked Prim, I'd be quite pleased. He's very attractive, and what's better, for an heiress, you can 

It may turn out so. trust him on sight. 

You never can tell!" SHOLTO-SCOTT, attired in his dressing-gown, was standing by the window of his sitting-room at halfpast two in the morning. It was pouring with rain; the houses opposite were all darkened, and a line of street lamps was dismally reflected in the glistening wet pavements.

Sholto-Scott stared into the darkness. Somewhere over there, he knew, lay Bloomsbury and Caroline Square.

Then, with a very deep furrow between his black brows, he dropped the curtain and came to the fireplace, where a few red ashes still glowed. Mrs. Renfrew had put everything ready for him; drinks, arm-chair drawn up to the fire, his pipe, even a match sticking out of the matchbox.

He disregarded the whisky, lit his pipe, and sat down soberly in front of the dying fire to face a fact of

every which he had been subconsciously aware for some time; the simple, devastating fact that he was in love with a woman who liked him a little because he had listened sympathetically when she spoke of her dead lover; that he found himself thinking of her in his waking moment, except when he was actually talking technical stuff with his flying friends, or pondering over some intricate diagram for his book; that he hadn't seen her for over a week-eight days to be accurate; he had counted them-and it had seemed longer than the previous eight months; that she was coming out to dinner with him the day after tomorrow; and after that-what?

It was useless to think of saying anything to her now; she would not listen. She was still thinking of the other fellow. Constancy was the most intolerable of the virtues, if it was not actually a vice! The only thing to be said for it was, it had kept her for him, if he could capture her!

He sighed, and stretched out his long legs to the dead fire. It was going to be a ticklish job, worse than the Pinkerton Trophy Race. How, without alarming her, was he ever going to make her see him as a man, Fitzroy Sholto-Scott, not as a distinguished airman, or an agreeable acquaintance. She wasn't even a flying fan.

Well-he must simply be somehow the best friend a

woman ever had, until the time came when he could safely turn into the most devoted lover. If-no, when, he got to the point when he could say one-tenth of the things that came boiling up in his heart whenever he thought of her, she would probably make a tremendous fuss because she was a little older than he. 

She was, of course; five or six years. Flip in the

Jolliffes' garden had been about sixteen, he guessed; her hair was in a pigtail. And he had been ten. Six years was an immense gulf at that age, so wide that Flip had been practically unable to see him across it, and only her irresistible girlish charm had bridged it for him.

But now, it was nothing. Thirty-one and thirtyseven; fifty-one and fifty-seven-what did it matter?

In her presence he never thought of age. The Rosalind of thirty-seven was the Flip of sixteen. 

She must not go to America. That must be prevented at all costs. How, he was unable to imagine. But he knew that if she did go to America, he would have to go too.

In the meantime, a man who hopes to marry must think very seriously about money. She hadn't much, he guessed. She had told him that she was living in that big, luxurious house because her sister wanted her company. Well-it didn't matter.

Frowning, he mentally went over his own resources. 

He had a small income inherited from his father; then, he had saved a little. It was the worst of luck that his elbow should have crocked up like this, just at this time. Mr. Sellars had told him it might take a year to mend or even longer. When that should happen, he was sure of a good position with Aerial Transit, Ltd. 

In the meantime, there was his book; Mr. Stanley 

Brookes seemed to think it would go off like hot cakes. 

He would probably get some writing to do on air matters for the papers. 

But he would have to leave off his present way of life, agreeable though it was. He would have to work at his book, really work, so many hours a day regularly. 

He would have to take his arm far more seriously; more rest, more massage. He was a dashed fool to go about so much, even if he did enjoy meeting charming and famous men and women. 

Also, he must spend less money.

This idea went very much against the grain. He liked entertaining people. The party he had given to-night had been a dazzling success-except that it did not include Rosalind. There had been eight of them, all young and attractive, some of them famous. 

They had dined and danced at Gustave's; perfect band, perfect floor, perfect dinner. Prettiest of his women guests was Primula Staniforth, the Golden 

Girl, looking like may-blossom in a white frilly thing

and pearls. But he no longer thought of her as the

Princess of Stocks and Shares; she was merely Rosalind's young niece.

He had danced with her more than with anybody else, partly because she was a little darling, but chiefly because he thought she was like Rosalind. The shape of her face, the lines of her mouth, her fair colouring, a sudden elusive expression-he couldn't help looking out for these things. They thrilled and delighted him. As for what the child talked about-he had no idea. 

Once, with Primula in his arms, he had caught young Lydney glaring at him. He had invited the young man out of fellow-feeling, someone having given him a hint that it would be an act of kindness. Personally, he thought that the Golden Girl would be dashed lucky if she could get him. Young Lydney was a fine young fellow, far better than a shifty princeling of Ruritania, equerry or no equerry. He wished him luck, and after that intercepted glare, he had not danced any more with Primula. "But-heavenly Rosalind!" · · At the same hour, in Caroline Square, Rosalind was lying fast asleep under her green silk eiderdown, and Primula sat dreaming in front of her mirror, and making no haste to go to bed.

She had sent Sophie away. She couldn't bear to have anyone about, talking to her and touching her and coming between her and the lovely bliss of her thoughts. So she sat, half-in and half-out of her flounced georgette frock, absently fingering the pearls which had once encircled the wrinkled neck of Greataunt Adelaide, and were now enjoying a happier destiny.

Something of this crossed Primula's mind, for she laid her cheek caressingly for a moment against her milk-white shoulder. How lucky that she looked—like this, and not like poor Adelaide, the plainest of the 

Staniforths! How heavenly to have so much to give!

For she wanted to give. She was in love, she knew it now, and revelled in the knowledge. It was quite different from anything she had ever felt before. All the things that had seemed so important up to now, her flying, her golf, her parties, were like dolls and teddy bears and the games one played when one was a child. Every day was filled with delicious new thrills, because, although it didn't happen nearly often enough, it was always possible that she might see him somewhere.

And at the end, far off, but coming nearer and nearer every minute, was the most exquisite thrill of all, which one could hardly bear to think of, it was so sweet!

Primula blushed and smiled at herself in the mirror.

Suddenly she wanted everybody in the world to be very happy.

They would go somewhere really original for their honeymoon; perhaps in the Dragon, over seas and deserts, to visit ancient overgrown temples in the jungles and queer primitive tribes the two of them alone together in-China or wherever it was. 

Or she would buy a yacht, and they would cruise round divinely beautiful islands in the magic South Seas!

But no! She would arrange nothing, make no plans, express no wishes. He should choose; and if it was a boarding-house in Brighton, she would gladly go with him there and be in Paradise.

How heavenly to feel that there was someone whom you were dying to obey and utterly submit to!

Of course they would have children. She had never wanted them before, but now, in some extraordinary way, she felt quite differently about it. A little creature in a white fluffy coat and cap, dark like his fathershe hugged him in her arms.

This evening had been bliss; not unalloyed, for he had to dance with his other guests. Still, he had danced oftener with her than with any of the other girls, even 

Griselda Green, who, though married, was an able and determined man-snatcher. 

Of course, he hadn't said anything in the leastthrilling. She was afraid he was going to be rather

slow. But over and over again, she had caught him watching her surreptitiously, and as long as he looked at her like that, she didn't mind his silence-for the moment. 

What was that? Three o'clock. She stood up, and let her frock slip off in white billows round her feet.

"Do you think Sophie could iron out my black frock?" asked Rosalind at breakfast. "It's a little bit crumpled, and I shan't be back till rather late." "Of course she can," said Vivien. "She loves doing things for you. . . . Do look at these letters. I had forty-seven yesterday, and there must be thirty here. 

It'll take me hours to go through them."

She gazed gloomily at a basket filled with replies to her advertisement. "Never mind," said Rosalind. "When you've found the pearl of housekeepers, you'll be able to go full steam ahead at Sarah Sorel, hammer and tongs, morning, noon, and night."

After she had gone, Vivien picked up the basket, and took it up to her sitting-room, where she began opening one or two, but half-heartedly. She was think-

ing, not of housekeepers but of Rosalind and Sholto- Scott dining together to-night. She did want Flip to look lovely, as she could sometimes, not with Prim's undeniable, healthy young beauty, but with some subtler and more elusive charm. 

Presently she threw the letters into a drawer, and went to Rosalind's room. There, in the wardrobe, hung Rosalind's three evening frocks, the white, the black, and the flowered chiffon. Vivien inspected them, frowning. The white wouldn't do at all; it ought to go to the cleaner's. The flowered chiffon she had worn before. There remained the black satin. 

It was new, it was well-made, it hung in severe, classical folds. There was nothing to be said against it, except that somehow it was essentially the garment of an aunt. "If she wears that," thought Vivien, "she'll talk about nothing but politics and education. She won't be able to help herself."

Nevertheless, she took the black satin gown to Sophie's sewing room, where the French maid spent several hours every day attending to Primula's clothes. 

Some of these lay about; snowdrifts of white tulle, flounces of flowered taffeta, and little piles of scanty, gossamer garments, waiting to have ribbons run through their laces. But Vivien's eyes were instantly held by the frock which lay across Sophie's knees. "Flip!" she said to herself.

"What's that, Sophie?" she asked. "I don't think 

I've seen it before." "It's a Jeanne Régnard, Madame. Mademoiselle wore it at 'Adlee Castle. C'est ravissante, n'est-ce pas?" "Very ravissante, indeed. Let me see it," said Vivien, a sudden inspiration coming to her.

Sophie held it up with the admiration of a fellow artist: frill after frill of chiffon of an exquisite sapphire blue. And the bodice not backless, for once.

Primula exhibited her charming back with the unconcern of a mermaid, but Vivien doubted whether Rosalind could be induced to do so. 

At this moment Vivien formed a criminal resolution. "Please put on the electric iron, Sophie," she said. "I want to press Miss Forey's dress. . . . No, don't trouble, you're busy. I'll do it myself. How long does it take to get hot? Three minutes? Very well." 

At the end of the three minutes she sent Sophie away on an errand, and waited three minutes more.

Then, with the air of a Lady Macbeth, she lifted the hot iron, planted it firmly on the front breadth of Rosalind's aunt-like garment, and held it there for several singeing seconds. 

Sophie, coming back with the gloves, found Madame gazing in dismay at a dark brown patch, the shape of an iron, in front of Miss Forey's gown. A smell of burning pervaded the room.

"Madame!" she shrieked. "Quel dom-mage!" "The iron was too hot," said Vivien tragically. "And Mademoiselle is going out to dinner to-night.

She has nothing else to wear. 

Sophie, we must find something for her one of Miss Primula's dresses. 

What about that blue one?"

As she had expected, Rosalind was difficult to persuade. "I won't wear it without asking Prim's permission," she said positively. "Darling, you can't. Prim's gone off with the Paddingtons to Hadleigh. And you can't wear your flowered chiffon. It wouldn't be decent.” "I'll send a message to say I can't come-I'm prevented by sudden bereavement," said Rosalind, gazing ruefully at the ruined frock. "Flip! Be a sport. The man's trying to give you a little pleasure. And you know Prim won't mind. She has twenty dance frocks. . . . Here, just slip it over your head. There! You look lovely!"

And gazing at herself in the mirror, Rosalind tasted the joy of beauty. She did look lovely in Jeanne 

Régnard's lovely frock. How pearly white her neck shone against that glorious deep blue, and her pretty bare shoulders! How blue it made her eyes! And her hair had Primula's own gleam in it, like winter sun-

shine. After all, it was rather fun looking like this! "Oh, very well," she said. "I'm not accustomed to being so unclothed, but I daresay Captain Sholto- Scott won't notice it. He's used to Pamphletts, and will probably think me overdressed. . . . Oh, Vivi, isn't it humiliating to think what a few yards of chiffon will do for a woman?"

SHOLTO-SCOTT, the cool and adequate, felt his heart beating like a love-sick schoolboy's as he waited in the lounge at Borgia's for Rosalind to come out of the cloak-room. And when she rather suddenly appeared, smiling at him with her usual careless friendliness, he could not think of anything to say but "I love you."

However, as that would have been fatal, he bit it back and managed to recollect that it was cold for April. 

He thought that she was the most beautiful creature in the room, though it contained Nicolette Nevill, and the famous American beauty, Mrs. Laura Pentonville, besides a number of blooming young girls. These were all very well in their own way, but none of them had Rosalind's fascinating air of slight mystery, the look of one who can "remember sad things, yet be gay." "This is great fun," said Rosalind, her spirits rising.

She had refused a cocktail, but the look in Sholto- Scott's eyes when he first caught sight of her had had the effect of one. She gave due credit honourably to 

Jeanne Régnard, but she felt exhilarated all the same. "You choose," she said, when the waiter came to their table. "I don't know one thing from another when I'm with a good companion.' 99 "Thank you," murmured Sholto-Scott. She was taking things in the right way. First, the good companion, then the firm friend, and then the adoring lover, since she would not have it the other way round. 

While he was ordering the meal, Rosalind looked about her like a child at a fair. Parchment-tinted walls, mirrors in faint gold frames, a shining expanse, through an archway, of pale straw-coloured boards, lights in oddly shaped lamps of white and amber glass. "So this is Borgia's," she said. "One's always reading about. It looks very-subdued." "That's to show off the dresses of the women. You, for instance, look like a cornflower in a field of ripe wheat. On the edge of a cliff," he added, thinking of Understrand. "How lovely!" Her eyes sparkled with amusement. 

Thirty-seven. "You're a poet." "Not until this moment," said he, then retreated, for this was dangerous country. "This isn't so subdued as it looks, all the same. I can see at this moment a

tragedy, a melodrama, and a French farce going on at these tables." "Which is the tragedy? I don't move in these circles, you know." "Do you see that woman over there in the silver dress?" "The quite elderly one in the gorgeous emeralds?" "Yes. Well, that's her husband, dancing with the girl in red."

Rosalind watched the couple he had pointed out for a minute or two. "They're in love," she said, at last. "And she can't take her eyes off them." "Yes. He's twenty years younger than she is, and he married her for her money." "How hideous!" cried Rosalind. "I do despise people who will not-pay up. It's the essence of immorality, it seems to me. "He would say that he doesn't love his wife." “Love is not everything," said Rosalind. "Even I know that-thank Heaven!" "That's Lily Lola, the film star, with her late husband, her husband, and her fiancé." "Oh!" she gasped. "Can that be true?" "So people say." "Oh, but people will say absolutely anything.

Do you see that jolly, countrified, ride-to-hounds sort of girl in pink, with the nice Oxford boy? He's pointing you out to her. Who do you think he's telling her that I am?" "My wife, probably," said he.

Rosalind looked at him with eyes as clear as a windswept sky. "More probably, your eldest sister," she said, laughing. "Or your aunt.”

Sholto-Scott reddened, and she felt that she had in some obscure way vexed him. "Now you are being quite absurd," he said abruptly, jumping up. "Shall we dance?" "I don't believe you ought to," she replied. "You don't give your arm a chance.' 99 "I don't use it dancing. But you're right. After this evening I'm going to give up dancing for a while. 

Not to-night, though. 'Who knows but the world may end to-night?" "

He slipped his arm round her waist, and they took

a turn round the room. Flip had been an impassioned dancer, and Rosalind had not lost her lightness and swaying grace. The band was playing a heartrending melody called "Lonely Without You." Sholto-Scott felt his heart thumping. "The first time," he said to himself. "The first time. . . ." He glanced at Rosalind. Her eyes were closed. For the moment, she was in the arms of Dick circling round the old schoolroom at Understrand to the strains of the gramophone, and when she opened her eyes and met those of Sholto- Scott, he knew, by an intuition of love, where she had been. Her eyes were haunted. "Something's set her remembering," he thought. "Wonder if it's the music. . . . Bad luck."

He did not know that it was his own strong clasp, and individual rhythm of movement which had reminded Rosalind of her dead lover.

By the time that they were seated at their table again, and the waiter was placing plump little birds before them, she was as serene as before. .. He adored her! He wanted to tell her everything! "I went to see Sellars on Saturday," he said. "My specialist man. He told me that this confounded tendon might take a year to get right—perhaps more. Of course, that was pretty serious news for me. I've lost my job for the time being-the only thing I can do a little better than the next man. I shan't be able to fly for a year."

"Oh!" she said. "I'm terribly sorry. Lord Lydney was saying only yesterday how wonderful you were in the air-especially in a race. He said you'd got the touch that means genius. You'll miss it." "Infernally," said he. "I feel like an old soldier." "What do you do with yourself—?” She checked herself. "Oh, but of course, you've heaps of friends, and you like to go about and see them. Prim seems to meet you quite a lot." "I go out too much," he said frankly. "I like people, and it's a temptation. But I'm going to turn over a new leaf. I'm going to slog at my book, and I've got some articles to write for the Weekly Sentinel. On flying, of course. I want to make money-quite a lotfor a certain purpose." 99 "Well," said Rosalind. "It's a great help to have something to work for. I saved up fifteen pounds for a bicycle, once. I could never have saved just fifteen pounds." "What about America?" he asked bluntly. "America's in abeyance. That is, Dr. Zellich is ordered three months' complete rest. So I have a respite. But it's only a respite. I want to make money, too." She hesitated, then went on. "Sir Benjamin left me five thousand pounds, you know, but I'm not old enough to retire on it yet. And all I've got is what

I've picked up from him about Chinese pottery and old furniture."

"What would you like to do best?" he said. “Oh-keep a shop! There's no question about it.

I've always hankered after a little shop. When I was a child, it was a sweet-shop, or a toy-shop, but now I'm not so devoted to bull's-eyes and nougat. . . . I think a little grocer's shop on a village green would be fun, don't you? With a post office. In between selling tea and bootlaces, you could be reading the postcards. 

Or a bookshop, in a cathedral town, not too far from the country. I adore the country." · • "So do I," said he. "I was brought up five miles from a railway station." "You surprise me," said Rosalind. "I rather thought- You have such an-urban air. At leastnot quite. I can imagine you in deserts."

She gazed frankly at the dark, hawk face before her. “Oh, I didn't take at all kindly to deserts," he said. "I like the tame-green-country. . . . Do you ever go for walks?" "Often, at the week-ends." "Good. So do I. Will you take me for a walk some day?" 

She looked at him in surprise. "If you'd really like it,” she said, doubtfully. And then, a brilliant idea struck her. She had hardly mentioned Prim at all, so far. Here was a chance. "We might get Primula to drive us down to her cottage

in Sussex, and start from there the three of us." 

Or the two of you, she reflected.

Sholto-Scott murmured something very polite, but he did not welcome the scheme so enthusiastically as she had expected. Nor did he seize the opportunity to talk about Primula. Probably he was one of those reticent people who can discuss everything except the thing that is nearest their heart. Well-she couldn't compel him to talk about Primula!

So they danced and they talked, and not another word was said about Primula for the rest of the evening. 

THE next Monday morning, two smart young men with cameras after hanging about outside the Paddington Registrar's Office for some time, were rewarded by the sight of two taxis dashing up from different directions and arriving almost simultaneously at the door. From the first of these alighted two very pretty girls, whose faces were perfectly familiar to all readers of the Babbler, the Chatterer, and the Planet. 

From the other, jumped two young men, straight of feature, sleek of hair, and wearing, one a white carnation, the other a red one.

Up went the cameras. The bride-to-be, instead of ducking her head and making a rush for the door, stood still and turned a brilliant smile upon them. She had a large spray of scarlet orchids pinned into her ermine coat. Her companion, shyest of the four, tried unobtrusively to conceal herself behind all this radiance. "Oh, Prim, isn't it a scream?" cried Poppy, with an excited laugh. "Don't be so retiring. We want everybody to see us. I'm going to talk to those men afterwards. . . . Hullo, old pancake!" The two young men, heedless of the cameras, greeted the girls with hilarity, and a very gay bridal party entered the office. Prim had seldom been in such a dismal place. "Buck up, old chap," said Jack Twickenham,

Freddie's friend, who had a vague idea that this was the correct thing to say to a bridegroom. But Freddie needed no such encouragement. Indeed, Primula soon began to be sure that he had taken the insidious one too many. And Poppy was laughing far too much, though Primula knew that she was intoxicated with excitement and nothing else.

She thought the old Registrar looked at them rather severely over his glasses, and for an uncomfortable moment she saw their party with his eyes: four frivolous empty-headed young creatures, giggling and joking over one of the most serious and fateful moments

of a lifetime—for it was a serious moment, even if you had arranged to get divorced the minute you suspected it might be going to be a flop.

She felt a stirring of distaste. Freddie's triumphant smile seemed a grin, Poppy's merriment a gaudy mask. 

She thought of all that had passed between these two in her own rose-chintz room in the cottage she would have those chintzes changed!--and wondered if this was truly love, even in disguise. Had she been rather a fool? Was Mother right about this?

But Primula's mind, unaccustomed to more than a momentary contemplation of the unpleasant, soon switched off in another direction.

She was at another wedding; not a farcical one, this, and not in a dreary registrar's office. Far above her head were the grey fan traceries and jewel-like windows of an ancient church, and against a background of spring flowers and chanting, she saw his dark face, usually so gallantly careless, but eager now, his blue eyes under their black brows looking at her with strange intentness, seeking for something. "Oh, my darling, I am yours-all, all of me,” cried 

Primula, almost aloud, and woke to hear the Registrar saying: 66 "... that you know of no lawful impediment to this contract of marriage. I, Pauline Elfrida 

Singleton. . . ." · • · Poppy repeated the words after him, and after-

wards the jaunty Freddie.

Then they signed an immense ledger. 

The Registrar stood up, and they had to stand up before him as if they were in church. Jack Twickenham decided not to pretend he had forgotten the ring. "I take this man-" said the Registrar a few minutes later. "I take this man-" repeated Poppy. "Frederick Iffley, to be my lawful wedded husband." "Frederick Iffley, to be my lawful wedded husband.” 

What a tiny little voice Poppy had! "Thank you," said the Registrar.

Freddie Iffley had secured his hundred thousand pounds.

Primula and Jack Twickenham were now requested to sign their names, and then Primula threw her arms round Poppy's neck. She was feeling quite sloppy and

Victorian-probably an effect of being in love. "Darling Poppy, I do hope you'll be terribly happy." “Don't,” said Poppy in a queer voice. “Or I shall howl, or be sick or something.' 99 

Her dark eyes looked suddenly scared and childish. 

Then Jack Twickenham came forward and kissed her heartily. "Best of luck, Mrs. Iffley," said he. "Don't let him beat you." "If he does," said Poppy, recovering her gaiety, "I'll elope with you, Jack."

"Right O!" said he. "Just ring me up-noughtnought-nought-nought-nought Gerrard."

There was some merriment when a clerk presented 

Poppy with her "marriage lines," and then Freddie, after bestowing the necessary tips, took his wife's arm, and walked her off towards the door. Jack Twickenham turned with great alacrity to escort the Staniforth heiress. He did not often get the chance of having her to himself. And Primula knew every thought in his head, and smiled to herself a little scornfully, lifting her chin.

Outside, the two young men were waiting like wolves to fall upon them, and Primula heard Poppy giving them a great deal too much information—and misinformation, as well. "It was a boy and girl affair,” she declared. "We've known each other all our lives. . . . No, we only decided to get married three days ago. . . . Nobody knew except Miss Staniforth, my dearest friend.

It'll be a complete surprise to our people. 

Honeymoon? Oh, we're going motoring. won't tell you where. It's a secret. · • • 

. . No, I 

But you can tell the world we're most gorgeously happy." 

She clung to the arm of the beaming Freddie, and showed the reporter her pretty pearly teeth. The other young man took a charming, if slightly disdainful snapshot of Primula, which duly appeared in the Comet, the Evening Post, and all the picture papers.

Lunch at the Berkeley followed, with plenty of champagne. It isn't every day that you marry a girl with money. And then the bride and bridegroom nerved themselves for the visit to Portman Square, in which they were to confess the marriage, be reconciled to the obdurate relations, and become eligible for 

Poppy's fortune. 

Lady Singleton was expecting them. After having pondered every way of avoiding scandal, she had decided that the young couple had better take the law into their own hands, have a secret marriage unknown to her and the family, and be formally forgiven afterwards. St. Margaret's, Westminster, would require too much time, and any immediate marriage in a quiet church would look odd, she felt, to prying friends. 

But a registrar's office-you just nip in for half an hour and the thing is done. "Like to come with us, Prim?" asked Poppy, who had taken a great deal of champagne, of Primula who had barely taken a sip. "You know how popular you are with Mother and Toby." "I'd upset the whole apple-cart," returned Primula. "No thank you, Jack, I'm walking home alone.” 

And the Golden Girl did walk home alone, very soberly, to Caroline Square. 

Sir Thomas Singleton, opening his Comet, the same evening, was confronted with "Secret Love Romance of Baronet's Sister." He hurled it from him, he swore,

and when Jill his wife innocently presented him with a dressmaker's bill, for only ten pounds, he was so disagreeable that she would have gone home to Mummy on the spot, only Mummy happened to be in Monte Carlo.
