AFTER the evening at Borgia's, Sholto-Scott did not see or hear anything of Rosalind for a fortnight, nor had he any certainty as to when he should see her again. She was maddeningly elusive, and at this stage it would be easy for him to lose touch, and slip into the category of agreeable acquaintances whom one is quite pleased to see two or three times a year, and never thinks of in between.

He had begun to work in earnest on his book, and stayed at home enough to satisfy Mrs. Renfrew, who now began to fear that he was working too hard, and not getting enough change. She had an attachment, half romantic, half motherly, to this reputed daredevil, who was so quiet and pleasant in the house. 

Besides, she found it duller now that the imposing people, and pretty "Society girls" no longer came.

Sholto-Scott had frankly informed his friends that he must cut out the pleasures of society until the book was well in hand.

While he was working at it with the help of a small typewriter, he forgot everything else, but at all other times his loneliness was filled with Rosalind. She moved about the room with her light and rather restless tread. 

She sat at his table. Sometimes she was Flip with her charming vehemence and merriment; sometimes,

Rosalind, who had been through it, and was subdued by life to her present detached serenity. 

He must see her again, and soon! And yet, he could not devise how. He could not go on asking her to dinner, nor could he call every Sunday at Caroline

Square, as he would have liked to do. She would see what he was up to, and nip it in the bud, for she was not ready yet to love again. She was still absorbed in the story of her disaster. He had by now divined that there was a streak of obstinacy in the composition of his Rosalind, but he didn't care! Let her have all the faults of woman! He wanted her in his little house with the Hepplewhites and the David Cox. 

He decided over a late pipe that he would call on 

Mrs. Staniforth the Sunday after next. That would be more than three weeks after Borgia's. Last Saturday, Sir John Taverner of the Air Ministry had carried him off almost by force for a short week-end down in Kent. Charming people, the Taverners; real friends, though he'd only known them two months. He thought with pleasure of Lady Taverner, with her deep voice, her sympathy and common sense, her

knowledge of men and matters. How he would like to take Rosalind there! He saw himself carrying her suitcase, showing her off to Lady Taverner and Sir John. 

They would be charmed with her. 

In the meantime, he was dining to-morrow with Sir 

Everard and Lady Mary Staniforth at Carlton House 

Terrace; he had not refused this invitation. Not that he expected to meet Rosalind there, being dimly aware that Mrs. Staniforth and her sister were not inhabitants of Lady Mary's world. He recalled the people he had met at Vivien's Sunday-a woman doctor, a clever young journalist, an old Professor of Classics. Not people of fashion. Still-Lady Mary was sister's mother-in-law to Rosalind, and that was a strong bond. 

Besides, Lady Mary was Lady Mary. 

His dinner partner at Lady Mary's was Mrs. Stillingfleet, the famous traveller, a grey-haired vivacious lady who was far less anxious to discuss the Pinkerton Trophy Race than to give him all the details of her discovery of a prehistoric Boo-Boo temple in the forests of the Amazon. Sholto-Scott was perfectly willing; so after five minutes of mutual politeness, he left her to it, making an interested sound from time to time. Mrs. Stillingfleet reported afterwards that he was not only a distinguished airman, but also a most fascinating talker.

Far down the long table, across a low hedge of fluffy mimosa, he could see the Golden Girl, a picture of youthful beauty in a Victorian frock that slipped off her shoulders. She looked bored and listless, and took little trouble to hide it from her partner, a witty young 

Member of Parliament. "Spoilt young monkey!" thought Sholto-Scott, but indulgently, for besides being related to Rosalind, she was exceedingly pretty. "She doesn't deserve to sit next to O'Hara. But most likely she's annoyed with the poor devil for not being someone else young 

Lydney, perhaps.' 99 He could sympathise with that; it was very annoying of people not to be somebody else. Lord Lydney he discovered not far from himself, in plodding, punctilious conversation with a brocaded matron in a tiaraprobably a countess. Being a marquis wasn't all jam, it appeared.

When the men went back to the big drawing-room after dinner, Sholto-Scott cast a hawk's eye down the room. More people had come in, for there was to be music: Scarpia, the great violinist, and Olga Rosencrantz, the Covent Garden soprano.

The long, beautiful room seemed like an amberlighted pool, full of subdued movement and colour; but after a moment he discovered her, sitting pensively by herself on an Empire settee. Three or four men, young and middle-aged, were already gathered round

her, all of them standing, for she had spread her rose-coloured flounces over the seat of the little sofa, and made no move to draw them aside for anybody.

If Sholto-Scott had hoped to make a bee-line for her, he was disappointed, so many people intercepted him: Lady Waterman, asking his advice about dear

Eric, who wanted to join the Air Force; Algernon 

Gore, the novelist, who talked about his new Moth; Sir 

Montagu Bootle, who talked about nothing. But gradually he worked his way nearer. And Primula, from beneath her long lashes, noted every step of his progress. 

At last, he boldly presented himself before her. "May I sit on your frills, Miss Staniforth?"

She lifted her eyes with a faint smile, then dropped them again, and made room for him beside her. There was a perceptible deepening of her apple-blossom tints, but he put that down to the fact that young Lydney had sprung, apparently, out of the ground, and was hanging about behind the settee. Well-he wouldn't keep them long, but he positively must talk for a moment to someone who could give him news of 

Rosalind. As for the other men, let them drop into oblivion!

They did; or at least, he never saw them again. "How's the Dragon?" he asked. "Been up in her lately?"

Primula laid her finger on her lip. "Madame Rosencrantz is going to sing," she murmured.

She was glad of the respite. She wanted to get calmer. She felt as stupid and as tongue-tied as any 

Victorian miss. Her heart was thumping, her thoughts were in a whirl, she was afraid of saying straight out, "I love you." Prim Staniforth had never dreamt that she could feel like this.

When Madame Rosencrantz's voice had come down from soaring at Heaven's gate, she answered his question. "No. I haven't been up in her at all. I've beentoo busy."

He chaffed her gently about the drop in her enthusiasm. But neither he nor she was really listening to the conversation they were making. He was edging nearer to his object, and she was listening to the sound of his voice. Somewhere behind the sofa, George appeared to be studying Sir Everard's Watteau. "How is Mrs. Staniforth?" asked Sholto-Scott next. "She has a bad cold. That's why she isn't here to-night. She does so love Rosencrantz's singing." "I'm sorry to hear that," he said sincerely. "I hope -Miss Forey's quite well?” "Yes, thank you," said Primula. Men were funny!

Why did he think it necessary to put in all this talk about mothers and aunts? "She's finished her catalogue," she added, for something to say.

“Oh, has she?” said he. Anything to go on talking about her! "I was tremendously struck by her knowledge of antiques-not that I'm anything of a judge. 

I'm a very ignorant chap, really, but I like old furniture and china. And the day she took me over the Cuffe

Collection, I couldn't help seeing how much she knew about every piece, its good points and its bad, and how it was made, and when." "Yes," said Primula. She knew that Rosalind was considered to have quite a flair for this sort of thing, and she remembered an occasion when Algernon Gore had come to lunch, and instead of entertaining the company with his ironic wit, had insisted on talking to Rosalind the whole time about crackled glaze and celadon ware. Well-if something happened, there was a lot of old furniture at Havisham. "Sir Benjamin used to send her to sales for him," she said. "Oh, did he? And does Miss Forey-?" "Captain Sholto-Scott!" came a deep, seductive voice, with a hundred echoes of past triumphs in its lazy assurance. "Where have you been hiding? Oh, I know I saw you when you came, but ever since you've been positively non-existent."

And Sholto-Scott received the impression that Lady Mary's evening had been a failure up to this point. "Lady Mary-" he stammered, springing to his feet.

She stood before them in the cold fire of her diamonds, which flashed from her hair, her firm white neck and arms, and the bodice of her black tulle gown. 

Marvellous woman! Other grandmothers of her age were glad to be at home by the fire, with woolly shawls on their shoulders, and their feet on footstools. "Oh, I'm not going to sit down. . . . Prim, darling,

I want Captain Sholto-Scott to see my new Sickert.” 

And somehow or other, Primula never thought of saying that she also would like to see the new Sickert.

She began to understand why Granny had been such a terror to wives and sweethearts in the days of her prime. Tears welled into her grey eyes. It was too bad!

Just when he was getting past the mothers and aunts! 

Lady Mary, carrying off her captive, was able to ascertain by a quick glance that George had slipped into his vacant place. Not a very auspicious moment for George perhaps, but she was not scheming now for him, but for Primula. Her Serene Highness She took Sholto-Scott into the green drawing-room, where the new Sickert was hung, and while they made a sufficiently commonplace talk about the little picture, Lady Mary amused herself by exercising her incomparable art of saying one thing and suggesting something quite different. She found this young man inspiring. "By Jove, that's a siren!" thought Sholto-Scott, when they came back to hear Scarpia play. “If she

were thirty years younger, I'd be head over heels. . . . 

But no, on second thoughts, I wouldn't. Not for an instant! Not for a single instant!"

WHEN he got home, there was a letter waiting for him on the mantelpiece-a cheap white envelope and a stiff round handwriting which he did not know. 

He tore it open and read: 

"CAPTAIN SHOLTO-SCOTT. DEAR SIR, 

I take the liberty of writing you because I read about you in the papers and thought you might perhaps remember me. I was your mechanic at Frampton. I lost my job six months ago, and had no luck since. I've been ill with pewmonia six weeks, and can't go out to look for work. I owe my landlady for board and lodging. 

She's a good soul, and that's why I take the liberty of asking you for help. 

Yours respectfully,

FRANK PITT." 

Frank Pitt. . . . Of course! The merry, roundfaced chap, who was so fond of playing practical jokes on the other men, jokes not always appreciated! A capable mechanic, too.'. . . Well, he must see what he could do.

Sholto-Scott glanced again at the address at the top

of the sheet: 4, DUKE'S HEAD PLACE, WELLESLEY 

SQUARE, S.W.1. Quite a smart address—in the neighbourhood of the fashionable square. All the same, "Duke's Head" suggested a public-house rather than the world of fashion.

Duke's Head Place was not easy to find when you had never been there before, although it was only five minutes' walk from the stately square. It lay in a confused hinterland of mews, back yards and small tobacconist and grocer shops. The Place itself was a paved triangle shaped by the haphazard building of former days. There were small houses on two sides, the third being formed by Douro Street, which led straight out of the square. At the far angle, a narrow lane, entered between posts, connected it with some other back street.

Sholto-Scott stood still and looked about him with interest. In its humble way, this was a genuine bit of 

Georgian London. The flat fronts of the houses were built of small, dark-brown bricks; there was a certain grace of proportion about them, and one of them still kept its fanlight and half-columns at the door, though it was obviously used as a kind of warehouse. 

A small, plain, iron lamp-post bore the cipher "G.R.," George Rex, but not King George V. At the corner, as he had expected, was the public-house, the

"Duke of Wellington" whose weather-worn features swung on a signboard above its modest door. He could imagine Thackeray's flunkeys coming here for their gin and water while my lord and my lady were diverting themselves in Wellesley Square. 

The "Duke" was Number 1; then came a newspaper and tobacconist's shop, then a window full of dingy second-hand books. Number 4 was an antique shop. 

Sholto-Scott glanced into the window before going in. The contents were dusty and unattractive—the débris and wreckage of many a vanished household, crowded together with no taste or arrangement. He looked above the window. The same name, "James 

Throckmorton," appeared above Number 3 and Number 4.

A bell on the door shrilled as he opened it, and an elderly woman came out of the room behind the shop, wiping her damp hands on a black sateen apron. 

Clearly there was not very much custom expected. "Good morning," said Sholto-Scott, "I have come to see Mr. Frank Pitt. I understand that he lives here." 

Her long melancholy face changed. "You'll be his 

Captain," she said. "He's always talking about you, but I cannot remember your name, sir." "Sholto-Scott. . . . And you are-Mrs. Throckmorton, I suppose? He told me you'd been very good to him." "I do what I can, sir. The poor lad puts me in mind

of a nephew that died. . . . Consumption." She sighed. "But it isn't so easy to make both ends meet nowadays, when business is so bad. And it's one woman's work to look after the lodgers." "I'm sure it must be," said Sholto-Scott sympathetically. "And now-can I see Mr. Pitt?" "Yes, sir. I'll go first, if you don't mind. He'll be jumping for joy when he sees you.' 

The languid form which lay in a dilapidated bed in the attic upstairs seemed incapable of anything so exuberant, but into the dull eyes there leapt a spark of incredulous joy when they fell upon Sholto-Scott. "Y-you, sir!" he stammered. "You've come yourself?" "Looks like it," said Sholto-Scott briskly. “And now, my lad, let's hear what's the matter with you." "I don't know, sir. I can't seem to get better, somehow." "And no wonder," thought Sholto-Scott. The tiny attic was clean, but the window looked out on a blank wall of blackish brick, and no sunbeam could ever make its way inside. Mrs. Throckmorton, too-good, kind soul!-was about as exhilarating as a wet November afternoon. This unlucky boy, accustomed to the limitless blue canopy of the air, had been staring for weeks at the cracks in the ceiling, six feet above his head. “Well, we must see what a change of air will do for

you," said Sholto-Scott cheerfully. Lady Taverner ... she would help him. "Thank you, sir. I knew you'd do the trick." And then his whole face lighted up with hero-worship. "You always did do the trick. I said so before the 

Pinkerton Trophy Race. 'He'll do the trick,' I told 'em. . . . And you did! How many miles per hour, sir ?" "Average three forty-five point seven," replied

Sholto-Scott, knowing well that he had not forgotten. "And how many records?" "Two."

After this, Sholto-Scott indulged him with a little shop about the race, and then asked a few searching questions before rising to go. "Don't worry about Mrs. Throckmorton,” he said. "I'll see to her. And I hope we'll have you out of this in a day or two." "Oh-sir-" And all day long Sholto-Scott was haunted by his eyes.

Downstairs, he had confabulation about ways and means with the landlady in a warm, but dreary back parlour. It was very satisfactory to Mrs. Throckmorton; a faint gleam of cheerfulness came into her faded eyes as she tucked a little wad of notes into some mysterious hidden pocket in her skirt.

She came with him to the door. Sholto-Scott looked about him with idle interest at the "antiques."

"You've got some-er-curious things here," he remarked. "Yes, sir. I don't know what half of them are.

It was very different in my poor husband's time. You see, he knew. He used to go down into the country, and come back with old chairs and tables-old rubbish, you'd have called them, but they looked beautiful when they came out of the workshop. You'd be surprised at the people who came here to buy-lords and ladies and gentlemen from all over London. You see, they knew my husband. He'd lived here over fifty years...

But now he's dead I can't look after the business. I can't go into the country like he did, and buy things. 

We've got the bookshop next door, too, but I just let them be; I don't understand about them. . . . If I could have a little business in a country place!" 

It was at those words, with their curious echo of some other words lightly spoken at Borgia's three weeks ago, that his great inspiration came to Sholto-Scott. 

Shops .. antique shops. Rosalind! Why shouldn't he and Rosalind start an antique shop to gether in Duke's Head Place? 

The idea-crazy and alluring-so took away his breath that he stood staring for over a minute at a very ugly picture of the Sack of Jerusalem, and Mrs. 

Throckmorton almost began to hope that he was going to buy it.

But he didn't. He turned his back on the fricassée of corpses depicted therein, and briskly began to ask questions, rather odd and inquisitive questions. But 

Mrs. Throckmorton, affected perhaps by the sudden breath of energy and purpose which had strangely blown into that stagnant atmosphere, answered him readily, with some faint hope of she knew not what.

The lease? . . . it had six years to run; the accommodation there was a parlour and a scullery be- hind the shop, and a workshop, now used for storing potatoes, in the tiny back yard. There were four bedrooms on the two floors above. The next-door house was exactly the same as this, only the other way round. 

Her husband had been dead three years, killed by a motor car in the Brompton Road. "Thank you, Mrs. Throckmorton," said Sholto 

Scott at the end of this catechism. "I'll be round tomorrow to see about young Pitt." He had nearly forgotten about young Pitt!"You'll get him the 

Brand's Essence?" "Yes, sir. Thank you, sir." 

As he walked home with a light-hearted swinging step, for second thoughts had not yet begun to exercise their cooling influence, he took careful note of the position of his dream shop.

It was not so difficult to find as he had supposed; five minutes in a direct line from Wellesley Square,

and not so very far from Oxford Street. A sufficiently central position. There was a touch of distinction, too, about the little place. He saw it restored to some of its 

Georgian dignity, with a fanlight above the door, perhaps, and half-columns. 

No doubt, before very long, all this would be swept away to make room for some damned soulless block of flats, so many boxes piled the one on top of the other, boxes full of people. But he hoped that before then it would have served his turn. 

He went into a telephone box at the corner of 

Wellesley Square, and put through two calls: one to

Sir John Taverner at the Air Ministry, and the other to Miss Forey at Caroline Square. Mrs. Staniforth answered the latter. Yes,

No. . . . Miss Forey was away in the country, but she was expected back to-morrow morning. . . .

Captain Sholto-Scott could certainly see her about some antique furniture. ... Would he come to lunch to-morrow? . . . That would be delightful.

Sholto-Scott accepted the invitation. He also found it delightful.

He spent a good deal of his evening in covering sheets of paper with calculations, and other sheets of paper with sketches and plans. A very happy evening!

§ THE cold fit came upon Sholto-Scott before he reached 

Caroline Square next day. Absurd, ridiculous, preposterous, senseless, unpractical, and even scandalous; these were some of the words that kept dancing through his head. Some or all of these epithets they would justly apply to him if he placed his hare-brained scheme before them. Also they would wonder. Why should he, an airman, whose dealings were with winds and machinery, and the future, suddenly turn so far out of his lines as to become a merchant of bygones? 

Why didn't he take an interest in an aeroplane factory?

Then there was the question of time. Everybody knew, Rosalind knew, that he hoped and expected to be back at his own job at the end of a year. But nobody knew, least of all Rosalind, what was the other hope he hugged to his heart.

By the time he was standing in the hall, among the cool, hard, modern young people of the frescoes, he had almost decided to hold his tongue. 

Jove! How he hated those frescoes! It was only the third time he had been in the house, and yet he had a personal grudge against the fellow in the plus-fours addressing a golf ball beside the dining-room door.

Very amusing, very modern-but they ought to be in a tube station, not here. 

And then he had a sudden lovely glimpse of Primula, wrapped in dark furs, rushing out to a waiting car. She stopped dead on seeing him, and looked quite stupefied with surprise. "I-I didn't know you were coming," she said naïvely. "Your mother asked me yesterday," said Sholto Scott, gazing admiringly at her. Really, she was worth going ten miles to see! "I phoned to know if I could speak to Miss Forey on business. 

About some old furniture," he added. Difficult to put his wavering purpose into words! Difficult also to understand why he should feel an obscure need to apologise and explain! "Oh," said Primula, her face relaxing. "Mother forgot to tell me. Are you thinking of buying some antiques?" 

What a pity she couldn't tell him that there were plenty at Havisham! "Well-that depends on Miss Forey," he replied cautiously. "Why do you always come when I'm away?” asked 

Primula, with a charming pout. "But, heavens, I must fly! I'm keeping a royalty waiting!" 

And she left him with a strange and wistful glance from her grey eyes which would have set any other man

to wondering. But Sholto-Scott, a moment later, was looking into the blue eyes of Rosalind, eyes which were not at all strange and wistful-but brimming over with laughter.

She had been teasing Professor Bancroft, who was fond of declaring himself a woman-hater. For she was in a gay mood. Somehow or other, in these days of spring, she woke in the mornings feeling like one who is emerging; emerging from some long dark oppression of the spirit, she didn't know what. Perhaps it was that Sussex was so lovely just now; she had been in a wood of bluebells before breakfast. Or perhaps it was merely that the catalogue was finished and sent to the printers.

Whatever the reason, she was conscious of a pleasant emptiness, a readiness to take whatever should come to her in the way of adventure. She was thirty-sevenyoung enough, and old enough. Young enough not to have lost all zest, and old enough to be released from the petrifying apprehensions of youth, and to have done with the agonies of Love. She had had Love, the very best of it; she still had it. But she was free from its penalties now for ever. 

So thought Rosalind; and welcomed her airmanor Primula's—with real pleasure. How handsome he looked to-day! She did like the bold outline of his profile, and his straight black brows! He seemed very nervous, though: shook hands quickly, and turned

away almost abruptly to greet Nicolette Nevill, who was the other member of this rather incongruous luncheon party. "No really intelligent woman is ever really smart,” resumed the Professor, cocking a truculent bright eye at Rosalind. "What about intelligent men?" she retorted. "You're rather a dandy yourself, Professor. Are we to conclude "Oh, I'm fearfully sorry," cried Vivien, bursting into the room at this point. “I'm always the last to arrive in my own house. I get so absorbed in things, and forget . . . Oh, Nicolette, how nice to see you! 

And Captain Sholto-Scott-"9

She shook hands with her guests. Her eyes were absent, there was a still perceptible inkstain on her finger, and her sister knew that in spirit she was only half-detached from the world of Sarah Sorel. 

After lunch, Professor Bancroft went away early, for he had to lecture at three o'clock, and Vivien carried off Nicolette Nevill to see her panelled sittingroom. Sholto-Scott was left alone with Rosalind, still bright-eyed and belligerent from her encounters with the Professor. Sholto-Scott felt a little jealous of the 

Professor, in spite of his sixty years. He wondered if he often came to the house.

He was in a complete turmoil. He didn't know whether his plan was a brilliant inspiration or the

scheme of a fool. When he looked at Rosalind, so adorable in her thin black frock, he knew that he loved her infinitely more than he had ever supposed himself capable of loving, and the thought that he might never win her gave him a queer, dreadful pang like the moment before a crash. "Well," she said, dropping into one of the toad arm-chairs, "I hear you're thinking of buying Chippendale or something. Vivi said you wanted to ask me about it. I feel quite frightened."

Something in her smiling, half-mischievous glance made him think of the hot afternoon on the cliff, the long fair pigtail and the white goats. Flip had been a sport. He took courage and made up his mind. "Not precisely buying them," he said. "Er-you told me once you'd like to keep a shop." "So I would. I'd adore it." "Well-will you? . I mean-will you join me as a partner, I'm thinking of starting an antique shop." 

She stared at him in wide-eyed bewilderment. "You-but you're a great-a famous airman." "Out of a job," he reminded her. "What about your book?" "I can work at that in the evenings. It's more than half finished, anyhow. You see, it's only a handbook." "Whatever put the idea into your head?" "Something that happened yesterday." And then

he told her about his visit to Duke's Head Place, and James Throckmorton's antique shop. She listened with breathless attention. "It seemed to me an ideal place," he finished up. "Or at least it could be made so. It was a good business once, and not so very long ago, and why shouldn't it be again? But, of course, it would be nothing without you.' 99 "It would be the most tremendous fun," she said slowly, her eyes sparkling.

Sholto-Scott had a moment's misgiving at his success. "It would be a great tie," he said. "It would take a lot of your time." "But I'm used to that. I haven't been a lady of leisure, you know." "You must see the place first. Perhaps you won't think anything of it." "When?" asked Rosalind practically. "Could you come now?" 

She jumped up. "Give me ten minutes," she said, and ran out of the room. 

Sholto-Scott, left alone, stared unseeingly at the blue, green, and silver spattered walls, and the squat, oddly shaped furniture of the small drawing-room. 

He was astonished-and rather frightened-at the way she had jumped at his suggestion. He felt rather like an insinuating scoundrel. His heart beast fastand yet he knew that Rosalind so far regarded him

only as the sharer in a jolly adventure. Well-let him have his chance! The rest would come-should come!

At the end of ten minutes she was down again, in her favourite dark blue tailor-made, with a small fluffy hat of blue chenille pulled down over her fair locks. Sholto- 

Scott glanced at her, then glanced quickly away with slight frown. "He can look very severe," just passed through Rosalind's mind. "I've told Vivi and Miss Nevill that we're going out," she said. "We'd better take a taxi in the square." “We shall have to be terribly business-like about this," pursued Rosalind, when they were seated in the taxi. "Equal partners, equal shares. How much do you think we ought each to put in?" "Well—” said he slowly. "Considering that you would supply all the expert knowledge-" "And considering-" she broke in, "that you would supply all the advertisement value-our shares ought to be equal.. ... If they're not, I won't play." "Well, then, suppose we put in a thousand each. 

There'll be rent and rates, heating and lighting and alterations, and new stock-I don't think the old is worth much. But you're the best judge of that." "All right,” she replied. "We must get someone to value it. And we shall have to have a book-keeper-I can't add up columns. And I'll go down to Pineholt, where Prim's cottage is—there's a sale at Pan-

ton's, a house there. We're having the most extraordiluck! It seems as if we were meant to do this. And 

I know where I can get a set of—” nary 

She went into a little trance of calculation. "One thing we haven't thought of," remarked

Sholto-Scott after a pause. "Oh, what?" cried Rosalind. "Suppose Mrs. Throckmorton doesn't want to give up the business." 

She gazed at him with a stricken look. "Then we shall have to murder her," she said quietly; and suddenly the two of them burst out laughing. UPP WHILE Sholto-Scott was upstairs, paying his visit to young Pitt, Rosalind wandered about the shop, looking at the antiques with a critical and appraising eye. 

Lots of rubbish-or at least rubbish for them: the marble bust of an unknown Victorian gentleman, in beard, frock-coat and Gladstone collar: the red glass bowl full of old false teeth, the terribly ornate whatnot, once the pride of somebody's heart. "It took her half an hour to dust," reflected Rosalind. On the other hand, there were one or two things- "Would you like to sit down, miss?" came the voice of Mrs. Throckmorton. "Because you're welcome to

come into the parlour. Pitt's got talking to the Captain about those aerioplanes, I shouldn't wonder. It's a treat for the boy, and he forgets himself."

Rosalind accepted, and sat for a time in the ugly comfort of the parlour, while Mrs. Throckmorton entered upon a rambling account of her husband's triumphs. Rosalind listened eagerly, and asked many questions, her thoughts wandering now and then to the famous Cuffe Collections housed in their beautiful and exquisitely kept rooms. How different! And yet there was a bond between Sir Benjamin, the famous connoisseur, and this obscure James Throckmorton-the passion of the born collector. "We've got the bookshop next door as well," pursued Mrs. Throckmorton. "James would have it." "Oh, have you? May I see it? I love rummaging among books."

She felt a little tired of sitting still, and the room was too warm. She wished that Sholto-Scott would come-she had thousands of things to say to him. "Certainly, miss, if you'd like, but you'll have to come round by the back," said Mrs. Throckmorton. "I keep the front locked up."

There were several hundreds of books on the shelves and floor of the stuffy shop, but after touching one of them with the tip of a dainty finger, Rosalind abandoned the idea of rummaging. Everything was covered with a furry grey mantle of dust.

"I'm afraid they're in a terrible state, miss," said 

Mrs. Throckmorton. "But I've really no time to dust them. That lot there"-she pointed to some bundles of books and magazines which lay in a pile in one of the corners-"they came in the week my poor husband was taken to the hospital. He never had time to look at them. They've never even been untied." 

Rosalind looked at them with the little thrill of speculation she always experienced in an old bookshop. 

What treasures might there not be in that grimy pile?

She knew a man who had found a letter from Charles Dickens in the pages of a "Tour in Palestine." 

Just then they heard the voice of Sholto-Scott in the next shop, and Mrs. Throckmorton bustled her back through scullery and back-yard to receive him, and listen respectfully to his talk about Lady Taverner's convalescent home. Rosalind, in the meantime, went outside to inspect the front of the shop. "It's perfect," she burst forth to Sholto-Scott, when they were walking along Douro Street towards the square. "Rather small, of course, but I've been thinking it out. We could use the two shops as showrooms, and have a door made between them. . . . We can't keep on the books. I'd like to look through them first, though, but not in these clothes. . . . Then we'd want the parlour behind for an office, and two or three of the bedrooms for stockrooms, when we've collected some new things. . . . Mrs. Throckmorton would

have the other house upstairs to live in-if she wanted to. 

What colour do you think would be effective for the woodwork? Something striking. Don't you think a good peacock blue?" "Yes-if Mrs. Throckmorton consents." "Haven't you asked her yet?" “No-I—” "Then go back, go back at once and fix it up. Somebody else may get the same idea, and come and buy it in the night. Oh, I couldn't bear to have my little shop stolen from me now.' 99 "Some day," reflected Sholto-Scott, "it will be our little shop." "You're not to decide anything," he said firmly, "before consulting your friends. Otherwise, they'll take me for a shady adventurer after your pennies.' 99 She glanced at him with a hint of Primula's pout. "You are an adventurer," she said. "That's quite obvious. But no one will think you shady. Still-have it your own way! I'll consult Vivien, and phone to you the minute afterwards, and you can go before breakfast to-morrow morning and settle with Mrs. Throckmorton. "Suppose she won't listen to me?" “Oh but she will-if you look at her as you looked at me just now!"

Vivien was sitting rather disconsolately at her writing-desk with innumerable scraps of paper before her. 

On each was scribbled a sentence, a phrase, a word or two, caught before it fled for ever. It was her task to weave them into the scene where Sarah Sorel, coming back from the ball with her lover, finds the door shut against her. She had to catch in a net of words the visions that floated so bright and clear before her eyes.

She had been reading Orlando, and she felt discouraged. "I'm nothing but a Laura K. Mull," she said to herself. 

Nicolette Nevill had only just gone; she would have been there still but for a blessed cocktail party at the 

Duchess of Bamborough's; Babsie Bamborough, Nicolette called her. Rather to Vivien's embarrassment, the young actress, newest of the stars, had conceived a schoolgirl passion for her, and by now she knew rather more about Nicolette's affairs, conjugal, financial, and professional, than the girl's own mother.

Into this preoccupation burst Rosalind, without a word of apology. She had her own preoccupation. 

Looking at her, Vivien caught a glimpse of Flip, when captured by a new enthusiasm. "She's accepted him!" flashed into her mind in the irrational moment that comes before thought. "No! It's impossible." "What is it?" she asked aloud. "Vivien," said Rosalind solemnly. "I'm going to become a Merchant Princess." And then she burst out

laughing at her sister's astonished face. "No, seriously-" she went on. "Listen to me." And she gave a little acount of her afternoon's adventures. 

Vivien listened with immense inward surprise, but with outward calm. "Clever man!" she said to herself.

She felt sure, now, that Rosalind was going to have the chance of a new sort of happiness if she liked to take it. 

Sholto-Scott was going to rescue her from the pots, as 

St. George rescued the princess from the dragon. She rejoiced. Only-perhaps it was her duty, as the elder sister, to say something sensible about financial risks?... But no! If the scheme should turn out a failure, and Flip did lose her money, it would be worth while. She wouldn't be penniless; her father had left her a thousand pounds, and she would have Sir Benjamin's four thousand besides. "I think I should venture," she said. "It would be very interesting, and there's quite a chance that it would pay. Look at Mrs. Locke and she doesn't know nearly so much as you. The only thing is-" she stopped. "What?"

Vivien played with the pencil that lay on the desk in front of her. "Darling, would you like being with

Captain Sholto-Scott as much as you would have to be, if this plan comes off? You know how-fastidious you are. You won't even dine and dance with a man if you think you are at all likely to be bored."

"You're thinking of Arthur Peyton," said Rosalind, with a laugh. "Now this is totally different. It's business, not pleasure. I can put up with the most boring of bores as long as I'm not supposed to be enjoying myself in his delightful company. And I shouldn't call

Captain Sholto-Scott a bore even about flying! . 

Besides, I like him. Of course, all men seem to me sort of-well, second-class, after Dick. . . . I can't help it, Vivi, for me they simply are, and that's that! But that doesn't matter. I believe we shall get on splendidly as business partners. I shall look upon him simply as a partner, not a man." "I wonder how long he will let you!" was Vivien's unspoken comment. "Besides," went on Rosalind, "look at the difference in our ages! I'm very nearly as old as the hills-" "You're not," interrupted her sister indignantly. "Will you please remember that I'm seven years older than you?" "We both are," said Rosalind, "in the eyes of Poppy and Primula, who think life ends at thirty. . . . Yes, they do I did myself when I was eighteen, and here I am, seven years beyond the span." "What does it matter what the young things think?" said Vivien. "It matters to people like Captain Sholto-Scott." "I hope it doesn't-too much," said Vivien. "Because I'm afraid Prim's going to accept Prince Dmitri

after all. Everybody asks her to meet him now. He was at Violet Paddington's dance last night. 

And yet the child doesn't seem happy somehow. I'm very worried about her, Flip, but she snubs me flat if I attempt to say a word." 

THERE was no need to murder Mrs. Throckmorton, for the old lady jumped at the prospect of being relieved of her responsibilities. In fact, she told them mysteriously, they were only just in time; she had been "approached." 

In spite of her sentimental longings for a little business in her native village, she was very willing to accept the suggestion made by Sholto-Scott and Rosalind that she should occupy the first and second floors of No. 3, keeping on the two young men who were now lodging with her at No. 4. Frank Pitt had been sent off to the Convalescent Home at Broadstairs in which Lady Taverner took an interest. "I've made inquiries about those two lodgers," said Sholto-Scott to his partner. "One's a young policeman, and the other is second chauffeur to the Earl of Bootle, who lives in the square. Nothing could be better. We can leave the shop now with an easy mind

if we know that those two hefty young chaps are sleeping on the premises."

So they bribed Mrs. Throckmorton with offers of a separate entrance, and a wonderful kitchen upstairs with a new sink and gas stove, modern improvements which she regarded at first with a sort of terror. 

They spent a busy month of May while the London season was raging all round them, Sholto-Scott in superintending painters and carpenters, and Rosalind in collecting merchandise. They had given a generous sum for the dusty remains of James Throckmorton's stock, and Rosalind had insisted also on buying the contents of the second-hand bookshop, which Sholto- Scott had thought of despatching to Charing Cross 

Road. 

"We shall get the money back," she said. “I'm sure of it. I've got a presentiment." "Have it your own way," he said, looking at her contentedly. They were standing in the little workshop into which the "antiques" had been removed, and Rosalind, in a flowered overall with a handkerchief tied over her fair head, had been sorting them out, examining each object with minute and critical attention. "There's a lot for the dust-bin," she remarked. "But those on that side are not too bad. That's a beautiful little walnut bureau, the one with marquetry and a toilet mirror. Luckily, Mrs. Throckmorton hid it away

at the back of the shop-she's the world's worst saleswoman!—otherwise somebody would certainly have snapped it up for about two and sixpence. . . . And there are some odd chairs, one or two Chippendale and 

Hepplewhite, I believe, and some doubtful ones. . . .

And there's quite a lot of Victorian stuff; it's fashionable now. Don't you love this tea-tray with the picture of Windsor Castle on it?" "You must teach me all about them," said Sholto Scott, and Rosalind gave him a little lecture on the spot about the dove-tailing of drawers, to which he listened with respectful attention. "We'll have to get Harrison to come and tidy up these things," she said thoughtfully, when this was finished. "He's an expert. He used to do that sort of job for Sir Benjamin. They'll look quite different when he's cleaned them up. I hope we'll be able to give him lots of work. . . . Now where shall we put these breakables? These candelabra with the lustres are really good, and I believe that tea-pot's a Spode. I'm going to take these spoons home with me if you don't mind." "Do whatever you like," replied Sholto-Scott. "As long as you sit down now and then. You've been on feet the whole afternoon. But I have observed that women have no sense!" your

He put his arm round her and gently pulled her down on to the bench. This was the first time he had

ever touched her except in dancing, and he waited the effect of this audacity with a certain apprehension, for 

Rosalind was something of a Sainte Nitouche. But she sank down with a little sigh of satisfaction. "It's a wonder that you ventured into business with me," she remarked. "Oh, I am so happy! I'm sure it will all fly away in the night."

And Sholto-Scott, living through those moments again, over his lonely last pipe of the day, was not quite sure sometimes that she had not leaned back ever so lightly against his arm.

She went down to Pineholt to attend the sale at Pantons, a solid mansion of the 'sixties in which two elderly ladies, the Misses Buckland, had drifted through the whole of their placid lives. She walked through the big ornate rooms, and amid a welter of handsome Victorian commonplaces she picked out a mirror from the cook's bedroom, a backgammon table, which had held hot-water cans in the bathroom for the past thirty years, three chairs from the kitchen, and two grimy little rugs from the top landing. "Persian," reflected Rosalind, as she looked at these. "They only want cleaning." And at the sale she acquired them, together with a bread-tin, for fifteen shillings. She bought the other things, too, and returned to London very well satisfied with her booty and the price she had paid for it. "I positively enjoyed that auction," she said to her-

self in the train, leaning back and closing her eyes. "Nobody spotted that mirror, and no wonder! It looked dreadful, but it's a little beauty. How nice it is to know something about things! . . . I believe I'm catching the passion of the chase. It wasn't the same thing with Sir Benjamin; he was too rich. He could buy what he liked. . . . I must tell Lady Cuffe about this."

Lady Cuffe was one of those people who did not quite approve of Rosalind's new adventure. Being seventy-one herself, she did not consider thirty-seven an advanced age, and she had some Victorian qualms about the propriety of this partnership with a man under fifty. Besides, she wanted Rosalind to come and live with her. 

Rosalind found her, dressed in grey silk and Honiton lappets, in a drawing-room where Horace Walpole would have felt at home. She was reading Aldous 

Huxley, in the same spirit with which she would have faced a dose of quinine. "One must live in one's own times," she used to say. She laid down Those Barren 

Leaves with an air of relief, however, when Rosalind came in, and listened attentively to a somewhat enthusiastic account of her discoveries. "Yes, my dear," she remarked at the end of it. “I am sure that the mirror is charming, and the chairs are 

Chippendale. You are a very clever young woman, and

Sir Benjamin always used to say that your judgment

was exceptionally sound. Besides, you have had the advantage of being trained by a great connoisseur. 

But I still think that you would be happier with me than with that young man." "But I'm not going to be happy with any young man," cried Rosalind, half laughing, half impatient. "It's the shop I'm going to be happy with. The young man's a pure accident. Only, as it was his idea, and he found it, I could hardly push him out, could I? It wouldn't be sporting. And it's too late now-we've signed all sort of papers." "I do not wish you to do anything dishonourable, my dear, but I think you have been rash. However, I want to do something to help you, if I can, so if you will come into the boudoir "

The old lady rose, reached for her stick, and swept her billowy skirt of grey silk over the Persian rugs, while Rosalind followed her, wondering. In the little boudoir there stood a beautiful Queen Anne settee with two arm-chairs upholstered in tapestry, probably from some back bedroom, for the whole house was full of treasures. "I hope you will accept these, with my love. They are not among those which Sir Benjamin selected for the Victoria and Albert Museum, but they are goodmy husband bought them-and the needlework is pretty. Scenes from the life of Moses." "Pretty? They're adorable!" gasped Rosalind.

"They're fit for any museum. Oh, Lady Cuffe, you are too kind to me.” 

And catching the stately old lady in her arms, she kissed her heartily on both cheeks. "My dear," said Lady Cuffe, a little taken aback by this impetuosity, and rearranging her lappets. "It is very little. Sir Benjamin would have wished it, and you might have been my dear daughter. I only wish you were." 

Rosalind embraced her again.

She spent two days over the books, examining each one carefully, for she had a romantic expectation of finding something in one of them, a letter, or an autograph of some famous person, or even a bank-note. 

She found it difficult, too, to refrain from dipping into the more interesting ones. So the work did not progress very quickly. "Found our fortune yet?" asked Sholto-Scott, coming in at the end of the afternoon on the second day, and looking down at her as she sat on the floor. "No," said Rosalind, lifting a radiant face, all streaked with dust. "At least, I don't know yet. I haven't done these two bundles. I'm taking that lot home; there are some lovely Millais illustrations in those magazines. But when I've done these, you can pack the whole lot off to Charing Cross Road."

She was untying a bundle as she spoke, the books which had lain in the corner, untouched for eighteen months. She cast a rapid glance over them. "Children's books," she said. "From some schoolroom-Ministering Children, The Basket of Flowers, Alice in Goodness!" She was staring at the titlepage. "When was Alice published?” "I don't know. About 1860, wasn't it?" "This says 1865. We must take it to Mr. Sutcliffe at once. Perhaps this is our fortune. Wouldn't it be gorgeous if it turned out to be a first edition?"

PRIMULA was the other person who felt some dissatisfaction with the commercial enterprise of her hitherto unobtrusive aunt. At first, when Rosalind had told her about it, a fierce pang of jealousy had shot through her heart, a gust of primitive emotion unknown to her before. But that had quickly passed. 

You couldn't suspect Rosalind of any designs against your peace; she was too frank and matter-of-fact, too genuinely taken up with her Spodes and her Chippendales. Plainly, He-Primula had only one He-was no more to Rosalind than any Mr. Brown, Smith, or Robinson.

But what about Him? What did he feel about it? 

That was the heart of her torment. Why should he do such a queer thing as start an antique shop, if it wasn't because he wanted to be with Rosalind? "Don't do that," said Primula sharply to Sophie, who was stripping off one dainty garment after another, in order that her mistress might take a bath and be dressed again for a dinner and a ball. And Sophie, who had been handling her as carefully as if she were a new-born baby, wondered what the "monsieur" had done to offend. She knew there was a "monsieur." 

People who wait on Golden Girls learn to be almost clairvoyant in their reading of moods and caprices. 

The composing influence of hot water and rosescented bath salts worked their spell upon Primula, and she became easier in her mind. Silly of her! Why must she always be thinking that Love was the only motive power of human actions? It must be because she was in love herself. But even she could remember a dim and joyless period when she did things for other reasons than love and she supposed that that was still true of most people most of the time. They took medicine because they wanted to be cured, and they opened shops because they wanted to make money. 

Besides-Rosalind was very sweet, and quite nicelooking still, but she was thirty-seven after all. 

Primula was not so youthfully blind as to be unaware that there were women who kept their beauty and

fascination long after thirty. Look at Grandy! She was thirty-nine when she had that perfectly devastating affair with King Augustus-and that was not her last by any means. But then, Rosalind was not one of those women. She had had her romance in the twenties, and after that had very sensibly abandoned the lists of love, devoted herself to her job, and left the field to the young.

The young! Primula hugged herself in the delicious consciousness of youth and beauty. Then she dried herself with towels from a hot silver rail, and stood still like a child while Sophie dressed her in a few gossamer garments and finally in blue tulle.

She was having a perfectly gorgeous time. Even the strange new pain which gnawed at her heart sometimes couldn't entirely spoil it. When the pain was soothed for the moment, she walked the earth on tiptoe, feeling gloriously sure that life was going to bring her her heart's desire.

And even if it did not, the second best was so dazzling that in some of her moods it almost out-dazzled the best. This was her fourth season. In the others she had had plenty of admiration, but now she moved in an atmosphere of romance and expectation. She was conscious of a subdued stir of excited interest wherever she went. It was as if a royal crown was already faintly shining in her hair. 

She met Prince Dmitri everywhere. Her name was

little now quite openly mentioned with his, and a coy paragraph had even appeared in the Babbler. That was Fluffy Fitzjohn, she knew; she bought her hats with these intimate little paragraphs. And Prince

Dmitri was heir to his brother, the Grand Duke of Istria. And the Grand Duke had phlebitis, and two or three other things, besides drinking heavily. 

Mary Primula, Grand Duchess of Istria. . . . 

As for Him, Sholto-Scott, she hardly ever saw him. 

It was a tiresome arrangement of things that all the interesting people had work to do, and were not available at any hour for the luckier ones to play with. She had racked her brains to think of ways of meeting him, and he had been to Caroline Square two or three times; but on these occasions she had had the experience, novel to her and intensely disagreeable, of feeling shy and stupid. And he did not seek her out. How maddening to think that perhaps her millions were keeping him away! Primula clenched her little teeth when the horrid thought intruded. Sometimes she was almost in a mood to throw away her money, and go to him with nothing but—well, about a thousand a year—and tell him, in the frank modern manner, that she loved him.

One point about this shop scheme she had instantly seized upon. It would make a link between him and herself. Rosalind had asked her to tell her friends about it—well, she would! She would bring someone

there nearly every day. She would see him, and make him understand that she didn't care really for anything but love.

But what about Mary Primula, Grand Duchess of Istria?

It was in this whirl of conflicting desires that 

Primula went off to dine at her grandfather's house.

Rather to her surprise, Sir Everard and Lady Mary were alone, or almost alone-you could hardly count old Mr. Curtis of the Bank. Grandy had written such a pressing little note that Primula had expected to find half a dozen of her own pals there, who would go on afterwards, like herself, to the Duchess of Bloomsbury's ball.

But as the stately meal proceeded on its unhurried course, Primula began to suspect that this was a plot of Grandy's; she must want to get her alone to talk about something. And then the gay evening in front of her began to seem portentous. Was something going to happen which would be what people called a turning point in her life? Primula's heart quailed. She wasn't ready for turning points. She wasn't at all sure yet what she wanted, and what she was willing to give up. You had to give up something, it seemed, even if you were a Golden Girl.

Across the lace and crimson roses of the small round dinner table, she watched Grandy, playing the siren to old Mr. Curtis, and with complete success. For Mr.

Curtis, although an authority on International Exchange, was gazing at her like an enamoured cod-fish. 

And no wonder! Grandy was wearing the Staniforth rubies with her white satin gown, and looked magnificent. There was nothing portentous about her. She made light malicious chat about a recent Cabinet scandal in a voice that managed to be mocking and caressing at the same time.

It was when they were alone with their coffee in the little Chinese room that the real attack began. Not immediately-Lady Mary made a very critical inspection first of Primula's blue tulle frock. Vivien had been rather uneasy about that frock, which exposed a great deal of Primula's white skin. But Grandy approved of it. They stood in front of a lacquer mirror, and Primula had the queer sensation that Grandy was gloating over her bare shoulders and burnished hair. 

She did not know that it was partly Lady Molly Fitzhubert whom Lady Mary was gazing at so hungrily. 

Then Lady Mary rang the bell, and as if by magic, Charlotte, her maid, came in, smiling mysteriously, with a case in her hand, from which Lady Mary took a sapphire necklace, the sapphire necklace given her by

King Augustus, and fastened it round Primula's white throat. “Grandy!" gasped Primula, staring, fascinated, into the mirror.

It was perfect! It picked up the blue of her frock.

It sparkled with a thousand flashes of blue lightning. "I want you to have it," said Lady Mary, looking at her with approval. Charlotte was somehow discreetly not there. Then suddenly a mischievous little smile came over her face, and she tapped the girl's cheek with her finger. "Our little royal lady," said Grandy affectionately. "Oh, Grandy-do you think-" faltered Primula, quite in the Victorian manner which she so despised. 

But when one's mind was in such a whirl, one did not somehow remember to be modern. 

Her crisis seemed upon her like a breaking wave. 

Besides, Grandy could make one feel so terribly inexperienced. "I certainly do," said Lady Mary smiling. "He asked Anne Bloomsbury for an invitation to this ball -he was in Paris. Well, he'd come back specially, and he practically told her that he hoped for an opportunity of speaking to you to-night. You know, she was a lady-in-waiting to his mother. . . . Ah, Primula!

I should like to be twenty-one again, and in your shoes."

Primula smiled nervously. She saw, rather than felt, that she was a very enviable young woman. "Elizabethgrad is one of the most picturesque places I know," pursued Lady Mary, watching the changes on the downcast face. "Quite a little Paris.

The opera house is magnificent. I went there once with

your grandfather on a visit to Count Willrich. There was a national fête on at the time-St. Elizabeth's their patron saint-and I shall never forget the sight of the Grand-Duchess driving through the streets to the cathedral and back again to the palace, with the crowds all cheering, half of them in the most gorgeous embroidered peasant costumes. And we were presented to the Grand Duke and his wife at a garden party which they gave at Bellavista, the summer palacesmaller than Versailles, but more beautiful, I think.

But you can have no idea. It is all so different from your present simple way of life. · · 99 It was a surprise to Primula that her way of life could be regarded as simple. Intoxication came stealing over her. Lady Mary saw her eyes grow dreamy.

Streets full of people cheering Mary Primula, sentries presenting arms when she passed by, herself in the cathedral, young and lovely, with the ermine mantle dropping from her bare shoulders. . . . But Prince 

Dmitri had no place in these visions. "There will be duties, too," went on Lady Mary, only half heard. "The times are critical for royal houses to-day. But a woman who has charm, intelligence and courage can do wonders for her husband. 

And of course you will be expected to give him an heir. . . . Your babies will be christened in that wonderful cathedral-"

Primula gave her a little troubled smile; and then

she said in a very low voice, pleating and unpleating her tulle frills: "Grandy, does it matter very much if you're not in love with your husband?”

Lady Mary glanced at her sharply. That bourgeoise Vivien again. "No," she said deliberately. "For the Victorian woman, perhaps. But not for the woman of to-day, whose world is wider, freer in every way. . . . . Of course it is necessary to like him and respect him—just as I do your grandfather.”

Primula drew a long breath. This was the first time 

Grandy had ever spoken to her of her own intimate affairs. She was being treated like a woman at last! "And he must be able to surround you with the conditions in which you can fulfil yourself and be happy. Some women are made for small things—I wasn't. And I don't think you are, either. I have to have beauty and luxury and a place in the great world, which only a husband can give. Your grandfather could give me all those, and so we have been ideally happy together. . . . Prince Dmitri will be able to give you much more." "I don't think . . . I could do . . . without love," murmured Primula. "My dear child, you'd be a monster if you could! 

Love keeps a woman lovely. Of course you will have

love as much as you want. I have been loved all my life-no woman more. 

And I have given love, too." 

She said the last two words in a low dreamy voice. 

Her eyes were shining, seeing visions. She was, for the moment, extraordinarily young and beautiful. "She's thinking of King Augustus!" reflected

Primula with a thrill. She felt immensely flattered that 

Grandy should of her own accord refer to that scandalous brilliant episode, which Mother always refused to discuss. As a matter of fact, it was Roddy Sloane who had helped Lady Mary to produce that particular effect; but Primula had never heard of Roddy Sloane.

The wonderful light died out of Grandy's face, leaving her the practical, humorous woman of the world. "Your mother would be horrified if she could hear us talking like this," she said smiling. "But I can be frank with you, child. You are young. You belong to a generation which is freeing itself from the old conventions, and I have always been free. I was born before my time. I never could see any reason why a clever woman shouldn't eat her cake and have it too. 

Now are you going to make someone very happy, tonight, little Prim?” "I think so," murmured Primula.

DANCING was going on in the white ballroom at Wimpole House. Light from the crystal chandeliers fell softly on to the banks of red flowers, and on the gay moving pattern of black coat and shimmering skirt far below. There were Jean, and Bunny, and Poffles, and 

Moira, and the Iffleys-Poppy, fresh from Paris, with the happy airs and graces of the young matron. 

Prince Dmitri had not yet arrived, and Primula, with one eye on the door, made absent-minded replies to her partner's conversation. But an heiress is always sufficiently witty; and those who felt piqued by 

Primula's alternate yes and no, could always look at her; she had the charm of a Dresden china nymph, emerging from a blue wave. 

At last, in a little stir at the door, she perceived the man who was in all probability to be her husband; slim and pale, with smooth dark hair and inscrutable dark eyes. He looked terribly distinguished, she thought. She felt elated, but at the same time rather frightened. Would she see that-shut-up face first thing in the morning and last thing at night for the rest of her life? And was it possible that he would be the father of the little creature in the white fluffy coat?

She followed his movements with the tail of her eye,

as he swept his hostess, the elderly Duchess, into the dancing crowd. She was faintly amused to notice that 

Grandy, on the arm of a good-looking young guardsman, had managed to be the first person to catch his eye as he entered the room. Grandy was one of those people whose guardian angels see to it that they always find themselves in strategic positions. 

She saw the Prince kiss Grandy's hand with empressement. Then Grandy said something, probably witty, for the two men burst out laughing. Prim's conversation with Mr. Gore, the famous novelist, went all to pieces at this point, causing him to make some sardonic reflections upon the understanding of great heiresses.

After that, the Prince danced with Lady Jane and Lady Margaret Wimpole, the daughters of the house. 

Primula ceased to be aware of her own partner's existence. 

At last he came straight up to her, bowing and clicking his heels in the stiff military fashion she had so often laughed at. "Miss Primula-may I have the pleasure?"

His English was excellent-he had been at Etonbut there was about it a faint romantic suggestion of his foreign origin. "Delighted," murmured Primula, dropping her nonchalant little curtsey. Excitement began to stir within her. All these people, she knew, were watching

them, openly or furtively. She immediately saw herself playing a leading part on a far larger stage, with every eye fixed admiringly upon her. The thought was wine to Primula.

She turned upon her suitor such a radiant glance that his languid pulses began to beat a good deal faster. He was marrying because he had had very serious losses at the tables lately. He felt kindly towards the little heiress; he wanted her to have a good time. But he had not the faintest intention of allowing her to interfere with any of his former diversions. 

It was lucky for him that she was beautiful. She might have been large-boned and horse-faced like Lady Margaret. He would still have married her, but it would have been impossible to work up any of that ardour which, in Primula's case, was quite genuine as long as it lasted. She was delicious-if anything, prettier than Suzy Rip, the dancer. 

And she was obviously in love with him. He looked forward with something like relish to the moment of the proposal. But not so early in the evening! He would dance with her later on, and bestow upon her then the honour which she and all these peoplewere expecting. his eyes,

So though he gazed eloquently into her talk was of the Dragonfly. "You must fly over to Elizabethgrad some day," he said, and Primula perceived with thankfulness that she was to have a respite. She

recovered her usual gaiety, and talked flying, golf, and speedboats, as they danced, with the intoxicating sensation of being the most interesting person in the room. "You will dance with me again?" he said before leaving her. "I have something to say which issupremely important for me."

It was impossible to mistake his meaning, and 

Primula dropped her eyes and blushed like someone out of Thackeray. Her heart was beating fast with excitement.

What a shock, after that experienced suavity, to find oneself in the arms of George-George who contrived at the same time to bully and adore! "Oh, Prim, you look such an absolute little darling!" at one moment; and the next, "I'll knock that damned dago fellow's head off!"

She took no notice. She was attending to other matters, the wedding with the royal guests, the arrival in Elizabethgrad, the flags, the salutes, the crowds.

Suddenly George felt her supple body grow tense on his arm. He looked round, bristling with a lover's alertness, but it was nothing-only Sholto-Scott, the airman, coming in with Lord Henry Wimpole, the unsociable one, who went in for seaplane designing.

But for Primula, the wedding guests, the cheering crowds, the bridegroom himself, all vanished like

figures in a feverish dream when the strong sunlight floods into the room. Instantly she knew that she could never, never give herself to anyone but the tall young man who stood just inside the door, bending deferentially to talk to little fat Lady Guildenstern. 

How she loved his brown face, with the straight black brows, and the sudden smile which showed his white teeth. Sir Lancelot, she would have called him, she supposed, if she had been a sentimental Victorian girl. . . . But sometimes the sentimental Victorians did hit the nail on the head.

By instinct she knew that he had seen her, that he was following her with his eyes, and when her dance with George was over, she was hardly surprised to find him at her elbow. "How do you do, Miss Primula?" he said, looking into her eyes with his direct blue gaze. "I thought-you never went to balls now," was all she could manage. "I don't. I'm not really at this one. I'm only looking on. I've been dining with Lord Henry-we swatted flies together in Mespot, you know—and he made me come in for a moment to see the dazzling sight. . 

I'm off at once-unless you will give me the pleasure?”

Almost sombrely the Golden Girl gave herself up to the bliss which did not make her happy. To be so close, almost as close as in a lover's embrace, to see his face so near to hers-and then to hear him talking

cheerfully about his intolerable shop, and Miss Forey's cleverness, and her lucky find Still, it was bliss! She forgot the brilliant ballroom, the smart crowd, the Prince. She even forgot Grandy. 

And gradually, her young happy confidence in life came back to her. She could not believe that Prim 

Staniforth, who had everything in the world, would not in the end have this as well. 

The dance came to an end, and he went away, taking the brightness of the evening away with him. Lady 

Mary came in from one of the other reception rooms, where she had been engaged in prolonged reminiscence with General Sir Roderick Sloane. She gave the girl an affectionate significant little tap with her fan as she passed, and suddenly Primula was seized with panic.

She, Prim Staniforth, who wasn't afraid of anybody, was all at once afraid of Grandy. What would happen when she refused Prince Dmitri-for she knew now she couldn't possibly accept him? Grandy, the understanding, the caressing, who had never been angry with her before, loomed up in front of her like an iceberg, all glittering and formidable. Desperately, she cast about in her mind for what to do. "Sorry, Poffles," she said to Lord Pimlico, who presented himself at this moment. "But I've torn my frock. 'Mind waiting a minute or two while I get a stitch put in?”

"Right O!" said his lordship. "I'll be hanging about here."

She flew down the corridor which led to the cloakroom, and asked for her emine wrap. Then she fumbled in her little blue bag. "I'm not feeling very well," she said to the attendant maid. "No-don't get me anything—I'm going home.

Would you mind asking one of the servants to get me a taxi? And please let him do it quietly. I don't want to disturb my grandmother, Lady Mary Staniforth.”

And the woman who knew all about Lady Mary, nodded understandingly. 

Home she fled, like a scared bird, through the warm summer midnight, to Mother, the only person she knew who was not in the least afraid of a stand-up fight with Grandy. "THROCKMORTON'S," which was the new name of the shop in Duke's Head Place, received a great deal of useful publicity when the Alice in Wonderland was sold in a famous auction room for three thousand pounds. Sholto-Scott, who found himself possessed of a commercial instinct he had never suspected, made the most of it, giving interviews freely, so that Rosa-

and lind, going home at night, could choose between the yellow placard of the Evening Post, with "Famous

Airman Discovers First Edition," and the green white one of the Comet: "Romantic Find in Antique Shop." "One thousand goes to Mrs. Throckmorton, of course," said Rosalind. "She'd never have known anything about it but for us; but then, we'd never have known anything about it but for her. . . . And two thousand into the business. . . . Hurray! I'm going to the Trumpington sale next week."

Her cheeks were pink with excitement. "You look quite dangerous," remarked Sholto- 

Scott. "I believe you enjoy those sales." "Oh, I do! I'm perfectly happy at last. I believe

I was born to buy things for twopence and sell them for twopence ha'penny."

He thought it a good beginning that Rosalind was losing some of her old serenity. She was waking up, becoming younger and more impetuous, and sometimes also more self-willed and exasperating. Sholto- 

Scott was glad even of this. "Less like a damned sainted widow," he said to himself. "Sweet and calm and all that. Those kind old people have been spinning a sort of cocoon round her, of elderly peace and serenity. Even her grief's partly convention by now. It's time she left off thinking of a man who's been dead fourteen years, and began to see that there's a living

one at her elbow whom she can bless or the other thing, just as she chooses."

It seemed to him that the more the old Flip returned, as he had seen her in the Understrand garden before she ever met her lover, the better would be his chance now.

They did not spend so much time together as he had expected, Rosalind having devised a Box and Cox arrangement by which he had two or three mornings free every week while she went off in the afternoons to attend sales sometimes, or to prowl about the Caledonian Market. She insisted that he must not neglect his book and his newspaper articles, and arranged a desk for him in the room behind the old bookshop, to which he could retire when no customers were about. 

She persuaded Mrs. Throckmorton, who was not at all unwilling, to keep watch in the shop and to call him when any came. For she had no opinion of Mrs. 

Throckmorton's business ability.

Ivy Mason, the book-keeper and typist, reigned in the office, which had been the parlour. There was very little correspondence for her to do in those early days, and as Rosalind had perceived at once that she would never learn to distinguish Wedgwood from Woolworth's, she was able to do a great deal of crochet and read many novelettes. She harboured a harmless adoration for Sholto-Scott, which was shared by her "boy," who worked in a garage not far off. Ernest

sometimes came to fetch his lady-love, hanging about by the box trees outside the peacock-blue door of 

Throckmorton's, in the hope of catching a glimpse of his favourite "Speed King."

Miss Mason was a pleasant, rosy, snub-nosed damsel, who liked talking nearly as much as she liked munching "chocs." It was not long before Rosalind, Mrs. Throckmorton, Billy, the errand boy, and

Sholto-Scott himself knew all about Mum and Dad and Lenny and Kath, not to mention the mangled plot of the latest talkie to which Ernest had taken her. "But after all," reflected Rosalind, after listening to a detailed account of Naughty Husbands, “she is alone a good deal. And one can always escape into the shop." 

She was very proud of the shop. A wide doorway had been cut between No. 3 and No. 4, and they had had the two rooms decorated in different styles. The inner room had a delicate mock-panelling in old ivory; the outer one was white-washed over the plaster, and decorated with black strappings of wood. "This is where we'll keep the homely, everyday things," said Rosalind; "and the aristocrats, the beauties, can go in the other." 

And so, in the quaint, Tudor-looking place you found solid court cupboards, chairs from some farmhouse kitchen, old wooden cheese presses, pewter tankards and platters, and gay or faded samplers hang-

ing on the whitewashed walls. There were two brilliant 

Paisley shawls folded on top of a carved Bible box, and two or three japanned trays.

In the other room, Hepplewhite chairs and tables displayed their delicate shapes against the ivory panelling. Two carved and gilded mirrors and some embroidered pictures hung above the needle-work back of Lady Cuffe's settee, and Rosalind had arranged her porcelain figures, her enamels and sparkling Irish glass on shelves. "Aren't they pets?" she exclaimed, the night before the opening of the shop. She was wandering round and gloating over her treasures. "I don't think I shall have the heart to sell them. I shall just send the customers away-if there are any customers." "You needn't be afraid of that," remarked Vivien. "The first few weeks, at any rate. I don't suppose any little shop ever got so much advertisement as this one. 

I haven't been able to open a paper for days without seeing a paragraph about Throckmorton's." "That's because he won the Pinkerton Trophy," said Rosalind, nodding at Sholto-Scott. "Silly, isn't it? But I'm not afraid of to-morrow. Prim's going to get up early to be the first customer and bring us luck." 

The Golden Girl was on the doorstep at nine o'clock next morning, with her gold net bag in her hand, and a heart which seemed to be fluttering somewhere inside her throat, to such a depth of Victorian sensibility was

she reduced by love. But, she told herself, she was going to get over this idiotic foolishness because she was going to see much more of him now he was almost in the family.

As for being jealous of Rosalind-she glanced into a mirror and then at Rosalind-it was absurd! Poor 

Rosalind was nearly forty, and looked it. The faint lines, the threads of silver, which Sholto-Scott no longer saw, were quite, quite plain to Primula. And then, to Rosalind, he was only an ordinary man. She wasn't trying to attract him. 

She bought the elegant little Chippendale mirror in which she had seen her lovely face, and a silver spoon for luck. She wanted to buy more, but Rosalind refused to allow her.

She bought them from Sholto-Scott, with a keen sense of the absurdity of things as she counted out the rustling notes into his hand. She-who would have liked to give him all she had. "I hope I shall bring you luck,” she said, raising serious grey eyes to his smiling blue ones. "Though

I don't know why I should, particularly. I don't seem to bring myself any." 

These were strange words from the Golden Girl. "It's always luck to see you," he returned with an admiring glance, and he meant it quite literally.

Primula Staniforth was the prettiest girl in London-a pleasure and refreshment to the eye.

The prettiest girl was going faithfully through the routine of the season, and having what other girls, in vicarages and shops and suburban villas, would have described as a heavenly time, though she was in severe disgrace with Grandy and the Duchess of Bloomsbury. 

Mother had borne unruffled the first shock of

Grandy's wrath, and then there had been a short period of diplomatic overtures over the telephone from the Duchess to Primula in bed. Her Grace was willing to explain to the Prince that Miss Staniforth had been suddenly taken ill on the night of the ball, and to suggest-very delicately—that matters should go on from where they left off. But Primula flatly refused. "No, no, I can't," she cried hysterically, and the 

Duchess flounced away from the telephone thinking she would like to box the minx's ears.

So Primula saw no more of Lady Mary for the time, but went about with Aunt Violet, who was of a softer disposition than her mother. The Prince, shrugging his shoulders philosophically, went off to Cap d'Antibes, where Suzy Rip was sun-bathing. After all, a wife was a tin kettle tied to one's tail.

But though the storm had settled down to a surface of unruffled calm, Vivien could not help watching her daughter with anxiety. The girl was gay enough, but she was thinner, and when she was off her guard, she allowed herself to look unhappy. Why had shepractically-refused the Prince? Something had hap-

pened, but Vivien had no idea what it was, any more than Lady Mary. Was there another man, and could it be that he did not love her?

But Primula kept her mouth shut tight, like a little oyster.
