AUGUST was hot that year, and in the last week of it Vivien used to sit under an apple tree in the garden at 

Chimneys, Primula's cottage at Pineholt. Her red typewriter stood on the little table beside her. 

For months she had been living in another world than that of the big London house with its bright, shifting population of the young and lively; and during the last three weeks, when "Sarah Sorel" was approaching the crisis of her fate, her creator found her more actual, more alive than placid Mrs. Borden, or Jenny the maid, or Timothy Bland, the postman, who were the three real human beings with whom she had to do.

Every evening she dragged herself away from Bristol after Waterloo to gossip with Mrs. Borden about the royal family; and every morning at breakfast she had a little chat out of the window with Timothy about 

Pineholt affairs. But almost before their last words

had died away, they vanished from the earth, leaving her alone with "Sarah," and her lovers, "James Pitcairn" and "Anthony Swallow," and her evil genius, "Mrs. Varney."

She wrestled for hours with intractable portions of 

Sarah Sorel. She would sit, staring without seeing them at the divinely mingled blues of a row of delphiniums, or the moving network of sunlight on the brown waters of the pool. Perhaps what she really saw was Sarah stealing down the stairs in the early morning with her bundle, only to encounter Mrs. Varney at the foot. Or perhaps it was the fight between James 

Pitcairn and his rival behind St. Mary Redcliffe's parish church, and Anthony's knife flashing out. 

And then, when it seemed clear, when she had felt every sickening heart-beat of Sarah, as she set her foot upon the creaking stairs-then, she had to find the words which would bring the same dull terror to her reader.

Altogether, Vivien rejoiced and suffered so much with her heroine that Mrs. Borden was seriously concerned. She didn't like to see her mistress so brooding, so absent. She would eat her mushroom soufflé as if it were nothing but macaroni and cheese. And sometimes she would laugh to herself as she sat all alone, scribbling, scribbling, under the apple tree. 

And then she stayed up so late at night. When Mrs.

Borden and Jenny were snug in bed, and the owls were

hooting in the trees on the other side of the pool, they could hear the muffled tap-tap of Vivien's not quite silent typewriter.

Only two people were more real to Vivien at this period of her life than Sarah Sorel and Anthony Swallow, and these were her Primula and her Rosalind. 

She was far from happy about Primula. The child was under some strain, she felt sure. After playing hard to the very end of the season, she had collapsed, and been obliged to spend a week in bed. But she refused to respond to her mother's delicate invitation to confidence. "Oh, Mother!" she said, with a petulant fling. "You're so terribly Victorian. I suppose you think 

I'm pining away for some man. Well-I'm not! There was something sinister in the mayonnaise at Poppy's party-and that's absolutely all."

When Lady Mary heard of this break-down, she relented, and came to spend an hour with the fractious invalid. But Vivien gathered from her demeanour as she came out of the bedroom that Grandy's diplomatic probings had had no better success than her own. 

Nobody knew why Prim had refused Prince Dmitri. 

Lady Mary was too experienced a woman to go on crying over spilt milk. She knew that what you have to do is to fetch some more and pretend that the first was only skim milk. So now she remembered the tales about Suzy Rip, and the roulette, and decided that

Prim would be far happier as Marchioness of Lydney. 

Probably Istria would be a republic before long. 

For once, Vivien found herself sharing her motherin-law's hopes. "I'd like her to marry George," she confided to 

Rosalind. "He adores her, but he won't stand any nonsense from her, and that's the kind of man who ought to marry Primula. What first attracted me to

George was his biting Lady Augusta Trumpington once at Havisham. Lady Augusta was intolerable. I always wanted to bite her myself, only a baronet's daughter-in-law doesn't bite the aunt of a Prime Minister. But George did it, quite simply and naturally, when he considered she deserved it. . . . He was five and a half then, a very judicious person, and he never bit anybody else.' 99 Primula was now away on a long cruise in the 

Mediterranean in Lord Paddington's yacht, Silverwing; and there was a tacit expectation that Lord 

Lydney would join them somewhere later on. Vivien sent him silently her heart-felt good wishes. 

About Rosalind she felt happier. Throckmorton's had made quite a sensational start, owing to the friends of Sholto-Scott and of Primula, and it seemed likely to go on well. Better than that, though, was the visible fact that Rosalind seemed to be blooming into a second youth. She was gayer, more energetic, and perhaps more provocative—like Flip of old. Vivien had a hope

that she was emerging from the shadow of Dick's death. Waste of life, it seemed to her now, was one of the deadly sins.

Of course, it might be that Rosalind would not end up as the wife of Sholto-Scott, after all. There was no sign of love in the easy friendliness of her manner towards him. Still. Love has many ways of approach, and Rosalind was not likely to repeat the swift headlong surrender of her first young passion. At any rate, she would have the time and the opportunity, and if she refused it, she knew her own heart best. 

Now, with Rosalind in Cornwall for a fortnight, and Prim in the Aegean, Vivien felt that her two dearest were out of her hands. There remained Sarah Sorel; and the pile of typescript grew thicker day by day. 

Happy, happy August, all alone! The vicar was and she didn't know his substitute. The doctor's family had gone for their holidays. So had the Thompsons, three fields off, their next-door neighbours. away,

One Friday morning, the last of the month, she was writing in the garden in one of those rare and blissful moods when inspiration flows free. Thoughts were ready, words came to clothe them and images to adorn them; her fingers could hardly move fast enough.

Into the golden peace there came the hum of a car, the twentieth that morning. 

But those others had speeded past—she wished them well. This one-oh, heavens !—it was stopping outside

the gate. A new American horn, which suggested both murder and seasickness, lifted up its voice. Vivien started up, and crammed her papers into an attachécase, swearing under her breath like a trooper, as she said to herself.

Round the corner of the old mellow-walled cottage came a figure out of Bond Street: Nicolette Nevill in spotless white and emerald, of the fashion after next. "Oh, please, don't let me disturb you!" called out the pet of the stalls. "Just go on writing exactly as if I weren't here I'd hate to think I was interrupting. ... I felt I simply must have a breath of fresh air or die. London's so artificial, don't you think? . . . I'm in such terrible, terrible trouble, I want to talk to you about it."

Vivien installed her guest in a long cane chair, and found her something to drink. She wondered whether the Peke had been stolen, or could it be that the American manager was sticking out for a paltry five hundred pounds a week? She mechanically put away the typewriter. Sarah Sorel was by this time a thousand miles away. "It's Claud," explained Miss Nevill, in her best

Duse manner. "He's making me very unhappy. It's a wonder I can act at all. I'm so highly strung. But I've an exceptionally strong sense of duty, and it keeps me up. I felt I simply mustn't disappoint my darling public."

Claud, it came out, to Vivien's surprise, was an engineer. She had vaguely thought of him as a sort of professional husband. And now ambition was beginning to stir within him again. He had been offered an excellent post by a firm who were building a bridge in India, and he wanted to go and make a career for himself. "But I told him," went on Nicolette. "Darling,' I said, 'I am your career. I couldn't possibly spare you. 999 "I should let him go to India," said Vivien.

Miss Nevill opened lovely brown eyes of astonishment. "But I'd miss him terribly. He always fetches me from the theatre, and takes the dogs out, and he dances divinely. . . . Besides, he wouldn't make as much in a year as I do in a month." 99 "All the same," said Vivien, "I should let him go.' 

And so they talked "about it and about . . ." till it was time for Nicolette to return for the evening performance. And evermore they came out by the same door as in they went. 

§ 

"ARE you free on Sunday afternoon?" asked Sholto- 

Scott of Rosalind one day in early October. "Because, you are, we could have our walk.” if

Rosalind looked at him in surprise. He had never said a word about a walk since the evening at Borgia's. "I'll ask Prim," she said doubtfully. "I think the cottage" "Where is the cottage?" he interrupted. "In Sussex." "Very well. We'll go and walk in Kent." 

Rosalind laughed. "Why should we walk in Kent?” she asked. "Don't we see enough of one another every day?" "No. I don't know what you are like on a country walk. I want to discover whether you are—walkative or not." "But why?" she persisted. "Because I'm fond of exploration." 

And Rosalind knew that she would go for that walk, if not next Sunday, then the Sunday after, for Sholto- Scott always managed to get his own way. He had taken control of the business side of Throckmorton's: supervising the books, dealing with intoxicated porters and careless errand-boys, and talking seriously to 

Miss Mason on the subject of punctuation.

Once or twice, when a truculent customer had been haggling about prices with Rosalind, he had come suddenly out of the office, and she had been amused to find how quickly, at one look from his eyes, bluster had changed into half-jocular apology. "I'd like to have kicked him out," said Sholto-Scott,

regretfully, after one of these encounters. "Only my arm is not up to a scrap yet." "If you had," returned Rosalind, "he wouldn't have bought that great clumsy court cupboard which takes sup such a lot of room. I think he was afraid not to," she added, laughing. "But I did let him have it quite reasonably." Sholto-Scott gradually extended his benevolent tyranny over Rosalind herself. He always seemed to know when she was tired. He found out, without being told, that she had occasional severe headaches, and packed her off home at the first sign of one, without listening to her protests. Rosalind had never been looked after like this before, for during her long engagement, Dick had been away most of the time with his ship; and anyhow, in those days she was young and vigorous, and did not want it. 

At first she had found in Throckmorton's an absorbing interest into which she could throw the whole of her enthusiastic personality. But this early ardour had subsided a little with success. The shop was doing very well; people had come to know that Miss Forey had a flair both for what was excellent, and what was likely to be a rising investment. But there were stretches of unoccupied time. One couldn't always be thinking of bygones, and insensibly Rosalind was herself making discoveries in the hitherto unknown country called Fitzroy Sholto-Scott.

She had always thought it a pleasant country, but she now found that it had its surprises, its mysteries, even its terrors. She believed he was capable of quite impressive anger. She had caught glimpses of it. But she meant to keep away from that perilous ground. 

She didn't want any emotional upheavals; they interfered with business, and with one's precious and hardly won tranquillity. 

Sholto-Scott was also finding things out about his 

Rosalind. Keeping a shop with a woman, he reflected, wasn't a bed foreshadowing of matrimony. She was a woman of moods, now pensive, now gay. Sometimes he found her provoking, and even disagreeable, but he smiled to himself on these occasions, not dissatisfied. 

The worse she treated him, the more intimate they were becoming. A constant politeness and amiability might keep him at arm's length for ever. 

Besides, the sweetness of her sudden repentances would bring all the old enchantment back like a returning tide. He had always decided that he didn't want a temperamental wife, but now he knew that the alternation of dark and bright had more charm for him than any mild continuing glow. "To-morrow," said Sholto-Scott as he locked up the shop at lunch-time on Saturday morning. It was the second Saturday in October. “Ten-twenty from Victoria. I'll call for you at ten o'clock."

"No," said Rosalind at once. "I'll meet you at the station."

Suddenly she was aware that she didn't want to explain to Vivien and Primula that she was going to spend the whole of Sunday with her business partner, whom she saw every day of the week. "Oh, very well,” he replied, with a quick, amused glance. 

Rosalind caught it, and felt a faint annoyance. "I've half a mind not to turn up," she said to herself. "But then-I shouldn't dare to show my face at 

Throckmorton's on Monday."

By ten o'clock next day, she was in a different mood. 

It was a golden autumn morning, with a chill in the air, and Rosalind's spirits rose as she put on her neat tweeds, and twisted a knitted silk scarf round her throat. She felt happy in tweeds. 

After all, she need not have troubled to keep her expedition a secret. Primula had not returned from 

Havisham, where she had gone for the cubbing, and Vivien, who was always thinking of something else these days, did not even notice that she was going out. "Five minutes to spare," said Sholto-Scott as he helped her out of her taxi at Victoria. "Come along."

While he was hurrying her along the crowded platform, she had time to notice that somehow or other he

contrived not to look ridiculous in plus-fours. Most of the other men— And then, half-way down the train, he stopped before an empty compartment. "First class? What a spendthrift you are!" "Don't you ever travel first class?" 99 "Heavens, no! I'm not a Staniforth. I go third." "So do I," said he. "Except on great occasions. 

On my honeymoon, for instance, I shall go first." "I should have thought," remarked Rosalind, "you'd go flying off in your own Dragonfly.' "My arm won't be strong enough by then," said he.

She looked at him, a little mystified. She knew that his arm was mending fairly rapidly now, thanks to skilful massage and patient exercising. Mr. Cedric 

Sellars had great hopes of it. But somehow, she felt a slight awkwardness, and hardly knew how to continue the subject. "Where are you taking me?” she asked instead, leaning back on the plump, pale grey seat. "Petersgate Halt, near East Grinstead, first," he replied. "We'll lunch at East Grinstead. But after that we're going along unbeaten tracks. I'll lead you a dance. I have an ordnance survey map with me here." 

They had lunch in an old inn at East Grinstead, and then they went swinging along together through the golden afternoon; by footpaths over meadows; under

the yellowing branches in the woods; catching glimpses from a hill on the far blue distance; looking into the clear brown waters of some brook in a hollow. 

A little wind which sang of winter whipped the colour into her cheeks, and blew her fair locks about her brow. She stepped along lightly in her small thick shoes. Sholto-Scott decided to himself that Helen must have walked about Troy in tweeds and thick small shoes like these.

Sometimes they talked. Often they were silent. 

She was one of those women, he reflected with contentment, who can on occasion not say a word. 

Late in the afternoon, when the sun was sending level rays across the tree-tops, they came to a stile at the top of a sloping meadow. "That's our station," remarked Sholto-Scott, pointing down among the trees. "We've got threequarters of an hour. We can stop here for a few minutes, if you like."

Without replying, she sat down on the step of the stile, while he leaned against the top rail and looked down at her. They did not speak, but let the rich peace of the autumnal land flow into them. "Heaven!" murmured Rosalind at last. "Yes," he replied dreamily. "To be here with the woman one loved." 

Rosalind shot a startled glance at him. He had never come near to mentioning love before. He was

gazing now over the sun-bathed scene below them, and all at once she felt that it was true that there was some woman whom he loved. Who could she be? Some 

American girl, perhaps. He was very attractive, and charming girls spring up like flowers in the path of the brilliant and the successful. 

She hesitated. Was he trying to talk to her about this woman? "I-I wish you were," she said, trying to speak quite lightly. Then she gathered boldness. "Is she does she live this side of the Atlantic?” "She lives in London." Rosalind was rather taken aback by the definiteness of this reply. In London? So near? .. But after all, he had lots of friends in London who were quite unknown to her. "You aren't-engaged to her?" she continued, daring still further.

Sholto-Scott removed his eyes from her face. "I'm engaged to her all right," he replied. "But so far, she's not engaged to me." "Oh, I see," she murmured, quick conjectures racing through her mind. Prim-could it be Prim? Was he shy on asking her because she was so great an heiress? Ought she, Rosalind, to suggest very delicately? But then, it might not be Prim, after all! "Have courage," she said, smiling up at him. "I'm

sure she will be in the end. You know you always get your own way.' 99 "If I do," said Sholto-Scott, "you will be the first to know it.” "Thank you," said Rosalind. "And I hope you will be as happy as-as- What date is it to-day?" "October the tenth," said he, looking at her surprised, but with a slight teasing twinkle. Rosalind never knew the date.

She made a small, shocked sound of dismay, and all the gaiety and contentment seemed to slip from her. 

She got up from the step with a startled face, and began to walk quickly down the hill. "Forgotten something?" inquired Sholto-Scott, striding to catch her up. "Yes," she said briefly. They walked almost in silence to the station. In the train with an obvious effort she made a few polite little remarks, and Sholto- Scott, though greatly wondering, let her alone for the rest of the journey. "What on earth-?" he asked himself. He thought she had overtired herself, and he took her home to the door in a taxi. "Good-bye, and thank you," she said on the doorstep. "It's been a lovely day." 

Then she raised her periwinkle-blue eyes to his.

"I'm sorry to have been such a wet-blanket the last bit, but you see-October the tenth was the day Dick was killed."

And Sholto-Scott only just saved himself from catching her in his arms in front of Robinson. "First time for twelve years she's forgotten,” he mused, as he walked home with a brisk swinging step. "Poor little darling! No wonder she was shocked.

Still, it's time she began to see that she can't bury herself for ever in the grave of that poor chap out there. She's too-alive for that. At any rate, I bet she won't forget our little conversation by the stile.” 

§ 

ROSALIND rushed upstairs to her bedroom, snatched off her hat and coat, and sat down on the bed. She had just made a discovery which caused a painful upheaval of all her previous notions. To forget the anniversary of darling Dick's heroic death! To go out with another man! She must indeed be a different sort of woman from that she had supposed-lighter, less faithful. Or else it was that no love lasts for ever. But she hated that idea, she didn't believe it; she rejected it.

It was no excuse to say that she never knew what date it was. She should have had Dick so constantly

present in her mind that days before she would have felt the approach of the old disaster. . . . Butwhen she came to think of it-whole days went by now without any very vivid memory of him. Of course she could never really forget him, but there was no doubt that she had changed. Even the thought of his loss was no longer the anguish it had been. . . . Perhaps it was due to Throckmorton's. . . .

Saddened and humiliated by these reflections, she went downstairs to find someone to talk to. But 

Robinson told her that Mrs. Staniforth had gone out, and would not be in till late, and Primula had not come back.

Rosalind did not see her sister until she returned from Throckmorton's the next afternoon, and then she found her in the hall, in her outdoor things. "Oh, Rosalind!" said Vivien, all flushed and brighteyed. "I've sent it off." 

It? Oh, of course. Vivi's novel. "Look!" She fumbled in the pocket of her short astrakhan coat and held out a scrap of paper. "The registered receipt. Bulstrode and Finch. . . . of course, I know it'll only come galloping back." .. But,

Rosalind said the proper things. After all, she found she couldn't tell Vivien that she had spent Dick's sad anniversary in walking and talking and-laughing with Captain Sholto-Scott. 

Vivien went upstairs to dress, for she was going out

to a party to-night with Primula. It was a small dinner-dance given by Lady Paddington, who had sent a good-natured perfunctory invitation to her quiet little sister-in-law. Unexpectedly, Vivien had accepted it. Why not? Sarah Sorel was finished. She would go and watch her own lovely girl for once, and perhaps find out what (or who) was troubling her. 

As her partner at dinner was inspired to give her a detailed account of his position with regard to Tariff Reform, Vivien had leisure to watch the guests who sat around the six small tables in the panelled diningroom.

They were nearly all young-boys and girls-and known to her personally or by sight. And then something queer struck her. Why, all these young people, except George and Primula, and one or two others, were either newly married, or engaged, or about to be engaged! There was something bridal, too, about the decorations-white and silver. What was Violet Pad dington up to? Of course it might be in honour of 

Rosemary, Lord Paddington's daughter, who had just returned from a honeymoon, but Vivien suspected some intention to work up a favourable atmospherefor what?

Instinctively she sought among the chattering crowd for the couple who were nearest her heart. They were separated-Violet had been too tactful to put

them together-but George was placed where he would get a full view of Primula, laughing and flirting a little with the youth called Poffles. And though 

Vivien could not help smiling at the thunderous politeness of George's demeanour, she wished with all her heart that her daughter could see the young man with her own eyes; big and strong and tender, even if he did ruffle up his hair and practise a dreadful outspokenness. She even considered him handsomer, in his somewhat rough-hewn style, than the celebrated Larry Desmond who sat near him; not unlike a young Viking, thought Vivien. 

As for her own little enigma, the girl had never looked lovelier than now, in a flounced, pale, pink frock, a faint glow of summer tan still lingering on her neck and arms and shoulders-enough to break the heart of any young man.

After dinner she lost sight of them, being spirited away by Violet's expert guest-shuffling to a distant room, to make a fourth at bridge.

In the midst of her gaiety with Poffles, Primula, also, had not been unaware of George. She had seen very little of him the last few weeks. A long bout of scarlet fever, caught in one of his Boy's Clubs, had prevented him from joining the Silverwing. She knew that she was going to have trouble with him to-night -something in her blood always told her about

George-but she also knew the calmly kind answer she was going to give him.

She was not unaffected by the atmosphere; really, it was perfectly priceless the way Derek was looking at Jean, and Rosemary at Colin! But it was rather thrilling, too. Oh, if some day she should look across the room and meet his eyes fixed upon her like that!

George came up at last and slipped his arm round her waist without saying a word. He whirled her once right round the room without saying a word. This was so unlike him that Primula glanced up at his face. 

He was gazing straight before him. His mouth was set firm, there was an odd little air of purpose and triumph about him.

Half-way round the room again, and she knew why. 

A door opened out of it, leading by a short passage to one or two other rooms. Before she could resist, she was being swept along the passage by George's steelstrong arm, and brought up, still in his embrace, in a small apartment which contained a desk and a telephone. Without letting her go, he contrived to shut the door and set his back against it. "Oh, George!" said Primula impatiently. "Don't be so silly. Open the door at once." 

In answer, George caught her to him, and before she could stop him he had kissed the mouth she was keeping for the only man; he went on to kiss her cheeks, her

nose, her eyes, and when Primula managed to bury her face in his coat-sleeve, he kissed the back of her head, and her ear. 

Primula wriggled and twisted until he was reduced to kissing the pretty white back revealed by the pale pink frock. 

It was shameful! George must have taken leave of his senses. Primula was filled with wrath. But underneath-could it be that through her just anger there ran a thread of something like pleasure? The suspicion made her more furious still. She wrenched herself free, and faced him, crimson-cheeked and panting. "George-you beast!" she cried. "I'll never forgive you. Open the door at once!" "Not till you've heard what I've got to say," said he, and there was a note of resolution in his voice which secretly impressed her. This was going to be more exciting than the usual commonplace proposal she knew so well. "I've heard it so often," she complained. But she no longer glared at him. "Not as you're going to hear it now," said George, putting his arms round her again. “It's no use wriggling-you can't get away from me. . . . No, I'm not hurting you. 

Prim, darling 

I love you more than any woman ever was loved before. adore you. I've been mad about you for years. ...

I've never looked at any other girl in the whole course of my life. . . . Oh, my darling-lovely-" "Don't, George," said Primula faintly. His intensity was exhausting. 

He checked himself. "Prim, darling, won't youmarry me? I know I haven't much to offer you. I've only got about twopence after keeping Ebbsworth going, and I know you're a great heiress. But this I can say. . . . I don't care a damn for your money. 

You can chuck it into the sea as far as I'm concerned. ... I only want you-your little, lovely self." "But I don't love you," murmured Primula. If only it had been another man saying those words! "Sweetheart, it will come," went on the eager lover. "Marry me, and you won't be able to help it. I'll love you so much, you'll have to love me back. Once we were married-darling—"

Primula disengaged herself from his arms. It was time to get this scene over, for she had a nervous consciousness that the dance must be finished. She didn't want to be found here with George. Everybody would laugh at them. "I'm terribly sorry, George, but I don't see how I can help it. Do please try to get over it. 

Let's go back to the ballroom at once." · · But George didn't stir. He kept his broad back obstinately against the door. "I'll never give you up," he said.

Primula decided to let him have it. "I'm afraid you'll have to, George," she said gently. -there's someone else.' "You see- 99 

And she looked at him with eyes so full of shining happiness, of pride and joy, that the young man recognised the truth. 

She saw him turn rather pale under his sunburn, and her hard young heart felt a pang of pity. Perhaps -other people could suffer just like Prim Staniforth! 

He turned and opened the door, letting in the blatant whine of the next foxtrot, "Breaking My Heart for You," and they walked in silence down the passage. 

Aunt Violet, keeping an anxious eye on the door, saw their return to the ballroom from which they had been absent a satisfactorily long time. Impossible to tell what had happened; but just before an offended partner claimed Primula, Lord Lydney said a word in her ear to which the girl responded with a tremulous smile. Aunt Violet had hopes.

She would have been less satisfied if she could have heard what he said. "Good luck to you, Prim darling," whispered George.

ABOUT midnight, when Vivien, wrapped in velvet, came downstairs to her car, she found George hanging

about at the foot of the wide staircase. He was in his overcoat, and appeared to be waiting for her. "I'm seeing you home, Aunt Vivi,” he explained.

This was the name he had bestowed upon her at Havisham. "But aren't you going on with the others to the cabaret at Folly's?" "No!" replied George, with such concentrated scorn that Vivien, glancing at his set young face, said no more.

Robinson was waiting up for her. In the midst of

her perplexity Vivien couldn't help wondering with amusement what would have been the emotions of the model parlour-maid if she could have seen her sedate little mistress three minutes before. But she went to bed thinking of George, all alone in his dreary bachelor flat.

Next morning, before Lady Mary had taken one sip of her orange juice, or one bite of her toast, or opened the most intriguing of her letters, she picked up the receiver of her bedside telephone and rang up her daughter.

The conference that followed left her frowning and thoughtful, but she proceeded without haste to the daily routine of breakfast, baths, massage, exercises, dressing, and making-up, which nothing was ever allowed to interfere with. So it was past twelve o'clock when a magnificent silver-haired lady in pale grey squirrel confronted her wisp of a daughter-in-law. 

She noticed that Vivien was not made-up, and looked as if she hadn't slept. "I'm sorry, Lady Mary," said Vivien, "but Prim's not in. She went off early this morning to play golf. 

I haven't seen her since last night." "Then you don't know-she hasn't told you that she has refused George?" "No," replied Vivien. "She told me nothing. I left her dancing. . . . I'm very sorry."

She knew that her tone was flat and unconvincing,

but she could not help it. Lady Mary always made her feel stiff and unnatural. "Sorry!" said her ladyship sharply. “It's a perfect scandal. What is the girl thinking of? She's refused three brilliant matches since June. First Prince

Dmitri, and then Sir Randal Curtis, and now George!" "I can't compel her to make a brilliant match,” said

Vivien, looking at Lady Mary with the big dark eyes which had stolen Ralph's heart away from his mother, long ago. "No," said Lady Mary drily. "Thanks to Adelaide and Roderick and the criminal wills they made. It was madness to leave so much money to a child of twenty-one. But the question now is—who is the man ?" "What man?" asked Vivien, purposely dense. "The man she fancies herself in love with. For of course there is some man." She went off into a fit of musing. "Captain Sholto-Scott!" she then said triumphantly. "Has she been seeing much of him lately?”

Vivien gave a slight start, not unperceived by Lady Mary, and stared at her inquisitor with troubled eyes. 

A new and most unwelcome idea had suddenly presented itself. "No," she said after a pause. "I don't think there's anything in that. Of course, she takes people to Rosalind's shop sometimes, but I'm sure Captain Sholto- 

Scott has no such idea in his head."

"That doesn't matter," said Lady Mary grimly. "It's the girls who do the wooing nowadays. We don't want another Iffley business. . . . I hear the Singletons are already talking of a divorce." "Captain Sholto-Scott isn't an Iffley," said Vivien. "And even if he was, the best thing we can do is precisely-nothing. 99 And to this conclusion, after much discussion, Lady 

Mary came herself.

All through her solitary lunch in Primula's white and yellow dining-room, Vivien kept turning over Lady Mary's suggestion. How could she have been so stupid? When Captain Sholto-Scott had first come to the house, she had at once leapt to the conclusion that he was attracted by Primula, but never had it occurred to her that Prim could have any feeling for him—beyond the rather sentimental admiration which most young girls feel for a daring and brilliant airman who has set the world talking about him. 

She tried to remember the times she had seen them together. They were not many. He had lunched and dined at the house once or twice, and as far as she could recall Prim had not taken much notice of himshe had been rather stiffly polite, in fact. But one could not judge from one or two parties, especially if one was biased, as she had been, by finding out Sholto- Scott's secret, and by her own plans for Rosalind. 

And besides, for six months now she had been prac-

tically living in another place and century. Sarah 

Sorel had prevented her from seeing what was—perhaps-under her very nose. 

She felt unhappy. Either Primula or Rosalind might have to suffer through this, and, as she had said to Lady Mary, they could do nothing. 

So through October and November, Vivien and her mother-in-law did nothing, and Primula was pleased and astonished to find that there was no need for her to run away and play golf. Grandy was as gracious and caressing as before, and never once referred to George. 

All the same, the girl dimly felt that she was under observation, and put on her defensive armour of nonchalance and flippancy.

She set up a violent protective flirtation with Larry 

Desmond, until that young man began to dream nightly of Rolls-Royces; and she seldom went near 

Throckmorton's. She was very unhappy. She envied

Rosalind, who saw him every day. She felt that if she could see him every day, she could make him love her.

It was so easy for Prim Staniforth to make men love her. She had done it again and again. 

She even sometimes spared a pitying thought for George, who was down at Ebbsworth, trying to solace his disappointed love with prize cattle and the latest thing in dairy machinery. Supposing poor George was suffering like herself! 

One murky afternoon at the end of November, she.

found her feet taking her down Douro Street towards Duke's Head Place. It really was on her way home, for she had been visiting Poppy Iffley, who had a flat in Wellesley Square. 

Throckmorton's was empty, as it was nearly closing time. The two rooms looked to Primula very much as they had on the day she had first seen them, but she knew that all these things were different things. 

Throckmorton's was doing very well.

He was not there. She knew it at once, and though a dreadful pang of disappointment shot through her, at least her knees left off trembling and her heart ceased to thump.

Rosalind came out of the office, followed by Miss 

Mason in her hat and coat. "Hullo, Prim!" called out Rosalind. "Come to fetch me? I'll be ready in five minutes. . . . Goodnight, Ivy. This is wonderful news of yours, though, of course, we'll miss you terribly."

Primula said a gracious good-night to the little typist, who gave her an admiring glance, and afterwards was able to entertain Mum and Kath with an accurate description of Miss Staniforth's black velvet and silver fox, her chenille beret and her enamel handbag. "She's going to be married," said Rosalind, nodding towards the vanishing figure of Miss Mason. "Her young man's just got a good job, and she's given a month's notice. So now we shall have to get another

girl, worse luck. Ivy's not over-intelligent, but we'd got used to her, and Captain Sholto-Scott keeps an eye on her." 

Another girl. 

Keeps an eye on her.

Rosalind put on her coat, pulled her hat down over her shining hair, and locked up Throckmorton's for the night. They had an iron grating now over the windows, and burglar alarms fixed upon them. "Taxi to-night," she said. "I've had a busy day.

Captain Sholto-Scott has gone away for a week." "Is he ill?" asked Primula. "No. Gone to see his people at Sidmouth.”

Primula longed to ask a hundred questions, but dared not. Love made you give yourself away so. So they sat in silence as the taxi bowled along. 

The moment the olive-green door of No. 20 had closed behind them, Vivien pounced upon them and dragged them into the library. "Darlings, you're terribly late," she cried with an excited laugh. "I've been lying in wait for ages. . . . 

Oh, Rosalind-Look!"

She held out a letter: Messrs. Bulstrode and Finch would be very glad to accept The Heart of Sarah Sorel, of which their reader had formed a high opinion. And if their terms were satisfactory to Mrs. Staniforth, it would be published in the spring.

ROSALIND was a busy and a happy woman. She could not help being happy with her jolly little shop, and the balance at the bank quite satisfactorily increasing. 

She was brimful of ideas for the future. When Esther pulled her green silk curtains back in the morning, it was Throckmorton's that she remembered first.

Once it had been Dick; that he was in the world, in 

China or the Mediterranean; that they loved one another; and afterwards, that he would never come home again.

But now--she couldn't hide it from herself-Dick, the figure round whom the other inhabitants of the globe had grouped themselves in a dim and shifting background, had receded into that background, though he was always present there. In the foreground was Throckmorton's. . Was that William Kent table a very clever fake, or not? Should they take on 

Harrison's son, who was such a good carpenter? • • Latterly she was beginning to think a great deal about Sholto-Scott as well. After that walk, she had expected further confidences: but they had not come.

He was his usual self, pleasant, gay, friendly, but he told her no more about the lady who lived in London. 

So that now she could not help watching every woman

who came into the shop. Surely the woman would come some day, as she lived in London!

Rosalind found herself paying far more attention also to the remarks he casually let drop about his weekend engagements. The Taverners-he went there oftenest had they a daughter? And who was Molly

Robson? But she easily found out that the Taverners had no daughters, and Molly Robson came to the shop one afternoon with her fiancé. Rosalind felt rather annoyed with herself for her curiosity, but she could not help asking a casual question now and then about the people he mentioned. He always answered them frankly and without self-consciousness; and Rosalind only discovered that the frank can be as mysterious as the reserved.

Then, little by little, a suspicion began to dawn upon her mind, perhaps sent up from some well of unconscious knowledge deep down within her being. 

Could it be that she herself was the woman? His sudden friendliness after Prim's party, his persistence in seeking her out, the way he had manœuvred the business of Throckmorton's-all these things seemed significant, now that she looked at them in this light. 

And yet-Rosalind turned and snubbed herself sharply for her vanity. Why, she was six years older than he was not a very big difference, but a difference on the wrong side.

Sholto-Scott at thirty-two was a young man; an attractive man, too, and now that his arm was rapidly mending he would be going back to his career of adventure in the air, to win fresh glory for his country and himself. He ought to have a lovely young wife like 

Like Primula? 

No. Rosalind didn't think, somehow, that Primula was the right wife for Sholto-Scott. 

As this point, she could not resist going across to the pierglass and staring frowningly at her reflection. She saw a tall slender figure in a black lace gown with a string of turquoise beads; she saw a delicate face, colourless and tired, and most distinctly did she see the faint lines which lingered about the corners of her eyes and mouth, the silver threads in her fair hair. 

No woman is fair to herself, however, when frowning into a mirror in search of defects; so Rosalind quite missed the charm of her faintly surprised eyebrows, her periwinkle-blue eyes, and the play of changing expression which had made Flip's lovers delight to watch her face. "Idiot!" she said after a minute, and went back to her desk with immense resolution, to study the advertisement columns of the Morning Standard. In three weeks, Ivy Mason would be leaving them, and she must find a successor.

The three of them had dinner alone that evening, Primula having backed out of a restaurant party at the last moment. "I am indisposed," she said to her mother. "Indisposed to be proposed to again by Jack Twickenham.” "It'll do you good to go to bed early for once," remarked Vivien, who cared very little about the possible disappointment of young Mr. Twickenham.

After dinner the girl followed her elders up into Vivien's sitting-room, and seated herself on a low stool beside the fire, from which she watched Rosalind as she read. Vivien was scribbling on a pad. 

Presently Robinson appeared at the door. "You're wanted on the phone, ma'am," she said to Vivien. 

Vivien threw down her writing-pad and went out.

There was a pause, filled with the pleasant noises of the fire. "Got anybody yet to take the place of Miss Thingumbob?" casually inquired the Golden Girl. "No," said Rosalind, lifting her eyes from Angel 

Pavement. "We haven't really tried." "W-will you give the job to me?" said Primula, taking the plunge.

Rosalind dropped Angel Pavement, and stared at her niece in the blankest surprise. "My dear Prim," she said, "you can't be serious. 

You'd simply hate it. Nine to five every day in a boring little office. It's all very well for Ivy Mason.

She's been trained to do what she doesn't particularly like, but you couldn't stand two days of it." think. "Yes, I could. I've got more grit than you 

Oh, Rosalind, do let me come. I—I'm so terribly bored with life."

The pale entreaty on the lovely face struck a chill of foreboding to Rosalind's heart. "But, Prim-" she began, casting about for words of refusal that should be kind. "You're not-I mean,

Ivy has to do typing and keep the books." "She hasn't much to do," said Primula eagerly. "You were saying so yourself. . . . Look here-it's three weeks before she goes. I'll get someone to coach me in typewriting and keeping books. . . . I'll work hours at it every day." "But why do you want to do this?" asked Rosalind curiously. "Oh, because-er-I'm sick of playing round and being so useless. I want to do some real work.” "We'll ask your mother about it," said Rosalind.

On her return, Vivien listened to them with inward disturbance; she seemed to be hearing again the voice of Lady Mary. 

"Darling, you'll miss St. Moritz," she said. She knew that three weeks' winter sports after Christmas was almost a necessity of life to Primula. "I don't care about St. Moritz. I want to help 

Rosalind with her shop. . . . Other girls go and

work in shops-look at Clarissa Roebuck-so why shouldn't I?" 

There was a little perplexed silence during which 

Vivien looked away from Rosalind, and Rosalind avoided the eyes of Vivien. "Very well, Prim," said Rosalind at last. "I'll have you. Provided that Captain Sholto-Scott consents." "Darling!” cried Primula, flinging herself upon her aunt and kissing her soundly on both cheeks. “I'll be the best office girl there ever was. I'll begin learning to-morrow." 

She went back to her stool by the fire, Rosalind picked up Angel Pavement, and Vivien began making notes again for a short story. Everything in the charming eighteenth-century room looked as reposeful as before.

But a stone had been cast into the peaceful pool. 

Vivien scribbled, but her heart was not with her storypeople any longer. "She is in love with him," she thought, casting a surreptitious glance at the child, in her slim black velvet frock, gazing pensively into the flames. “Oh dear! I'm afraid there's going to be a lot of trouble for someone. And I daren't say a word to Rosalind, for fear of saying too much." "It may be horrid of me," reflected Rosalind, running her eyes once more over page forty, “but I don't want her at Throckmorton's. If she's not the

one, she's in for a bad time, poor child. And if she is -I don't believe in mixing up love and business. Besides, it means a lot of extra work." "I shall see him every day," thought Primula. "I'll make him love me. It isn't so difficult to make men love you-" she smiled happily at a dozen triumphant memories. "Darling Mother and darling Rosalindso quiet and absorbed! . . . But, oh!" the thought crept in. "It must be terrible to be on the wrong side of thirty, and to have finished for ever with everything that really matters."

SHOLTO-SCOTT was secretly very much astonished when Rosalind came to him with her request about 

Primula, but then he remembered that the girl of today collects experiences as her predecessor collected autographs. The Princess of Stocks and Shares had her caprices, he supposed, in abundance. Well, if she had the grit to turn up in the office at nine o'clock every morning, his opinion of her would go soaring upwards. Personally, he gave her a week. 

Not that he didn't look forward with some pleasure to this gay invasion of business routine. What man could be quite unmoved to hear that the prettiest girl

in London was going to bestow some of her bright company upon him every day? "Er-I suppose we shall have to lend a hand with the book-keeping," he remarked. "It's hardly in Miss 

Primula's line." 

"Yes, at first. But Prim's not so ignorant of business as you might suppose. You see, her grandfather has insisted that she should have some training in the management of money. He doesn't want her to be the ordinary helpless female when she comes into his fortune. And I believe she's inherited quite a lot of Sir 

Everard's ability, too. Besides, she goes every morning to a business college to be coached." "Quite an honour for Throckmorton's," he observed, smiling.

Rosalind glanced at him curiously. She thought he didn't seem like a man who has just heard that he is going to see quite a lot of his beloved one. Still, one never knew-with men. 

Primula made her first appearance at Throckmorton's at a quarter to nine on 1st January, stepping out of her big green car, to the admiration of the other inhabitants of Duke's Head Place. She came shyly out of the tiny cloak-room where Rosalind left her out-door things, and Sholto-Scott felt for a moment as if he were keeping shop in some musical comedy. For the Golden 

Girl was attired in Madame Isolde's conception of a business girl's dress: a simple but exquisitely cut little

frock of dark blue, with a demure collar and cuffs of hand-embroidered linen, which had to be renewed by Sophie every night. She looked enchanting, but she was so timid and quiet that Sholto-Scott inwardly wondered. Where had the Serene Highness vanished to?

Presently, however, when Rosalind was showing her the attractive contents of the shop, she recovered her self-possession. Her heart ceased to flutter and her knees to quake, and she was able to attend to what 

Rosalind was saying about Chippendale and Sheffield plate, though it was strangely mingled with other thoughts and perceptions. 

How nice he looked in those dark grey clothes. . . .

Hepplewhite came after Chippendale, she must remember. . . . She did like a man to have a thin, dark face.... 

Then Sholto-Scott explained to her about the books. 

She made a determined effort to understand his explanations, and not merely listen to his voice, for she was going to do everything she could to please. He should see that she was more than a spoilt and frivolous society girl. She had worked very hard at the dingy business college, and had managed to pick up a good deal, so that Sholto-Scott was impressed by her quickness and intelligence. 

Rosalind gave her two or three letters to answer, which she tapped out slowly but accurately on the typewriter. And then customers came, and bought an

oak settle; she could hear them through the half-open door of the office. Sholto-Scott was in and out very often. Really, it was great fun keeping a shop! 

Getting up at seven o'clock in the cold January mornings was not such fun; nor was the crowded tube as comfortable as the Rolls-Royce. But Primula put up with it. He must not suppose that she had less grit than Rosalind, who was so much older. 

January went by, and most of February, and Rosalind confessed to herself that she didn't know what was going to happen. Sholto-Scott was to her as inscrutable as the sphinx. He and Primula were soon on the very best of terms. He chaffed and teased her; there was a half-humorous, half-affectionate note in his voice whenever he spoke of her, and it was easy to see how much he admired her in her piquantly plain little frocks. 

And yet why was he so long about it? Why didn't he hurry up and propose, if Primula was the girl he had spoken of? He surely must know that he would stand a good chance, for Primula opened out like a flower in his presence. And Miss Staniforth couldn't go on indefinitely typing letters for Throckmorton's, though Rosalind had to admit that the girl was throwing herself into her work as if her living really depended on it. She was already more valuable than Ivy Mason.

Well, sooner or later something must intervene Cupid, or the Staniforths-to put an end to a state of affairs which Rosalind secretly found very irksome indeed. 

She scorned herself for this feeling. She fought it down. She was unvaryingly sweet to Primula, and kept herself out of the way as far as she could. The loversif they were lovers should have every chance. "How's Prim getting on?" asked Vivien, one evening when Rosalind came into her room. "Oh, splendidly. She can type quite fast now, and she's much more useful than Ivy. Spells better, too." "She seems much happier," said Vivien thoughtfully. Then, carelessly. "How does she get on with 

Captain Sholto-Scott?" "Oh, very well indeed," replied Rosalind, hoping that Vivi would not pursue the subject. She felt oddly reluctant to discuss the affairs of Prim and Captain Sholto-Scott. "Does he does he still like having her at Throckmorton's?" "Of course. He's delighted. Any man would be. 

She's perfectly charming so sweet and frank, and so adorably pretty. What are those papers, Vivi? Anything to do with Sarah?"

Vivien nodded, and pushed them towards her sister. 

Her dark eyes were shining as they always did when

she was stirred, and she looked no more than twenty. 

The Heart of Sarah Sorel had been published about a fortnight now, and Vivien had decided to have a guinea's worth of press cuttings from a well-known agency. "Of course, I shan't get anything like my guinea's worth," she said to Rosalind. "But I shouldn't like to miss my modest two or three."

Rosalind picked them up; there were seven of them, attached to the pale green slips of Messrs. Dewar and 

Heilbronn. 

““The Dictator, 15th Feb.,'” she read out in an awed voice. "Quite a long one, too." "Read that one first," said Vivien. "It's by Augustus 

Pettitt. It's the earliest one."

Rosalind read it through with increasing excitement. "Oh, Vivi, how gorgeous.... you have ‘authentic genius'!" 

Do you see, he says 

Vivien gave an excited laugh. “Yes,” she said, "but then, so has Deb Temple-Templeton-Temple. 

Everyone has it nowadays. It's far more uncommon to have a knowledge of grammar. . . . But the rest of the review is terribly flattering—the critical part. 

Augustus has done me another good turn. . . . Oh, Rosalind-fancy Sarah!"

But Rosalind was already devouring one of the other cuttings.

"The Morning Standard says it's another Wuthering Heights," she observed when she had finished. "Any tolerable novel with a moor in it is ‘another 

Wuthering Heights,"" said Vivien. "It's a convention of criticism. But I'd rather be a first Vivien Staniforth than a second Emily Brontë. Look-this one's Charles 

Martin in Pallas Athene. I got a most terrible swelled head after reading that. I do admire Charles Martin so much. And he is so difficult to please.' 99 When she had read all the seven cuttings, Rosalind sat back and looked at Vivien with new eyes. Sincerely as she loved her, she could not help it. That quiet, not smart little woman, her sister Vivi, who had somehow made rather a mess of her own private affairs, the housekeeping mother of the dazzling Primula, was apparently a gifted and brilliant person, greatly admired by such men as Augustus Pettitt and Charles Martin, not to mention the critics of the other five papers. It seemed impossible. Of course she had liked 

Sarah Sorel when she had read it in manuscript: it was a thrilling and passionate story, and beautifully told but is scarcely seemed like a book by a real author. "It's too good to be true," she said. “Oh, Vivi—I shouldn't be surprised if it turns out a best-seller!" "No!" said Vivien, laughing and shaking her head. "I expect I shall be read by exactly sixty-seven persons. If I could write like Deb Temple, now—”
