"PRIMULA," said Rosalind to her niece, as she came down to their eight o'clock breakfast on the last 

Saturday in February. "I'm not coming to the shop this morning-you might tell Captain Sholto-Scott. 

I think I'll run down to the sale at Oakden Slade.

Lily Maxwell says those old people collected things like a magpie, so perhaps I may pick up something interesting." 

She did not fail to notice the light of satisfaction that leapt into the girl's eyes, though she quickly concealed it by a demure dropping of her dark lashes over her coffee cup. 

Won't you "All right, Rosalind, I'll tell him. . . . take the Rolls? It'll be much more comfortable than going by train, and Purvis is simply eating his head off now I've become a business woman. Besides, you'll be able to bring back the loot, if it isn't very big."

Rosalind accepted this offer; it would be more satisfactory to bring the things back with her. She was amused at Primula's funny little way of expressing her gratitude for the opportunity of being alone with 

Sholto-Scott. 

It was true she had contrived it. She had no great hopes of finding anything worth while at Oakden

Slade. Still, a day in the country would calm her nerves, and perhaps help the hesitating couple to make up their minds. "I shan't go till about eleven," she said to Primula, when the girl looked in before starting for the office. "The sale's at three. . . . How pretty you look today!"

Primula glanced at her aunt with a small, sweet smile of satisfaction, as she pulled on her gauntlet gloves, and turned up the fur collar of her trim tweed coat round her little decided chin. "Good hunting!" she said affectionately, dropping a kiss on the top of Rosalind's head. "I won't think about people at all to-day," said Rosalind to herself, as the magnificent Rolls-Royce slid out of Caroline Square. "Only about things. 

People are so disturbing. Whatever they decide to do, you feel as if you didn't want them to do it. I shall think about trees, and lambs, and Lowestoft shepherdesses, and birds, and silver spoons, and little clouds like pebbles on a beach, and rushing through the air like a comet."

The car whirled her down forest roads to Epping, and then out into a country of flat, pale-green fields, sprinkled with mirror-like pieces of flood water, reflecting the sky, and bordered by pollarded trees of an elfish and threatening aspect. 

Oakden Slade House was a square, grey box of a

place, approached by a bare, treeless drive across meadows. There were half a dozen cars drawn up under the laurels in front. The flower-beds were trampled, and the steps were dirty, and strewn with papers and bits of straw. "Sales are so depressing," thought Rosalind, as she wandered with a crowd of strangers through the halfdismantled rooms. "Only a few months ago, that poor old lady was thinking this a very handsome drawingroom, and here we all are saying, 'How hideous!. . .

They haven't left any furniture that's much use to me, though."

She marked two or three lots on her catalogue: a wine cooler, a little table that had held a tea-urn in the days of Dr. Johnson, and a collection of odd bits of china in a tin bath. She rather liked the look of one of the jugs.

She sent Purvis to have his lunch at the village inn, and went out herself to eat her sandwiches in the garden. A door in the brick wall tempted her into the kitchen-garden. It felt warmer there; the old red walls seemed to collect and keep in all the pale February sunshine. At the other end, a man was digging manure into a vegetable bed, for the place was not neglected like the rest of the grounds. 

Rosalind ate her lunch on a wooden seat in an angle of the walls, to the music of a bird which sent forth his two or three piercing sweet notes again and again

from a bare branch above her head. Looking up, she could see his little throbbing body against a patch of intensely blue sky. Tranquillity came flowing into her. 

The spaced rows of onions in the onion-bed before her were showing tips of pale-green. Little armies of chives poked up their tiny spears. On the knotted black boughs of the peach-trees trained against the walls, the buds had scarcely yet appeared, and beyond, there were stretches of finely raked brown soil, flat and empty to the eye, but she knew that underneath asparagus and turnips, carrots and spinach were straining and swelling and forcing their way to the light. 

She thought of the lambs she had passed in the fields on the way. "How lovely everything is !" she thought. "And how divinely young! Even the old earth. And how hard they are all working at their job to fulfil themselves, and express themselves, and grow to perfect carrotand onion-hood! And aren't they all full of themselves, the darlings?" 

And then, for some reason, her thoughts flew to 

Primula. "That's what Prim's trying for, poor child. 

And she thinks she'll achieve it through him. 

Perhaps she will. . . . I wonder if anything's happened at Throckmorton's this morning. . Oh dear! What happiness love brought me! And what unbearable pain! Well-it's done with now, and I don't

want it over again. I want to be peaceful and friendly, and to work hard and be free. . . . Why, I believe there's actually a bee inside that crocus!"

She returned to the house a little later, to find that the auction had already begun, and was going on briskly in the drawing-room. The room was crowded and stuffy, and as Rosalind found that some time would pass before the auctioneer reached the lots she wished to bid for, she came out again, and wandered with idle curiosity through the kitchen regions. 

What a grim, old-fashioned range! What a cavernous oven, and what sinister dark cupboards, riddled with mouse-holes! The cook must have had to walk miles every week across that vast flagged floor between the sink and the fireplace. And she knew from Lily

Maxwell that the Slades had been in the habit of giving imposing dinner-parties, and large Christmas gatherings had been held every year in the old house.

Opening out of the kitchen was a china-pantry, a small room lined with cupboards and painted a dark yellow. There was a black forbidding sink in one corner, and the window was covered with stained-glass paper. Someone, perhaps a bored parlour-maid or footman, had at some time scraped off a portion of this, and through the hole, Rosalind could see a patch of green turf, with a little lovely group of white and purple crocuses rising from it.

"Spring looks in even here," she thought, and went closer to peer through the hole. 

Suddenly she heard a step behind her. Someone else had found his way into this dismal corner. Rosalind turned round quickly, and came face to face with 

Sholto-Scott. 

He stood in the doorway, looking at her, and something in his look, and in his presence there made her heart beat uncomfortably fast, the heart which she had schooled to such happy tranquillity during her hour in the kitchen-garden. All the same, she was determined not to behave like a chit of seventeen. "Captain Sholto-Scott? Why, what are you doing here?" she demanded, unconsciously lifting her chin a little. Flip looked at him out of her eyes. "Taking a walk in Essex," he said, cheerfully. "I've walked from Clavering. Don't you see the Essex clay on my shoes?"

She glanced involuntarily at his splashed brown brogues, but she knew all the time that he was looking at her in a very—unusual way. And more than that, he meant her to be aware of it. "Wh-when did you come?" She couldn't think of anything more brilliant to say under that disconcerting gaze. "I've been here half an hour. I saw you come into the drawing-room. Miss Primula was kind enough to tell me you'd gone to the Oakden Slade sale."

"Oh! . . . Well, I'm afraid you haven't had a very interesting walk. I thought the country was dreadfully flat and dull." "I didn't notice the country. I was thinking of something else. . . . Rosalind- 99 "I must go back to the auction room,” said Rosalind, very quickly and decidedly. "They must have got to

Lot 25 by now. Please Captain Sholto-Scott ---let me pass." "Rosalind. .. You're the densest woman of intelligence I've ever met. . . . Will you misunderstand this?" 

And then for a moment Rosalind was rapt away from the dingy pantry of Oakden Slade House. Singularly enough, she had the sensation of being on a cliff path, with sea-birds screaming overhead. A strong arm was holding her close, and kisses were being showered on her mouth, her eyes, her hair. Like long ago. But this wasn't Dick. It was Fitzroy Sholto-Scott, her friend and partner. She did not struggle, for even in the tumult of her emotions she remembered that his left arm was still weak, and easy to injure. "Let me go, please," she said at last, in a stifled voice. 

He let her go, and then for a moment their glances crossed, Sholto-Scott's smiling and defiant, Rosalind's inscrutable.

Then she made an immense effort. "I'm afraid Lot

25 will have gone," she remarked in quite an ordinary voice, and she walked out of the room with no sign of emotion beyond the brilliant colour in her cheeks. 

Sholto-Scott looked after her, knitting his black brows. He was puzzled, but not in the least repentant. 

He knew that it would be worse than useless to follow her now, so he left the house, and set off at a swinging pace for the station, three miles distant.

As for Rosalind, she went back to the drawingroom, and plunged blindly into the bidding; to find at the end of five minutes that she had parted with twenty five shillings, and become the owner of a copper teaurn of rather dilapidated appearance, and late Edwardian date. 

SHOLTO-SCOTT went to Throckmorton's before nine on 

Monday morning in complete uncertainty as to what reception was awaiting him. He did not know whether 

Rosalind was angry with him or not, nor did he much care. He was aware that he had as difficult a job before him as when he had climbed into his plane on the morning of the Pinkerton Trophy Race; but he also knew that he was bringing to it the same tenacity and will to win.

Throckmorton's was already open: the box trees, which were taken in every night, were standing on each side of the door. "Good morning," said Rosalind, looking up from the Irish glass decanter she was dusting. "You're late this morning."

He thought she looked young and defiant, in the blue silk smock which she usually wore in the shop. She shook her fair hair away from her brow, and met his gaze with serene inscrutable blue eyes. There was no trace of self-consciousness in her manner. Saturday's encounter seemed to have faded from her memory. "It's you who are early," he retorted. And then: "Did you manage to secure Lot 25?” he asked, blandly. "Yes," said Rosalind with equal blandness. "It's a little beauty, a wine cooler, and I got it for fifteen shillings." She did not add that she had also secured 

Lot 24, the copper tea-urn. "And I picked up a table and some rather nice old plates and jugs-they're in the office, if you'd care to look at them.' "Quite a successful day, then?" "Oh-quite." 99 Primula came out of the office at this moment, and greeted him with her attractive mixture of frankness and shyness. She had been disappointed with Saturday; but then, it was not his fault. It was really Poppy 

Iffley's, for bringing Mrs. Hendrikson, the newest wife of the Cough-Drop King of America, whose vivacity

filled the shop and overflowed into Duke's Head Place. 

This pretty young woman had met Sholto-Scott in New York when she was Miss Mary Hermann, and she had the air of one who resumes an interrupted-and rather advanced-flirtation. She rolled her bright eyes at him in a way that Primula thought was exceedingly unattractive.

But she was good for business. She was generous, and tried to buy the whole stock of Throckmorton's.

Sholto-Scott seemed to enjoy it, but Primula noticed that he kept back Rosalind's favourite pieces. 

When Mrs. Hendrikson had departed, with a lingering last glance and an invitation to dinner at her hotel, Sholto-Scott turned to Primula with a slight twinkle in his eye. “That's a charming lady," he said. “But I wasn't going to let her have the lac bureau. She'd put it in one of her houses where she spends about three weeks in the year, and Miss Forey would never forgive us for letting it go. She doesn't like her pets to be lonely and neglected."

Immediately afterwards, someone else had come in asking for maiden-head spoons, and then another who was interested in pewter. 

Altogether, a wasted morning for Prim Staniforth. 

All Monday, Rosalind went on treating him with the same, or very nearly the same, friendliness as usual. 

Sholto-Scott followed her lead with surprising docility,

but she was not quite easy in her mind. She felt there was a subdued twinkle in his eyes, and she could not forget his dreadful tenacity.

A day of strain went by, and she grew tired of it. 

Better perhaps to have it out, after which they could perhaps get back, little by little, to their old comfortable relations.

Before long, his arm would be as good as ever, and then he would become one of the pilots of Aerial Transit, Ltd., and would be away for long stretches of time. 

He would be practically a sleeping partner. Her mind wandered back to Throckmorton's. She thought she would ask her friend Mrs. Costello to come and help her when Sholto-Scott was no longer there. Kate Costello had very good taste, and she had just been left a widow, not well-off.

So when Primula came down with toothache on 

Tuesday morning, Rosalind quite welcomed it, and packed the girl off to the dentist, whither she went willingly enough. Grandy's beauty lessons, conveyed in little mocking comments on other women, had borne fruit in her granddaughter. "Miss Primula not coming?" asked Sholto-Scott when Rosalind came in alone. "No," replied Rosalind. "She's gone to have a filling put in one of those nice little teeth of hers. . . . Oh, I don't think it'll hurt much. It sounds ridiculous, but

Prim's terribly afraid of becoming a toothless hag."

He laughed. "Does Aurora really have to go to the dentist?" he asked. "Well, I'm rather glad for once that Aurora's away. I want to have a talk with you about business." "Yes," said Rosalind, frankly meeting his eyes. "It's time we did, I think. But we can't this morning. 

Professor Simonson's coming at ten to look at those ivories, and Mr. Bennet about eleven, and the Guildensterns any time after that." "Confound Mr. Bennet, and confound the Guildensterns, and confound the whole damned lot of them!" "That's not the way to talk about customers. I'm afraid you're trying to mix up business with other business, and that's fatal. What would happen to 

Throckmorton's if-" "Oh, confound Throckmorton's! I wish to heaven we'd never started the shop. You think of nothing else." "But I do! I spent the whole week-end thinking about you and me, and what would be the best for us in the long run."

He came round to her side of the desk and took both her hands. "Rosalind-put all that aside now and don't think of it any more. I love you, my dearest, with all my heart and soul, and I believe you're coming to love me. Look in my eyes and tell me if it isn't so."

She raised her blue eyes to his without hesitation.

"Perhaps it is perhaps I could love you if I let myself. But I'm not going to let myself. I have loved and suffered, and I don't want to love in that way any more. 

I only want peace. When you go away on long, dangerous flights, I shall be anxious even now, because you are my friend. But if I let myself love you—I'd be in torment. Love grows so terribly fast. . . ." "I know it does," said he. "I know as much about love as you do. Rosalind—I ask you to think of me for a moment." "I do. I do even more than of myself, and that's why I'm saying this. I'm too old for you. I'm six years older than you are more, really, because a woman is always older than a man. I've got grey hairs and wrinkles. I've grown quiet and dowdy—" "How much longer are you going to talk nonsense?” "I'm not. It's sense. You ought to marry some charming young girl-you've got plenty of choicewho'll love you, and be able to play with you, and give you some jolly children—” "I don't want children. I want you. Rosalind, you are not going to let a miserable six years stand between us? What possible difference can it make?” "It does-to my feelings. I can't help it. Oh, Fitz, don't look like that. You haven't known me for very long. You'll soon get over it. Why don't you go away for a bit? I'll look after Throckmorton's.' 99 "I'm not a boy, Rosalind. I'm a man. And I have

been in love with you for years, though I didn't know who you were. . . . I loved you before he did-Flip!" 

She stared at him. "Do you remember staying with the Jolliffes at 

Understrand one summer? You were about sixteen.

Do you remember a Mrs. Mallandaine bringing her young nephew to spend the afternoon? It was a very hot day, and you went about arm-in-arm with a girl called Norah. You were the most bewitching creature that ever walked the earth, and I never forgot you... I fell in love with then... you 99 Rosalind was gazing at him a little wildly. Then she began to laugh hysterically. "You-you were the little boy that came with Mrs. Mallandaine? 

Oh, Fitz, don't you see how ridiculous that makes it? . . . For me to marry the little boy who came with Mrs. Mallandaine and played with the babies-played with Roy and Sylvia. 

Oh, Fitz, don't be angry with me. I can't help it." For he was looking at her with a very pale face, his black brows drawn into a bar above his burning eyes. "I think you have no heart," he said, and walked out of the office. She could hear him greet Professor

Simonson, who was evidently coming in, but when she hurried out into the shop, the Professor was there alone. "Good morning, Miss Forey," he said cheerily. "I've just met Captain Sholto-Scott. Looks a bit off

colour this morning. He said he was going home-best place for him, too." "VIVIEN," said Rosalind, standing by Vivien's bed in the early light of the next morning. "Will you telephone to Captain Sholto-Scott and ask him to be sure to be at Throckmorton's this morning? I've got one of my headaches coming on, and Prim can't manage by herself."

Vivien started up in bed, and sat blinking at her sister. Yes, there was no doubt poor Flip was in for one of her bad days. Her apple-green kimono made her seem even paler than she really was, and there was a strained look about her blue eyes. "I was very tired yesterday," Rosalind offered hastily in explanation. “I had Professor Simonson, and a lot of other people, and all that bother about the fake settee-it worried me." "You must get someone to help you," said Vivien. "Someone responsible, not a child like Primula. Now get back to bed, and Rose shall bring you some tea. 

I'll see to Captain Sholto-Scott." 

Rosalind went back to her room, and let her head sink with relief against the cool linen pillow. She

wished that life were not so complicated, and oh, how she wished she hadn't laughed when he told her about Understrand! She hadn't meant to; it was simply overstrain, a nervous reaction. But soon the throbbing pain in her head banished every other thought; it was almost a comfort to give oneself up to it. 

After Vivien had dressed, she went to telephone to Sholto-Scott. She vaguely wondered why Rosalind should suppose he wouldn't go to Throckmorton's to-day. However, it was probably over-conscientiousness on Flip's part. 

"Really? . . . I'm very sorry," came the voice of

Sholto-Scott, politely concerned. "I shall certainly turn up at Throckmorton's. Tell Miss Forey not to worry.' 99 

Then she met Primula coming downstairs in a new frock, and told her about Rosalind. Did she imagine it, or did a gleam of satisfaction spring into the girl's eyes? Vivien frowned. She didn't like Prim to be alone at the office, not from any notions of propriety—she was less of a Victorian than Primula supposed-but she did not think it good for the child to feed her infatuation so. Should she say a word to her? . . No; she might do more harm than good. Vivien also had some experience of the perversity of young things. If she could have seen Sholto-Scott at the other end of the line, a good deal would have been revealed to her, for he looked nearly as haggard as Rosalind. He had

spent a night of wakefulness, of bitter memories, of loneliness and the sense of loss.

After getting away from Professor Simonson, he had flung off in his anger, he knew not where; but presently he found himself at Piccadilly Circus. To the spot where he stood red buses came surging up one after another like waves on a beach. He watched them without seeing them for a minute or two, and then, with a sudden impulse, he jumped on to the nearest one, and went on top. "How far do you go?" he asked the conductor who came for his fare. "Richmond? . . . Well, then, Richmond, please."

After a dogged muddy tramp for two or three hours along the towing path and across fields, he was done with anger. But the hurt remained. If she was the sort of woman who could laugh at what was so sacred to him, she was not his Rosalind. Better, perhaps, to find it out in time. . . . But he was not ready for philosophy just yet.

His rooms looked inexpressibly dreary that night, in spite of the kind attentions of Mrs. Renfrew. He was finding out now how much he had been thinking of a home of his own, with Rosalind to laugh with him and comfort him, quarrel with him and bless him. 

Now that was over.

His mother's house was far away, and full of hefty

young Thompsons. The Taverners had gone for a long trip to South Africa. His cousin Barbara, Mrs.

Blair, was in Brighton; and besides, she was always trying to convert him to Christian Science, and he couldn't stand it. He couldn't shut his eyes, like her, and say there was no pain. For there was, and he felt it now.

In the end, hating his own company, he spent an unmirthful evening at Not All There, the latest spectacular revue at the Vivacity Theatre. 

During the night, he wondered how he was to meet Rosalind next morning, for he could not desert 

Throckmorton's. But then, he decided, he need not worry. She would turn up serene and smiling, he thought bitterly. She would probably have almost forgotten the incident of yesterday, and be thinking of some damned Chippendale chair or other. 

Still, he was relieved, when Vivien rang him up. It would make things much easier for him, not to see her for a day or two.

He received a small shock when he entered the shop, for there was Primula, in Rosalind's blue silk overall, dusting the more fragile treasures, just as Rosalind used to do. But his heart resumed its normal beat when he perceived that it was only the Golden Girl. She lifted her large, clear grey eyes to his, with something in their depths that he had never been able to fathom.

She told him briefly that Rosalind was a little better, and then went into the office, and began typing very fast. The click-click of the typewriter irritated him; he felt a longing for a little human intercourse, and he waited impatiently for it to stop. Surely she couldn't have so many letters to type!

He did not know what a desperate resolution was forming under Primula's waves of silver-gold. "I'm so tired of this. I can't bear it much longer. 

How silly it all is! Why are we fooling about like —like a couple of Victorians? We're not Victorians. . . But—even-if-we-were, Queen Victoria did it!"

A Princess of Stocks and Shares was in almost the same position as a queen.

Still, however modern you were, it was not quite so easy to do as to talk about. Primula had done some of the talking herself, among her young friends, but now, she felt an inclination to put off the doing, at least until the afternoon.

So she went on typing and typing; pages out of a 

History of Chinese Ceramics, which Rosalind kept in the office. It made her feel less frightened. 

There were no customers in the afternoon, and Sholto-Scott took to prowling restlessly about, hesitating sometimes outside the door of the office, from which there still proceeded the maddening click-click. 

At last he boldly went in. Primula looked up at him,

startled. How very pretty she was in that sort of dovegrey thing!

He came round behind her, and bent to look at the letter she was typing. "Ku Yueh Hsuan," he read, "was the studio name adopted by an artist named Hu. His style was-" "What on earth-?" he said. Primula jumped up from her seat. They stood very close together in the narrow space behind the typewriter table, and he looked at her with a slight twinkle in the blue eyes under the black brows. He was so very dear that her courage rose. "Why are you typing out all this stuff?" 

"I was frightened," she said, trembling but steady. "I'm going to do something dreadful, or at least, perhaps you'll think it dreadful. . . . Fitz—”

She raised her eyes to his, and at what he saw in them at last, his heart gave a leap. "You see I'm so horribly rich that nobody decent ever asks me to marry them—and I love you so—” 

Her eyes met his bravely, though she could not prevent herself from turning an old-fashioned pink. 

There was a heartrending suggestion of Rosalind in her lovely face. She was a little darling-he had always thought so! "Of course if you'd rather not—” "Primula." Before he knew what had happened, he had the Golden Girl in his arms, and was kissing the

lips so frankly held up to his. She clung to him with a sigh of contentment, and he put his dark cheek against her soft hair. There was comfort in this warm human contact, and why should he not take what was offered him with both hands?

He would only be doing what Rosalind had advised, taking a young, charming wife, who loved him. .

Later on, no doubt, there would be those jolly children. 

They would perhaps make up to him in the end. "Love me?" murmured Primula. "Of course I do." He kissed her lips again, and then her soft white throat. After all, it was true. He did love her for loving him, and he swore to himself that he would make her a good husband, and never let her find out- What?

He pushed the thought resolutely away. He must never think of that again. He must thrust Rosalind out of his life—as far, at any rate, as she would consent to go. "I'm glad I was brave enough to be so dreadful," said Primula, happily. "We might have gone on forever, and I should have died an old maid. I could never marry any man but you. Oh, Fitz, I feel as if

I'd like to give a diamond necklace to everybody in the world."

THE wedding of Miss Mary Primula Staniforth was to be the social event of mid-April, and Vivien found herself snatched at last from her secluded existence and immersed simultaneously in two entirely different streams of events. 

One of these was the consequence of Sarah Sorel. 

Vivien had long since received her guinea's worth of press-cuttings from Messrs. Dewar and Heilbronn, for Sarah Sorel was now selling over a thousand copies a week, and its author was fast becoming a celebrity. 

She had already been interviewed six times, and invited by two Sunday papers to give the public her views on Companionate Marriage and Face Powder. 

She was greatly amused by all this, and would have enjoyed it with her whole heart, if she had had time. 

But there was the other great event to which they were all too quickly moving-the event which was to take place in St. Margaret's, Westminster, on the 17th of April.

She would not have much to do with the actual wedding, for the Staniforths had taken that into their expert hands, and the reception was to be held at Sir 

Everard's house in Carlton House Terrace. 

"Lucky for me!" thought Vivien. "They're invit-

ing all Debrett." Two secretaries, specially engaged were busy all day in a room upstairs with lists and 

Lady Mary and Lady Paddington also supervised the trousseau, about which nobody dreamed of consulting the bride's mother. But there were plenty of odd jobs which could very well be performed by the not-smart Mrs. Staniforth, such as the arranging and disarranging of appointments with dressmakers; and these were left to Vivien.

She was a little shocked at the reckless way in which Primula discarded the lovely clothes which hung in her wardrobes. "It's perfectly new, Prim. You've hardly worn it. 

And look at all the beautiful embroidery." “Oh, mother—I couldn't possibly wear those old things when I'm married!" And Vivien understood that nothing was good enough for her to appear in before her lover's eyes.

Vivien's most difficult and anxious task was that of looking after the lovely, excited bride herself, who was in such a rapture of happiness that her mother was secretly afraid.

Somehow her unconscious knowledge told her that there was something not quite right about this engagement, though it was impossible to find the least fault with the demeanour of the bridegroom. 

Watching the charming, half-humorous tenderness

of his manner towards his young fiancée, she wondered sometimes whether she had really surprised his secret in his eyes that night in the theatre. But of course he might have changed. Men often did.

She was present when Rosalind offered him her congratulations. "Prim tells me you are to be my new nephew," said 

Rosalind, smiling and holding out her hand. "I think you're both very lucky people, and I hope you'll be very happy." "Thank you-Rosalind," he had gaily replied, taking the offered hand, but dropping it very quickly. "I'm luckier than I deserve, since Prim's willing to put up with me." "Silly!" said Prim, unashamedly standing on tiptoe to put a light kiss on his cheek. And everybody had laughed.

What was there about this happy little scene which had struck a false note? Vivien pondered over it afterwards, and then it came to her suddenly. He had not looked at Rosalind once. He had avoided her eyes.

Still, that might be her own imagination. With her novelist's sensitiveness, she was apt perhaps to see too much significance in such slight signs as this. And at least she would have a son-in-law whom she liked and respected-no Jack Twickenham or Larry Desmond with their greedy visions of perpetual idleness and luxury as the heiress's husband. 44

Rosalind she found quite inscrutable; serene, remote and more absorbed in Throckmorton's than ever before. As soon as she heard of her partner's engagement, she had sent off a telegram to Kate Costello in York, and now that capable, eager-faced woman was in the shop from morning to night, attending to the books and learning all she could from Rosalind about furniture and china and Sheffield plate. Between them they carried on the whole business in those busy days before the wedding. Sholto-Scott seldom had time to look in. "It's all right, Vivi," said Rosalind to her sister who had commented on this. "I'm not wearing myself out.

Kate is wonderful. It's heavenly to have someone so intelligent after the Ivy. . . . Besides, Fitz would have to go away some time when he got back to his own job. It's come a little sooner than we expected, that's all.. 

Anyhow, I can never be too thankful to him for slipping on that banana skin and starting Throckmorton's. I don't know what I should do without it." 

She smiled teasingly at Vivien. "What are you thinking of, Vivi?” "I was thinking," said Vivien, "that I'd like to buy that little china gentleman in the green coat. He'd be very happy on my mantelpiece, I believe." 

One day, a fortnight before the wedding, Primula brought her fiancé to Throckmorton's to buy a wedding present for a friend. It was the first time that they

had been there together, and Sholto-Scott had shown some reluctance; but at this Primula had pouted. "It's very queer the way you fight shy of your own shop," she said. "There's a red lacquer bureau there that I've set my heart on for Pamela. She will love it, and Rosalind will be pleased to get rid of it, so we'd be killing two birds with one stone." "I don't fight shy of Throckmorton's," said Sholto Scott, reddening a little. "I'm only too busy to go there, that's all. Still, have it your own way, darling." . "But I don't want to have my own way ever any more," said Primula, giving him a glance, and Sholto- 

Scott laughed and kissed her.

Nevertheless, he found himself following her through the peacock-blue doorway, and there was

Rosalind, slim and tall in her blue overall, brushing back her fair front lock with the familiar gesture. 

She looked pale-she was doing too much. 

Mrs. Costello, who came out of the office as they entered, thought to herself that she had never seen a better-matched pair than the handsome airman and the Staniforth girl, who was so very like that famous beauty, her grandmother. They seemed devoted to one another, too, though the man, naturally, was less demonstrative than the girl. 99 "I hope you haven't sold the red lacquer bureau,' said Primula, "because I want it for Pamela's wedding

present. She's getting married the day after us." "No, it's still here," replied Rosalind. "Do you mean Pamela Watson-Cox? I'm glad. I'd like her to have it. She'll love it and look after it properly."

When the details of the purchase had been settled, Primula began wandering round the shop, though Rosalind was conscious to her finger-tips that Sholto- Scott was longing to be gone. She picked up a child's cap, delicately worked in fine muslin. "Darling," she said laughing. "Would you like to see your son and heir in that? I must have it, Rosalind. 

It's adorable." "Rosalind's got a headache, Prim," put in Sholto- 

Scott quickly. "We oughtn't to keep her standing about." "Me?" cried Rosalind. "No, I'm all right. A little worried about those repairs Jobson's made such a mess of. However, Mrs. Costello's tackling him, and I think he's going to be sorry he ever was born." "Splendid!" said Sholto-Scott. "I believe you have a headache, though, all the same. Come along, Prim." 

And he swept the Golden Girl out of the shop and into the big green car which was waiting outside. “Kate,” said Rosalind, looking after them, "I believe I do feel a bit headachy. I'll go and have sixpennyworth of the top of a bus if you'll keep an eye on things. I shan't be long."

But as she walked with mechanical lightness and

briskness along Douro Street to the nearest bus, the fresh spring breeze brought her no relief. She did not feel it. She was painfully looking inward upon the turmoil of her own troubled heart. "Headache? I wish it was! I'm feeling the queerest, horridest feeling I ever had in my life. It's dreadful, but I believe I'm-jealous. I've never been jealous before but I suppose I've never had reason to be. ... When Prim kisses him, or puts her arm through his in that way she has, and to-day, when she talked about that baby's cap-I could have killed her. And yet, I never hanker after babies, like some women. I never did. . . . It's terribly degrading. I must conquer it somehow. . . . I wonder if I've made a great mistake? I wonder if it's possible to walk into love as well as fall? . . . Well, it's too late now. I must get over it. . . . I'm not going to let it spoil my life again. Love isn't everything, and I've got such a lot besides. I have Throckmorton's, and Vivi, and the girls at the Club. . . . There's only another fortnight. I shan't see so much of him when they are married, and then I shall manage splendidly."

"DARLING, I met a man last night who asked me if I were any relation to Vivien Staniforth"?

Vivien looked into the charming face lying back against a pale blue pillow. It was alight with amusement, but there was a little wonder, too, beneath the amusement. Primula was not accustomed to be regarded as a relation-she, the Golden Girl, who was nearly as well known as Lady Pamela Pamphlett, and who was making the most popular, if not the most brilliant, marriage of the season. "And had you ever heard of Vivien Staniforth?” asked Vivien, pinching her cheek. "Darling, of course!" She pulled her mother down to her, and gave her a hug. "Everyone has to, now, if they want to be intelligent at dinner-parties. But really, darling, I did love Sarah. So did Grandy and

Aunt Violet. Grandy sat up till two in the morning to finish it-there's secret history for you! . . . Only one's so terribly busy now with The Wedding that one hasn't time to think of anything else." "Naturally," said Vivien. She had, as a matter of fact, received polite congratulations on the success of Sarah Sorel from both Lady Mary and Lady Paddington. It's impossible to be quite sure with the novels of these clever young men. You see, they all write the same novel over and over again." "Dearest," said Primula, stretching her white arms luxuriously over her head. "I believe you've inherited my talent. I wrote a novel once at least, I did five chapters of it." "Prim, how exciting! Vivien's three rings. "I didn't know you knew so much about love," she said, after a little pause. "I knew enough to run away with your father against the will of both our families. West Linton? That was one of the suburbs, rather far out. Furtively and hurriedly, she looked up the number of the Cottage Hospital. West Linton 331.

Five minutes later she came out of the little room with a very troubled face. Poor darling Prim, still chattering away about brocades and velvets!

Primula and Lady Moira were sitting side by side on one of the dumpy little settees, with the Babbler open between them. Primula looked up as her mother came in. "Mother," she began. "Moira says Mother, what is it? Fitz?"

She sprang up, scattering the picture paper, and clutched Vivien's arms with an unconsciously painful grip of her strong young hands. "No, no, child, it's all right. In the car, holding Primula's cold, clutching little hand in hers, she told her what she had been able to gather about the accident. "It was a collision with a motor cyclist who came dashing out of a side road. The chauffeur and the cyclist are both injured, but not badly. Fitz israther worse." "Did he ask for me?" "No, dear. He's unconscious.” "O-oh!" moaned Primula. “Be brave, darling. Most people are unconscious after a motor smash. . . . I think we must be nearly there, now."

She stared out into the dusk. The neat, suburban roads were thinning out into patches of woodland and meadow, between the prim gardens. West Linton was on the edge of the real country. 

Presently, they turned in at a gate between two lamps. One or two people by the door looked curiously at the white face of the beautiful girl who sprang out of the car almost before it had stopped. Primula did not see them, but she flinched and shuddered at the faint, suggestive smell of iodoform which hung about the place.

The matron and the doctor came to see them in a small, bare waiting-room, and Vivien explained who they were. "His fiancée?" said the doctor, looking at Primula

with new interest. "Good! He's been asking for you ever since he was brought in, almost.” "Calling for me? Oh, Fitz! But you said he was unconscious." "So he is. He's a bit delirious, that's all." "Delirious?" said Primula, staring at him with wide, horrified eyes. She had seen so little of illness in her sheltered young life that the hospital word struck her with terror. “Is he is he terribly bad?” "We can't say yet, my dear, but we will hope not. 

You will do him more good than any doctor." "Let me go to him," she said, moving towards the door.

The three elders exchanged glances, and then the matron led the way up a polished staircase, and along a shining corridor. "Only for five minutes," said the doctor, outside the white door. "I allow no visitor but you, and I trust you to be a brave girl. I warn you he's pretty well done up in splints and bandages." 

Primula gave him a faint smile. "He's calling for me," she said.

Vivien followed her into a small room, dimly lit by a green-shaded lamp. 

In spite of the doctor's warning, Primula felt a little chill of horror as she approached the bed, for the outline of the long form which raised the coverlet into such strange lumps and angles was not that of the

strong, shapely man's body which lay beneath it. 

Primula knew nothing about splints and cradles. 

But there was Fitz's face, unchanged, against the low pillow, although it looked out from a helmet of white bandaging.

She knelt down very softly beside him. "Fitz, darling,” she said, very distinctly. "I'm here. 

Darling, it's Primula. you."

He turned his head slightly, and opened his eyes; but there was no recognition in them. She gazed with agony into those unseeing blue depths as if she could force her way through into the consciousness beneath.

Flip, darling!"

Suddenly, Vivien went to her daughter's side. "Come, my darling," she said, her hand on the girl's shoulder; and led her out of the room unresisting, while the astounded doctor stared at the astounded matron.

§ By the time they were standing under a dim light in the corridor, Primula had frozen into a little block of ice. "Well, Mother," she said. "We'd better go. We don't seem to be wanted here." 

Vivien looked at her pitifully, almost feeling in her own heart the wounded love and pride which she knew were torturing her daughter. Primula returned her gaze with bright, almost mocking eyes. "Wait a little, darling," begged Vivien. "I must speak to the doctor. Don't you don't you want to know any more about Fitz?"

A spasm of pain crossed the stony young face. "I'll wait in the car, then," she said. "Don't be long. . . . I want to go home." 

Vivien looked after her uncertainly; she hated to leave the child alone in this mood, but she could not go away without speaking to the doctor. Besides she ought to phone to Rosalind.

At this moment, the doctor came out of the private ward. Vivien tried to choose her words. "My daughter is rather-upset," she stammered. "There's been-some mistake" 

He looked at her kindly.

"It would probably relieve his mind," said the doctor cautiously; and he took Vivien to the telephone in the matron's office.

Privately, he wondered what would come of this curious complication in fashionable life. He was sorry for the girl, but she was so lovely that the man would probably return to his allegiance the moment he regained his senses. Provided she could forgive him his momentary lapse.

Vivien could not think of anything to say which would not jar on the girl's mood.

She felt a growing anger against Sholto-Scott. 

Why, if he loved Rosalind, had he proposed to Primula? He had yielded to temptation, no doubt, as nine men out of ten would have done, the temptation of almost fantastic wealth. She thought that he was honestly fond of Primula, too. But then, it is easy enough to feel fond of a beautiful girl who lays herself out to please you.

And now poor Prim was in for weeks and weeks, if not months, of suffering, to which the publicity of the affair would add enormously. . . . That reminded

During the long taxi drive out to West Linton, turning the matter over and over in her mind, she wished that Vivien had told her more. What did Primula think? Had she heard him calling for another woman? In the midst of her anxiety, Rosalind felt a rising excitement, and she despised herself for it. What a man says in delirium does not count. When he was conscious again, he would have forgotten it. After all,

he had chosen Primula, and asked her to marry him, when he was in his sober senses, and she, Rosalind, was not going to take advantage of a momentary weakness. 

Poor little Primula! He belonged to Primula, and she wished she hadn't come! 

And then, her heart would melt again. Dear Fitz!

Her friend and partner, with his kindness and his audacity, his thin brown face and the twinkle in his eyes. Could she refuse to give him anything he wanted -anything? She sat forward, twisting her hands, and willed the taxi to go faster.

In these alternations of feeling she spent the journey, but when she went up the hospital steps, it was her pride that rushed to the fore.

The little doctor, looking at her with covert curiosity, decided that he could not understand the sick man's taste. To call for this pale, distant creature, whose blue eyes had the chill serenity of stars on a frosty night, when he might have had the warm, soft loveliness of the other! There was a puzzling resemblance about the two; cousins, he judged, or perhaps half-sisters. "My sister phoned to me," said Rosalind to him. "I believe Captain Sholto-Scott was asking for me.” "If your name is Rosalind, he was," said the doctor, briefly. "Yes," said Rosalind, reddening a little. She wondered what Fitz could have said.

"I'm glad you've come," said he. "Perhaps it will set his mind at rest."

He led the way upstairs. She asked him one question as they went. "How-is he?" "Considerably knocked about, but nothing dangerous as far as we can see," he replied.

Rosalind belonged to a generation which had a wide acquaintance with the sights and sounds of suffering, but her self-control in the sick-room was less appealing to the doctor than the distress and shrinking of the beautiful young girl who had stood there before.

She went to the bedside with her swift, light step, and knelt down beside Sholto-Scott. He had ceased muttering now, his eyes were closed. She dared not say a word.

For five minutes she knelt in silence, never taking her eyes from the clear-cut mask surrounded by the white bandages. It was like a flying helmet, she thought. 

Her heart softened dangerously to see how pale he was under his tan, and how sharply the lines of his cheek bones and his arched nose stood out.

Suddenly he opened his eyes, and she found herself face to face with Sholto-Scott himself, alive, alert, and —yes, with a twinkle in his eyes. For a moment they gazed at each other without disguise, till everything else vanished, Primula, the doctor, the nurse, the world.

"Well, Rosalind?" he said at last in his natural voice. "Fitz, darling." She bent over him and put her lips to his. "HER ladyship wishes to see you, ma'am," said an alarmed voice by Vivien's bed the next morning.

She started up out of sleep. It must be very late indeed, for Lady Mary to be about. She glanced at her little clock-half past ten. "Tell her I'll be down in ten minutes."

Vivien had lain awake for hours, after sitting up for 

Rosalind. At last, at eleven o'clock, Robinson had brought a telephone message; Miss Forey would not be back until to-morrow. Nothing else. Vivien had pondered over that message in the small hours. 

She dressed quickly and went downstairs, making a few inquiries of Sophie on the way. She found her formidable mother-in-law, in the smartest of grey cloth tailor-mades, enthroned in the small drawing-room and glancing at the Morning Standard through disdainful lorgnettes. Miss Marks and Miss Bennett are doing them now. 

Everard has sent this morning to enquire."

When Lady Mary had taken leave of her unresponsive daughter-in-law, Vivien tried to eat some breakfast. She was full of anxiety for Primula; not for the girl's bodily safety, but for her poor little wounded spirit, trying to run away from its pain, doping itself

in the modern fashion, with speed and change. She was angry with Sholto-Scott, and even with Rosalind, for being happy when her Prim was sad. At least, she supposed they were happy: Rosalind did not think fit to let her know.

After breakfast, she ordered the car, to take her to West Linton. It was the proper thing to do, to make inquiries at the hospital. As they drew up before the steps, the swing door opened, and Rosalind came out. 

No, not Rosalind-Flip! Flip, young, happy, radiant!

Vivien jumped out of the car and ran up the steps to meet her. "Flip!" she cried, catching at Rosalind's hand. "What has happened?" "Nothing," said Rosalind, looking round her. Vivien looked at her sister a little wistfully. "What a difference an illness makes!" she reflected. Rosalind seemed as absorbed now in Sholto-Scott as she had been long ago in Dick. 

She went home, and spent a restless afternoon of waiting, for it occurred to her now to fancy that Primula, driving recklessly perhaps in her unhappiness, had met with some accident. She had to restrain herself, over and over again, from phoning to the police. 

And then, just when Robinson was bringing in the

tea-pot and the hot tea-cakes, an inspiration came to her-no, a certainty. Prim was at the cottage. "Please order the car again, Robinson,” she said, and ran upstairs for her motor coat, leaving the disapproving Robinson with the untasted tea-cakes.

She told Jarvis to put her down at the "King's Head" in Pineholt village-she did not want to startle a shy bird—and took the short cut across the April meadows. For once she did not smell the freshness of the grass or hear a single bird-note. She slipped into

Chimneys by the back door, and peeped cautiously into the red-tiled kitchen. 

Mrs. Borden stood stirring something over the oil cooking-stove. She gave one glance of instant comprehension at Vivien. "Upstairs, ma'am," she said, pointing upwards with her wooden spoon in the direction of Primula's bedroom.

Vivien nodded, and crept out of the kitchen and up the ancient oak stairs between the warming-pans and the sporting prints. She softly tried Primula's door, praying that it might not be bolted.

It yielded, and she slipped inside the room. 

It was filled with the soft, spring dusk, but she could make out the shape of Primula lying on the bed, her face buried in the pillows. And when she stole closer, an occasional convulsive shaking of the slender shoulders told her something else. Primula was sobbing—

Primula whom she had never seen crying since her baby days.

All the mother in Vivien rushed to the surface now. "My little girl, my baby—don't cry, darling,” she cried, and putting her arm beneath the fair head, she drew it on to her shoulder. Primula feebly resisted at first, as Vivien expected, for she knew her own conduct was "sloppy” and un-modern to the highest degree.

But soon the girl yielded to comfort, and clung close to her mother. So they stayed for a while in the gathering darkness. "Is-Rosalind with him?" she asked at last in a hoarse little voice. "Yes," said Vivien. "He wants her, Prim. He's loved her for a long time. . . . You've had a lucky escape, dear. A man who loves another woman is not good enough for you." "But I-love him so dreadfully, Mother. . . . Will 

I always have this awful pain?” "No, darling, you won't. . . . I-loved someonedreadfully once, too. Not-not your father. He was a doctor in the hospital where I worked during the war. 

But I had a husband and he had a wife, so we said good-bye. It was like death to say good-bye. . . . But the pain went at last, and I could be happy again. 

You will be happy again. "I-don't-want-to-be-happy again," came a smothered voice from her shoulder.

Vivien stroked the bright hair soothingly. "I can't forgive him for proposing to you," she said.

There was a pause. Then Primula said in almost a whisper. "He didn't, Mother. I-proposed to him."

Vivien held her closer. This, then, was the explanation of so much that had puzzled her. She waited. "I don't want you to think he's behaved badly, Mother. It was my fault. Do you think I was veryoutrageous?" "My darling, I think you were very brave-since you loved him. Only-it's not yet easy for a man to be perfectly honest in these things-if he likes the woman." "Do you think he likes me?" "He likes and admires you immensely, Prim." 

Primula said nothing, but Vivien divined that some tension had relaxed. She went on stroking the girl's hair, her brain working busily. "Don't you think it would be a good idea,” she said presently, "for you to go away for a while? Somewhere fascinating and far away. How would you like to go away with Grandy? You know she's sailing for the West Indies after the next month, and you love going about with her." "I want to be with you. 99 Vivien hesitated, and then she made an immense inward surrender. "Well, suppose we go round the world together, you and I?" she said cheerfully. "I've

never been, and I'd love to. You'd show me India, and 

I'd show you Japan. . . . Would you like that?" "I'd like to go away-with you. . . . Can we go soon?" "As soon as it's possible to arrange it," said Vivien. "In a week or two, perhaps."

She thought of the play she was planning to make out of Sarah Sorel. She thought of her new novel, with six chapters written. She thought of the Scribblers' 

Club Dinner in May, at which she was to be the guest of the evening. Well-she could perhaps fit in one or two conversations with Roland Charters, to give him her ideas about Sarah, and then she must leave it to him. The novel must take its chance. As for the 

Scribblers' Club Dinner, that would have to go. She sighed, but it could not be helped. Was not her one and only baby in the direst distress, and was it not her job to help her out of it? "Isn't it funny?" said Primula with a mirthless little laugh. "There were only two things in the world that I really wanted-and you've got one of them, and 

Rosalind has the other." "I met George this morning, Prim," said Vivien a week later to her daughter, who lay in a long chair under the apple trees at Chimneys, where she still was. "It seems he's going to India to see the Taj Mahal,

· · and he asked if we'd mind his going by the Empress of Libya. The date suits him, or something. But I think I'll ask him to take the next boat. We want to get away from people, don't we, darling?” 

Primula closed her eyes wearily, and her mother, covertly watching the pale face against the green cushion thought she had not heard. But presently she spoke, without opening her eyes. "I don't see that we can very well expect to keep the whole boat to ourselves, Mother. . . . 

After all, we needn't pay any attention to him if we don't want to, need we?" "Oh-no, certainly not," said Vivien.

ROSALIND SHOLTO-SCOTT hung out of the window as the train slowed into Waterloo Station. She was looking out for a small, slight, inconspicuous figure among a crowd of other inconspicuous figures, for Vivien had wired to Southampton that she would be there to meet her. 

Rosalind had been away in America for only six weeks, but the time seemed more like a year, so packed was it with new impressions, new experiences and new friendships. The familiar passing crowds had a mo-

mentary air of strangeness, they were so-low-voiced and easy-going.

She was standing on the platform, gazing about her a little bewildered, when Vivien pulled her sleeve from behind. Rosalind wheeled round and faced her. Yes, here was Vivien, her dark eyes shining, looking about twenty; which was absurd, considering that she had been three weeks a grandmother. 

Rosalind caught her in her arms, and gave her a schoolgirl hug. "Oh, Rosalind," said Vivien, laughing and putting her hat straight. "You'll never leave off being Flip, even if you do turn into a fine lady! . . . Yes, you are a fine lady! That coat is quite intimidating.” "Fitz bought it for me in New York," said Rosalind, glancing down at her elegant and complicated travelling wrap. "It cost a hideous lot. I had to get some more evening frocks, too-mine were worn to rags. 

People were so terribly kind; they entertained us within an inch of our lives, and I didn't want them to pity Fitz for being tied for life to a dowdy Englishwoman." "I wonder he let you come back without him." "Oh, he hated it. But I had to get back to Throckmorton's. It isn't fair to Kate to leave her so long by herself."

Purvis was waiting for them with Lady Lydney's new Rolls-Royce. Rosalind's flat being let, she was

putting up at Caroline Square for a week or two. He grinned at her in a friendly fashion, and inquired after the Captain. For Sholto-Scott was in the news again, having just won the Round America air race in a 

Kingsley Drake Dragonfly. "How's New York?" asked Vivien, when they were sitting side by side in the big car which Purvis manoeuvred so cleverly through the traffic. "Oh, wonderful! Exactly like a Titan's lumber room. But I'm thrilled to be back in nice, flat London. ‘Little, little, little London.' Ben Jonson was quite right, Vivi. That's just how one feels about it."

She leant forward gazing affectionately out at the shops, the traffic, the people passing along the pavements. A bus swept by with flaunting red letters along its side. ""The Heart of Sarah Sorel. Last Weeks.' Oh, Vivi, is it coming off?" "Well, it's been running for eighteen months," said 

Vivien. "And Mr. Charters is putting on Behind the 

Throne in May." "You must get a new coat and skirt," said Rosalind, and they both burst out laughing. "I mustn't giggle like this," said Vivien, sobering down after a minute. "I'm a grandmother now." "Darling, I can't believe it. . . . But how remiss of me! How is my great-nephew?" "Earl Fitzhubert is his correct style and title," said

Vivien, her lips twitching with amusement. "It always makes me laugh-he's so little and red. . . . But, seriously, Rosalind, he's a beautiful little creature, and 

Prim's inordinately proud of him. She'll bore you horribly. . . . I'm very glad for George's sake. I think things are coming right for him. He has the immense pull now of being the father of this wonderful baby." "Oh, I should be so happy to know that,” cried Rosalind. 

Vivien glanced at her. "Don't worry, Flip. It wasn't your fault, and that motor smash was the greatest piece of luck for the four of you." "It was, for me," said Rosalind.

On the doorstep at Caroline Square, they encountered a couple coming out to a rich and glossy car which was waiting outside. "How do you do, Sir James?" said Vivien to the stout, kindly gentleman in spats. "Poppy, you remember Mrs. Sholto-Scott? . . . Rosalind, let me introduce Sir James McKillop." 

Poppy gave Rosalind a flashing smile, and stared at her hat. There was a little talk about babies and airraces and Le Touquet, and then Sir James and Lady McKillop stepped into their car and were whirled away. “The "McKillop's Marmalade," explained Vivien. "The decree was made absolute last month, and Poppy's

taken care that her second venture should be the human antithesis of the first."

Inside, No. 20 had been entirely changed. The frescoes had disappeared and been replaced by flower paintings in panels. "I'm so glad we've at last got rid of the man playing golf by the dining-room door," said Vivien. "Everybody hated him so."

Primula was not waiting for them in the drawingroom. "Her ladyship's in the nursery, ma'am," said Robinson. "She thought she heard his lordship crying." 

Vivien giggled. "Come on, Rosalind. You must pay him your respects before he goes to sleep."

They climbed up the Chinese staircase, no longer vermilion, but dull gold. The top floor had been converted into nurseries and where the Black Sheep had once rioted, Rosalind was amused to discover an innocent scene of white enamel and painted nursery rhymes, with a frilly cot and a fireguard.

In front of the fire sat Primula, an apron tied over her pale pink Paris frock, gloating over the human morsel which lay on her lap. The Golden Girl had regained all her beauty. She looked up as they came in. "You're just too late," she said, more in pity than in reproach. "If you'd come ten minutes ago, you'd have seen him in his bath."

"Sorry, Prim," said Rosalind, laughing, and dropping a kiss on the apple-blossom cheek. "If I'd known,

I'd have hurried up the liner at New York. But I hope he can still be introduced to his Aunt Rosey. 

What an absolute lamb! Sweet thing! I'm your Aunt Rosey!"

Earl Fitzhubert looked up with wise dark-blue eyes into the laughing face of his great-aunt bent over him, and waved his mottled fists feebly in the air. His mother unceremoniously whisked up the skirt of his nightgown to display the beauty of his legs and feet. "Look at his little toes!" she said, kissing them. "Aren't they perfect? Don't you think he's-rather nice?" "I think he's the most beautiful baby I've ever seen,” declared Rosalind, "with the exception of his mother, whom I first met when I was fifteen."

Primula felt pleased. For a moment it struck her that Rosalind looked extraordinarily young and attractive. "What an adorable coat!" she said. "New York," said Rosalind. "Oh, of course! . . . We were terribly excited about the race. George read it out to me every day when I was ill. Wasn't it too thrilling to be on the spot?" "Rather too thrilling, sometimes," agreed Rosalind. "When's Fitz coming home?" asked Primula, look-

ing at Rosalind with large grey eyes in which there was no shadow of unhappy consciousness. "I'm simply longing to see him. You see, George and I want him to be one of the Baby's godfathers-if he will, and we can't arrange about the christening till we know when he's free." "Fitz will be honoured," said Rosalind. "What are you going to call him?” "George Theodore Francis Athelstan Fitzroy." "Rather a mouthful for such a scrap," said Rosalind laughing. "I shouldn't call him a scrap," said Primula reproachfully. "He weighed ten pounds when he was born. Oh, here's George." George's hair was as ruffled as usual, his eyes were as honest, but his manner was considerably less pugnacious. He went straight to the back of his wife's chair, and tilted up her chin. Their lips met in a long kiss. Watching that kiss, Rosalind was conscious of a great relief.

Then George turned to Vivien and Rosalind, and became the perfect son-in-law and host. He asked 

Rosalind intelligent questions about America, and listened to her answers. He told Vivien that the Prime 

Minister had been six times to Sarah Sorel. He was going on to further feats of politeness, when his son gave a sneeze. Instantly he was on his knees by 

Primula's side.

1 "Do you think he's caught cold?" asked George anxiously. "No," said Primula. "He's only a little bit excited at seeing his Grandmamma and his Great-aunt Rosey." "Come, Rosalind," said Vivien, laughing, "it's time you got your things unpacked."

Outside on the golden staircase she paused and cast a mischievous glance at Rosalind. "That's what we are for Prim henceforth,” she said. "Just Grandmamma and Great-aunt Rosey."

THE END
